3.8 Complete docker-compose.yml for anihpj

Here's a production-like Docker Compose setup for local development. This mirrors what Kubernetes will do in production:

docker-compose.yml — anihpj Local Dev
version: "3.9"
services:
  db:
    image: postgres:16-alpine
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: anihpj
      POSTGRES_USER: anihpj_user
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: dev_password
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    ports:
      - "5432:5432"
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U anihpj_user -d anihpj"]
      interval: 10s
      retries: 5

  redis:
    image: redis:7-alpine
    ports:
      - "6379:6379"

  web:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile
    image: anihpj:dev
    command: python manage.py runserver 0.0.0.0:8000
    volumes:
      - .:/app  # Live code reload (dev only!)—remove for production
    ports:
      - "8000:8000"
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://anihpj_user:dev_password@db:5432/anihpj
      REDIS_URL: redis://redis:6379/0
      DJANGO_DEBUG: "true"
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy
      redis:
        condition: service_started

volumes:
  pgdata:
📖 Walkthrough: What Each Section Does
🗄️
db (PostgreSQL)

Uses postgres:16-alpine (small, secure). healthcheck runs pg_isready every 10s — the web service waits for this before starting. pgdata volume persists data across restarts.

⚡
redis (Cache)

Lightweight redis:7-alpine for caching and session storage. No healthcheck needed — condition: service_started is sufficient.

🌐
web (Django app)

build: uses the local Dockerfile. volumes: mounts .:/app for live code reload (edit Python → instant restart). depends_on: waits for db (healthy) and redis (started).

🔄
K8s Mapping

db → StatefulSet + Service (stateful, persistent). redis → Deployment + Service (stateless, scalable). web → Deployment + Service + Ingress (app tier). volumes → PVC (persistent storage).

3.9 The Orchestration Evolution — Docker → Kubernetes

Understanding how container orchestration evolved helps you grasp WHY Kubernetes works the way it does:

🐋
2013
Docker Engine

Run ONE container on ONE machine. Manual start/stop.

Manual
📋
2014
Docker Compose

MULTIPLE containers, ONE machine. YAML config. No auto-healing.

Dev Only
🐝
2015
Docker Swarm

Multi-host clustering. Built into Docker. Lost to Kubernetes.

Declining
☸️
2015+
Kubernetes

Full orchestration: auto-scale, self-heal, rolling updates. Industry standard.

Production ✅
CapabilityDockerComposeKubernetes
Multi-host❌❌✅
Auto-scaling❌❌✅ HPA/VPA
Self-healing❌restart only✅ Auto-restart + reschedule
Rolling updates❌❌✅ Zero-downtime
Service discovery❌DNS✅ DNS + Services + Ingress
Secrets mgmt❌File-based✅ K8s Secrets + External Secrets Operator
Load balancing❌❌✅ Service + Ingress + ALB
Use caseSingle containerLocal dev/CIProduction at scale
🐋 Essential Docker Commands — Quick Reference
CommandWhat It Does
docker build -t anihpj:v1 .Build image from Dockerfile in current directory
docker run -p 8000:8000 anihpj:v1Run container, map host port 8000 → container 8000
docker psList running containers
docker imagesList all images on this machine
docker logs -f <container>Follow live logs from a container
docker exec -it <container> bashOpen a shell inside a running container
docker stop <container>Gracefully stop a running container
docker rm <container>Remove a stopped container
docker rmi <image>Remove an image
docker push ECR_URI:v1Push image to AWS ECR
docker pull ECR_URI:v1Pull image from AWS ECR
docker system prune -aClean up ALL unused images/containers/volumes
🔐 Docker Security Best Practices for DOP-C02

1. NEVER run as root: Add USER 1000 in Dockerfile. Root can escape to host.
2. Pin image tags: Never :latest in prod. Use python:3.12.4-slim.
3. Scan images: ECR scan-on-push. Fail builds on CRITICAL CVEs.
4. Minimal base: slim/alpine variants. Smaller = fewer CVEs = faster.
5. Multi-stage: Separate build tools from runtime. No gcc in production.
6. Read-only rootfs: readOnlyRootFilesystem: true in K8s Pod spec.
7. Drop capabilities: --cap-drop=ALL --cap-add=NET_BIND_SERVICE.
8. Secrets in images: NEVER! Use IRSA, Secrets Manager, or K8s Secrets.

🐋 Container Networking Basics

Bridge (default): Containers on same host communicate via docker0 bridge. Isolated from host network.
Host: Container shares host's network stack directly. No isolation. Faster but less secure.
None: Container has no network. Use for batch jobs that don't need networking.
Overlay: Multi-host networking for Docker Swarm. Not used with K8s (K8s uses CNI plugins instead).
Kubernetes networking model: Every Pod gets its own IP. Pods on different nodes communicate via CNI plugins (Calico, Cilium, Flannel). Containers within a Pod share localhost.

⏱️ 20 minutes
Scenario: Build & Push anihpj to ECR

You have the anihpj Django project on your machine. Your task: (1) Write a production-ready multi-stage Dockerfile; (2) Build the image; (3) Tag it for ECR; (4) Push to ECR; (5) Verify the image is in ECR with scan results.

  1. Create the Dockerfile (use multi-stage build for smaller image)
  2. Create .dockerignore to exclude secrets and build artifacts
  3. Build, tag, and push to ECR
  4. Enable image scanning and verify no CRITICAL vulnerabilities
💡 Hint: Use python:3.12-slim as base. Multi-stage: builder stage installs gcc+libpq-dev, final stage copies only pip packages. ECR URI format: <account-id>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com/<repo>:<tag>
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
# STEP 1: Create ECR repository
aws ecr create-repository --repository-name anihpj --region us-east-1

# STEP 2: Authenticate Docker to ECR
aws ecr get-login-password --region us-east-1 | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin \
  123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com

# STEP 3: Build the image (multi-stage)
docker build -t anihpj:v1 .

# STEP 4: Tag for ECR
docker tag anihpj:v1 \
  123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1

# STEP 5: Push to ECR
docker push 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1

# STEP 6: Verify & Scan
aws ecr describe-images --repository-name anihpj
aws ecr start-image-scan --repository-name anihpj --image-id imageTag=v1
aws ecr describe-image-scan-findings --repository-name anihpj --image-id imageTag=v1

# RESULT: Image is in ECR, scanned for vulnerabilities, ready for EKS!

📝 Chapter 3 — AWS DevOps Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

You build the anihpj Docker image on your MacBook (ARM64/M1) and push it to ECR. When EKS (x86_64 EC2 nodes) tries to pull it, the Pod fails with "exec format error." What's the issue?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

CPU architecture mismatch. The image was built for ARM64 (Apple Silicon) but the EKS nodes are x86_64 (Intel/AMD). Use docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 to create multi-architecture images, or build on an x86_64 machine/CodeBuild.

📖 EXPLANATION
# CPU Architecture Mismatch — Q1
# MacBook M1/M2 = ARM64 (aarch64)
# EC2/EKS nodes = x86_64 (amd64) ← default
#
# SOLUTION 1: Multi-arch build (RECOMMENDED)
docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 \
  -t 123.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1 \
  --push .

# SOLUTION 2: Build on CodeBuild (x86_64 by default)
# buildspec.yml in CodeBuild:
#   docker build -t anihpj:v1 .
#   docker push ECR_URI

# SOLUTION 3: Use Graviton (ARM64) EKS nodes
# eksctl create nodegroup --instance-types t4g.medium

CodeBuild is the recommended approach for production — it ensures the image is built on the same architecture as your EKS nodes.

Q2

Why use a multi-stage Docker build for the anihpj production image?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

To keep the final image small and secure. Stage 1 installs build tools (gcc, libpq-dev) and compiles dependencies. Stage 2 copies only the built artifacts — leaving behind compilers and build dependencies. Result: smaller image, fewer vulnerabilities, faster pulls.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Multi-Stage Build — Q2 ANSWER
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# Stage 1: BUILD (compiler tools here)
FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder
RUN apt-get install gcc libpq-dev  # ← 200MB of build tools
RUN pip install -r requirements.txt
# Stage 2: RUNTIME (no compiler!)
FROM python:3.12-slim
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local  # ← Only packages!
# No gcc, no headers, no build artifacts!
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# RESULT:
# Without multi-stage: ~800 MB (gcc, headers, build artifacts)
# WITH multi-stage:    ~200 MB (Python + pip packages + app only)
# Savings: 75% smaller image!

Benefits: faster ECR push/pull, faster Pod startup, fewer CVEs (no compiler to exploit), smaller ECR storage costs. DevOps best practice tested on DOP-C02.

Q3

Which Docker command pushes the anihpj image to AWS ECR?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER
# 1. Authenticate Docker to ECR
aws ecr get-login-password --region us-east-1 | \
  docker login --username AWS --password-stdin \
  123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com

# 2. Tag the image for ECR
docker tag anihpj:v1 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1

# 3. Push to ECR
docker push 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1
📖 EXPLANATION

The ECR push flow: (1) Get auth token with aws ecr get-login-password; (2) docker login to ECR using that token; (3) docker tag your local image to the ECR URI format; (4) docker push. CodeBuild can do all of this automatically in a buildspec.yml — the image is pushed to ECR as a build artifact, ready for EKS to pull.

Q4

What is the primary difference between a Docker IMAGE and a Docker CONTAINER?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Image = read-only template (built from Dockerfile). Container = running instance of an image with a writable layer. You build an image once (docker build). You can run many containers from the same image (docker run). Containers are ephemeral — when you delete a container, its writable layer is lost.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Image vs Container — Q4 ANSWER
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# IMAGE (Read-Only Template):
#   Dockerfile → docker build → anihpj:v1
#   Stored in ECR (immutable layers)
#   Like a CLASS in OOP (blueprint)
#
# CONTAINER (Running Instance):
#   docker run anihpj:v1 → container-abc123
#   Image layers (read-only) + Writable layer
#   Like an INSTANCE of a class (object)
#
# 1 Image → MANY Containers:
#   anihpj:v1 → container-1 (Pod-1 on Node-A)
#   anihpj:v1 → container-2 (Pod-2 on Node-B)
#   anihpj:v1 → container-3 (Pod-3 on Node-C)
#   Same image, 3 separate running instances
#   Each has its own writable layer (logs, temp)

When a container dies, only the writable layer is lost; the image layers are immutable and shared across all containers.

Q5

You accidentally include a .env file with AWS credentials in your Docker build. How do you prevent this?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Add a .dockerignore file that excludes .env, *.env, and any credential files from the build context. Also: NEVER put credentials in Docker images — use IAM Roles (IRSA for EKS), Secrets Manager, or environment variables injected at runtime.

📖 EXPLANATION
# .dockerignore + IRSA Security — Q5 ANSWER
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# STEP 1: .dockerignore (prevents secrets in image)
# File: anihpj/.dockerignore
__pycache__/
*.pyc
.git/
.env           # ← Blocks .env from build context!
*.env
venv/
db.sqlite3
Dockerfile

# STEP 2: IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts)
# EKS Pod gets AWS credentials WITHOUT secrets in image
# Pod → OIDC → IAM Role → temporary AWS credentials
# No access keys in code, Dockerfile, or environment!
#
# ❌ NEVER: AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID in Dockerfile
# ✅ ALWAYS: IRSA + IAM Role per Service Account

Security best practice: credentials should NEVER be baked into images. Use IRSA so EKS Pods get temporary AWS credentials automatically.

Q6

When should you use Docker Compose vs Kubernetes for anihpj?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Docker Compose = local development and CI testing (single host). Kubernetes = production deployment (multi-node cluster with auto-scaling, self-healing, and advanced networking).

📖 EXPLANATION
# Docker Compose vs Kubernetes — Q6 ANSWER
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# DOCKER COMPOSE:          KUBERNETES:
# ─────────────────        ─────────────────
# Single host              Multi-node cluster
# docker-compose up        kubectl apply -f
# No auto-healing          Self-healing Pods
# Manual scale             HPA auto-scale
# Bridge network           CNI + Services + Ingress
# Local dev use            Production use
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# DEVELOPMENT:             PRODUCTION:
# docker-compose.yml       deployment.yaml + service.yaml
# docker compose up -d     kubectl apply -f .
# localhost:8000           anihpj.example.com (ALB→EKS)

Never use Compose in production — that's what Kubernetes is for. Compose is for local dev and CI testing only.

Q7

What AWS service provides vulnerability scanning for Docker images stored in ECR?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Amazon ECR Image Scanning (built into ECR). It scans images for Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) using the Clair vulnerability database. Can be triggered on push or on-demand. Results appear in the ECR console and can be integrated with Amazon Inspector for continuous assessment.

📖 EXPLANATION
# ECR Image Scanning — Q7 ANSWER
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# BASIC SCANNING (free, on push):
#   docker push → ECR → Clair DB scan → Results
#
# ENHANCED SCANNING (Amazon Inspector):
#   docker push → ECR → Inspector scan → Security Hub
#   Continuous monitoring (rescans on new CVEs)
#
# buildspec.yml integration:
#   - docker push ECR_URI
#   - aws ecr wait image-scan-complete
#   - SCAN_RESULT=$(aws ecr describe-image-scan-findings)
#   - if CRITICAL found → exit 1 (fail build!)
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# DevSecOps: Shift security LEFT — catch vulns
# BEFORE they reach production, not after!

For DOP-C02 DevSecOps scenarios: enable scan-on-push, fail CodeBuild on CRITICAL findings, integrate with Security Hub for centralized visibility.

Q8

What does the CMD instruction in the anihpj Dockerfile do?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

CMD defines the default command that runs when the container starts. In our Dockerfile: CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"] — this starts gunicorn with 3 worker processes, binding to port 8000. The container runs as long as this process runs.

📖 EXPLANATION

CMD vs ENTRYPOINT: CMD provides defaults that can be overridden (docker run anihpj:v1 python manage.py shell). ENTRYPOINT defines the executable that always runs (harder to override). For web apps, CMD is preferred because you can override it for debugging. For K8s: CMD is equivalent to the command field in a Pod spec. The container's lifecycle is tied to this process — when gunicorn exits, the container stops.

Q9

You need to run a database migration (python manage.py migrate) before anihpj starts in EKS. Where should this be configured?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Use a Kubernetes Init Container or a Job. An init container runs BEFORE the main container and must complete successfully. A Job runs the migration as a one-off task. Do NOT put migrations in the Dockerfile CMD — that would run migrations on EVERY Pod restart, which is dangerous.

# Init Container approach (runs before main container)
initContainers:
- name: db-migrate
  image: anihpj:v1
  command: ["python", "manage.py", "migrate"]
# Main container starts only after init succeeds
containers:
- name: anihpj
  image: anihpj:v1
📖 EXPLANATION

Best practices for Django migrations on K8s: (1) Init Container — runs before main app, Pod only starts if migration succeeds; (2) Kubernetes Job — run migration separately, then deploy; (3) CI/CD pipeline — run migration as part of CodeDeploy lifecycle hook. NEVER run migrations in the main container's startup (CMD/ENTRYPOINT) — if you scale to 3 Pods, you'd have 3 simultaneous migration attempts which could corrupt the database.

Q10

Which statement BEST describes the relationship between Docker and Kubernetes?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Docker builds and runs individual containers. Kubernetes orchestrates hundreds of containers across multiple servers. Docker = the engine (build, ship, run ONE container). Kubernetes = the fleet manager (deploy, scale, heal, network MANY containers across MANY machines).

📖 EXPLANATION

Analogy: Docker = a single truck that delivers one shipping container. Kubernetes = the entire port authority that manages hundreds of ships, docks, cranes, and containers. Docker handles: build (docker build), ship (docker push), run (docker run). Kubernetes handles: scheduling (which node?), scaling (how many Pods?), networking (how do Pods talk?), self-healing (Pod died → restart), rolling updates (deploy new version with zero downtime). They work TOGETHER: Docker builds images, Kubernetes runs them at scale.

🟢☸️ Part 2: Kubernetes FundamentalsChapters 4-7

Chapter 4: What is Kubernetes? — The Big Picture

Kubernetes has a reputation for complexity. It doesn't deserve it. In this chapter, we use the SHIPPING PORT ANALOGY: Control Plane = Harbor Master's office, Worker Nodes = Docks, Pods = Ships, Containers = Cargo. Every K8s component maps to something you already understand. By the end, you'll know how ALL the pieces work together — and you'll trace a Pod from kubectl apply to a running container.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~4 hours ☸️ Core K8s 🎯 DOP-C02: EKS Foundation

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why Kubernetes exists — the problem it solves for container management
  • ✅ The Shipping Port Analogy — every K8s component as a harbor element
  • ✅ Control Plane deep dive: API server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager
  • ✅ Worker Node deep dive: kubelet, kube-proxy, container runtime
  • ✅ The full 9-step Pod creation journey — from kubectl to running container

4.1 Why Kubernetes? — The Problem It Solves

Running one container is easy: docker run anihpj:v1. Running 100 containers across 10 servers is chaos. What happens when a container crashes at 3 AM? When traffic spikes 10x? When you need to deploy a new version with zero downtime? Manual container management doesn't scale — that's why Kubernetes exists.

🔄
Self-Healing

Container crashes? K8s restarts it in ~2 seconds. Entire node dies? K8s reschedules Pods to healthy nodes automatically. No pager, no SSH, no 3 AM wake-up.

📈
Auto-Scaling

Traffic spikes 10x? HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) spins up more Pods automatically. Traffic drops? Scales back down. You define the metric (CPU, memory, custom), K8s handles the math.

🚀
Zero-Downtime Deploys

New version ready? Rolling update replaces old Pods one-by-one — users never notice. Something wrong? kubectl rollout undo — instant rollback to the previous version.

💀 WITHOUT KUBERNETES

Manual management. You SSH into servers, restart crashed containers, configure load balancers by hand. You ARE the orchestrator — and you need sleep.

"anihpj pod down!" → You wake up at 3 AM
"Traffic 10x spike" → Site crashes before you can react
"Deploy v2" → SCP files, pray nothing breaks
"Rollback" → Hope you made a backup
"Scale to 10 servers" → Manually provision 7 more EC2s

☸️ WITH KUBERNETES

Declarative automation. You tell K8s WHAT you want ("3 copies of anihpj, always running") and it figures out HOW to make it happen across the cluster.

Pod crashes → Auto-restarted in 2 seconds
Traffic spikes → HPA auto-scales Pods
Deploy v2 → Rolling update, zero downtime
Rollback → kubectl rollout undo (1 command)
Scale to 50 Pods → kubectl scale deploy/anihpj --replicas=50

🧪 REAL SCENARIO: The 3 AM Pager That Never Rang

Without K8s: It's 3:12 AM. An EC2 instance running 4 anihpj containers crashes (hardware failure). You get paged. You SSH into the remaining instances, manually start 4 replacement containers, update the load balancer targets, and verify the site is back up. Total: 23 minutes of downtime.

With K8s: Same crash. kubelet on the dead node stops reporting. Controller manager marks the node as NotReady after 5 minutes. ReplicaSet controller sees 4 missing Pods → creates 4 replacements on healthy nodes. Scheduler assigns them. kube-proxy updates iptables. You sleep through it. Total: ~5 minutes of automated recovery, zero human intervention.

💡 The Core Philosophy

You never say "start a container on node 2." You say "I want 3 copies of anihpj:v2 with 256Mi memory each" — and K8s figures out the HOW. This is the declarative model: describe the desired state, let the system reconcile to it. This single idea is what makes Kubernetes different from every orchestration system that came before it.

4.2 Kubernetes Defined — The Simple Explanation

Kubernetes (K8s = K + 8 letters + s, Greek for "helmsman" or "pilot") is an open-source container orchestration platform. You declare what you want, and Kubernetes ensures it stays that way — continuously reconciling desired state with actual state.

🏛️
Born at Google

Evolved from Google's internal Borg system (2003) that ran Gmail, Search, YouTube. Open-sourced 2014, donated to CNCF.

🔤
Written in Go

Built with Google's Go language for high concurrency, compiled performance. Clean, fast, and memory-efficient.

📜
Apache 2.0 License

Fully open-source. Free to use, modify, distribute commercially. No vendor lock-in.

📊
By the Numbers

112K+ GitHub stars, 3,500+ contributors, 96% CNCF adoption rate, runs at 77% of Fortune 100 companies.

Declarative Management — You Declare, K8s Delivers
📝 YOU DECLARE (YAML):             ⚙️ KUBERNETES ENSURES:
┌───────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ replicas: 3           │         │ ✅ 3 containers running        │
│ image: anihpj:v2      │  ────▶  │ ✅ All using v2 image          │
│ memory: 256Mi         │         │ ✅ Each limited to 256Mi       │
└───────────────────────┘         └──────────────────────────────┘

Pod crashes?     K8s restarts automatically  🔄
Node dies?       K8s moves pods to other nodes  🔀
Traffic spikes?  K8s can auto-scale up          📈
New version?     K8s rolls out with 0 downtime  🚀

💡 You never say "start a container on node 2"
   You say "I want 3 copies" — K8s figures out the HOW
🧠 DEEP DIVE: The Reconciliation Loop — K8s' Core Philosophy
Every Kubernetes component follows the SAME pattern:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  🔄 THE RECONCILIATION LOOP                   │
│                                                               │
│   ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐            │
│   │ OBSERVE  │────▶│  COMPARE │────▶│   ACT    │            │
│   │          │     │          │     │          │            │
│   │ "What    │     │ Desired  │     │ "Create  │            │
│   │  exists  │     │ vs       │     │  missing │            │
│   │  now?"   │     │ Actual?" │     │  things" │            │
│   └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘            │
│        ▲                                  │                  │
│        └──────────────────────────────────┘                  │
│                   LOOP FOREVER                               │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 THIS is the genius of Kubernetes:
   • NOT "do this once" — it's "ensure this FOREVER"
   • Scheduler doesn't "place a Pod" — it constantly checks
     "are there Pods without nodes? Let me fix that"
   • Controller-manager doesn't "create a Deployment once" —
     it watches "are there enough replicas? If not, create more"
   • This infinite loop means the system is SELF-HEALING

   🌊 ANALOGY: Kubernetes is NOT a mailman (delivers once and
      forgets). It's a LIFEGUARD — constantly scanning, always
      watching, ready to act the moment something goes wrong.

4.3 The Shipping Port Analogy — Understanding K8s Visually

This analogy makes every K8s concept intuitive. Imagine a busy shipping port:

🌊 KUBERNETES = A BUSY SHIPPING PORT
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              🏗️ PORT AUTHORITY (Control Plane)                │
│                                                               │
│  ┌─────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐   │
│  │ 📡 SIGNAL    │  │ 📋 MANIFEST    │  │ 🎯 DISPATCH      │   │
│  │ TOWER       │  │ OFFICE        │  │ TOWER            │   │
│  │ kube-       │  │ etcd          │  │ kube-scheduler   │   │

                          │

│  │  ▓ 📦 anihpj 📦 ▓     │  │  ▓ 📦 anihpj 📦 ▓     │    │
│  │  ═══════════════════   │  │  ═══════════════════   │    │
│  └─────────────────────────┘  └─────────────────────────┘    │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

MAPPING:
🏗️ Port Authority  = Control Plane    📡 Signal Tower = kube-apiserver
📋 Manifest Office = etcd              🎯 Dispatch     = kube-scheduler
🔄 Harbor Master   = controller-mgr   🏭 Docks        = Worker Nodes
🏗️ Cranes          = kubelet           🚦 Traffic Ctrl = kube-proxy
🚢 Ships (Berths)  = Pods              📦 Cargo        = Containers

4.4 Kubernetes Architecture — Control Plane & Workers

A Kubernetes cluster is split into two planes that work together: the Control Plane (the brain — makes decisions) and Worker Nodes (the muscles — runs your containers). Every component communicates exclusively through the API server — nothing talks directly to etcd except the API server.

🧠
Control Plane (The Brain)

kube-apiserver — front door for ALL operations
etcd — the cluster's memory (all state stored here)
kube-scheduler — decides WHERE each Pod runs
controller-manager — runs reconciliation loops (Deployment, ReplicaSet, Node controllers, etc.)

On AWS EKS: AWS manages this entire plane for you. You never SSH into control plane nodes.

💪
Worker Nodes (The Muscles)

kubelet — node agent, talks to container runtime
kube-proxy — network rules so traffic reaches Pods
Container Runtime — containerd/CRI-O, actually runs containers

On AWS EKS: You manage worker nodes (EC2 or Fargate). You choose the AMI, instance type, and scaling configuration.

Complete Cluster Architecture
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                    ☸️ KUBERNETES CLUSTER                    │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │           🧠 CONTROL PLANE (The Brain)                │  │
│  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐   │  │
│  │  │kube-     │ │ etcd │ │kube-     │ │kube-     │   │  │
│  │  │apiserver │ │  💾  │ │scheduler │ │controller│   │  │
│  │  │   🔌     │ │Store │ │  🎯      │ │-manager  │   │  │
│  │  └──────────┘ └──────┘ └──────────┘ │  🔄      │   │  │
│  │                                     └──────────┘   │  │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                          │                                  │
│  ┌───────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │              💪 WORKER NODES (The Muscles)            │  │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐        │  │
│  │  │   Worker Node 1  │    │   Worker Node 2  │        │  │
│  │  │  ┌────────────┐  │    │  ┌────────────┐  │        │  │
│  │  │  │  kubelet   │  │    │  │  kubelet   │  │        │  │
│  │  │  │ kube-proxy │  │    │  │ kube-proxy │  │        │  │
│  │  │  │ containerd │  │    │  │ containerd │  │        │  │
│  │  │  └────────────┘  │    │  └────────────┘  │        │  │
│  │  │  ┌────────────┐  │    │  ┌────────────┐  │        │  │
│  │  │  │ 📦 anihpj  │  │    │  │ 📦 anihpj  │  │        │  │
│  │  │  │ Pod        │  │    │  │ Pod        │  │        │  │
│  │  │  └────────────┘  │    │  └────────────┘  │        │  │
│  │  └──────────────────┘    └──────────────────┘        │  │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔌 How the Two Planes Communicate

The kubelet on each worker node is the critical bridge. It watches the API server for Pods assigned to its node, pulls images, starts containers, and reports status back. The kube-proxy watches for Service/Endpoint changes and updates iptables rules. Neither the scheduler nor controllers ever talk directly to worker nodes — they update objects via the API server, and the kubelet on each node picks up the changes. This hub-and-spoke design is what makes K8s scalable: add 100 nodes, the API server remains the single coordination point.

4.5 Control Plane Components — Deep Dive

🧠 The Brain — Control Plane Components

📡kube-apiserver
The front door of Kubernetes. EVERYTHING goes through the API server — kubectl commands, node heartbeats, controller actions, scheduler decisions. It authenticates (Who are you?), authorizes (Can you do this?), and validates (Is this YAML correct?). If the API server is down, the cluster is blind — but running workloads continue unaffected.
💾etcd
The cluster's brain memory. A distributed, consistent key-value store holding ALL cluster state — every Pod, Service, ConfigMap, Secret. Uses RAFT consensus (3-5 nodes for HA). If you lose etcd without a backup, you lose your ENTIRE cluster. On EKS, AWS manages etcd for you — but you still need to know how to back it up for self-managed clusters.
🎯kube-scheduler
The matchmaker. Watches for new Pods with no node assigned, then picks the best node. Two phases: FILTER (remove nodes that can't fit — not enough CPU/memory, tainted, cordoned) and SCORE (rank remaining nodes by fit — least requested, image already cached, affinity rules). The Pod gets nodeName set — now the kubelet takes over.
🔄controller-manager
The watchdog. Runs controller loops that continuously compare desired state (from etcd) vs actual state (from cluster) and take corrective action. Examples: "ReplicaSet wants 3 Pods, only 2 running → create 1 more." "Node went down 5 minutes ago → evict its Pods." "Deployment strategy is RollingUpdate → gradually replace old Pods with new ones."
🔐 DEEP DIVE: API Server — The 3 Security Gates

Every API request goes through THREE gates in order. A request must pass ALL three before touching etcd:

1
AuthN
2
AuthZ
3
Admission

🔐 GATE 1: AUTHENTICATION — "WHO ARE YOU?"

🔑
X.509 Client Certificates

kubeconfig certs. The original K8s auth method. Cert CN becomes the username, O becomes the group.

🎫
Bearer Tokens

ServiceAccount tokens automatically mounted in Pods at /var/run/secrets/.... Used for in-cluster API access.

🔗
OIDC (OpenID Connect)

Integrates with AWS IAM via EKS. Users authenticate with their AWS credentials, get a K8s token.

🔓 GATE 2: AUTHORIZATION — "CAN YOU DO THIS?"

📋
RBAC

Role/ClusterRole + RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding. The standard K8s authz mechanism. Most exam questions use RBAC.

🖥️
Node Authorizer

Kubelet can only access its own node's Pods. This is automatic — you don't configure it.

🔌
Webhook

External authorization: OPA, Gatekeeper, Kyverno. K8s calls out to an external service for the allow/deny decision.

✅ GATE 3: ADMISSION CONTROL — "IS THIS REQUEST VALID?"

✏️
Mutating Webhooks

Run FIRST. Inject sidecars, set default values, add labels. Modifies the object before validation.

✅
Validating Webhooks

Run SECOND. Reject non-compliant requests. Does NOT modify — only allow/deny.

🏗️
Built-in Admission

ResourceQuota, LimitRanger, PodSecurity. Always active — no webhook config needed.

🎯 EXAM TIP

The order is ALWAYS AuthN → AuthZ → Admission. This is tested frequently. Remember: authentication establishes identity, authorization checks permissions, admission mutates/validates the object before persistence. A request that fails any gate never reaches etcd.

💾 DEEP DIVE: etcd — RAFT Consensus & Quorum

etcd uses RAFT consensus: a LEADER is elected, ALL writes go through the leader, then replicate to followers. A write is COMMITTED only when a majority acknowledges:

3️⃣
3-Node Cluster

Quorum = 2   (3/2 + 1 = 2)
Can survive 1 node failure
Losing 2 nodes = DOWN

5️⃣
5-Node Cluster

Quorum = 3   (5/2 + 1 = 3)
Can survive 2 node failures
Losing 3 nodes = DOWN

✅
3 nodes, lose 1

WORKS
2/3 have quorum

❌
3 nodes, lose 2

DOWN
1/3 — no quorum!

✅
5 nodes, lose 2

WORKS
3/5 have quorum

❌
5 nodes, lose 3

DOWN
2/5 — no quorum

🧠 SPLIT BRAIN — RAFT Prevents This

A leader without quorum cannot accept writes. If a network partition isolates the leader from the majority, the leader steps down and a new election occurs in the partition that has quorum. This guarantees consistency — no two leaders can ever accept writes simultaneously.

💡 Exam & Production Tips
  • On EKS: AWS manages etcd — you don't touch it. The control plane is fully managed.
  • For self-managed clusters: ALWAYS deploy 3-5 etcd nodes in different AZs. Odd numbers only (3 or 5) — even numbers waste resources without adding fault tolerance.
  • ALWAYS: etcdctl snapshot save → push to S3 regularly. etcd backups are your last line of defense.
🎯 DEEP DIVE: Scheduler — Filter → Score → Assign

The scheduler runs a two-phase algorithm for every unscheduled Pod:

🔍 PHASE 1: FILTERING

Remove nodes that can't fit

  • NodeResourcesFit: enough CPU/Memory?
  • NodeUnschedulable: is node cordoned?
  • NodeSelector / NodeAffinity: matching labels?
  • TaintToleration: Pod tolerates node's taint?
  • NodePorts: port conflict?
  • PodTopologySpread: even zone distribution?
  • VolumeZone: volume available in this AZ?

Nodes that fail ANY filter are eliminated.

📊 PHASE 2: SCORING

Rank remaining nodes 0–10

  • LeastRequestedPriority: prefer less-loaded nodes
  • ImageLocality: bonus if image already cached
  • NodeAffinityPriority: bonus for preferred affinities
  • BalancedResourceAllocation: balanced CPU/Mem ratio

Highest-scoring node wins → pod.spec.nodeName = "node-3"

🔄 DEEP DIVE: controller-manager — Many Controllers, One Binary

The controller-manager is a single binary that runs dozens of controllers, each with its own reconciliation loop:

📦
Deployment Controller

"Deploy wants 3 → RS has 2 → create Pod"

🔄
ReplicaSet Controller

"RS says 3 replicas, only 2 Pods → create 1"

🖥️
Node Controller

"Node-2 no heartbeat 5min → NotReady, evict Pods"

🌐
Service Controller

"Service type=LB → call AWS API, create NLB"

🔗
EndpointSlice Controller

"Service selector matches 3 Pods → update IPs"

⚡
Job Controller

"Job needs 1 success → create Pod, monitor"

💾
PV/PVC Controller

"PVC 10Gi, no PV → dynamic provision via SC"

📁
Namespace Controller

"Namespace marked delete → delete all inside"

🗑️
Garbage Collector

"Owner deleted → cascade delete dependents"

🧠 Key Insight for the Exam

Each controller does ONE thing, loops forever, and reads state from etcd (not memory). If the controller-manager crashes, kubelet restarts it — and reconciliation picks up where it left off. This stateless design is why Kubernetes is self-healing.

4.6 Worker Node Components — Deep Dive

💪 The Muscles — Worker Node Components

🏗️kubelet
The node agent running on every worker. Watches the API server for Pods assigned to its node, calls the container runtime (containerd/CRI-O) to pull images and start containers, runs health probes (liveness/readiness), and reports Pod/node status back to the API server. If the kubelet stops, the node shows as NotReady.
🚦kube-proxy
The network plumber. Maintains network rules (iptables or IPVS) on every node so Pods and Services can communicate. When you create a Service, kube-proxy writes rules: "Traffic to Service IP 10.100.50.2:8000 → randomly route to Pod-1 (10.0.1.5), Pod-2 (10.0.2.8), or Pod-3 (10.0.3.12)." On AWS EKS, kube-proxy manages the VPC networking rules.
🐋Container Runtime
The engine that actually runs containers. Kubernetes supports any CRI-compatible runtime: containerd (default since v1.24), CRI-O (Red Hat), or others. Docker's built-in runtime (dockershim) was removed in v1.24. On EKS, the default is containerd. The runtime pulls images (from ECR!), creates containers, and manages their lifecycle.
🏗️ DEEP DIVE: kubelet — The Bridge Between API and Containers

The kubelet is NOT a container — it's a SYSTEMD SERVICE running directly on the node's OS. It's the ONLY component that talks to both the API server AND the container runtime.

1
Register Node

Registers itself with the API server (or joins an existing cluster).

2
Watch for Pods

Continuously watches the API server: "Any Pods assigned to MY node?"

3
Create Pod

a. Call CRI (containerd): "Pull image from ECR"   b. Call CNI: "Set up network, assign IP"   c. Call CSI: "Mount volumes (EBS, EFS)"   d. Start the container

4
Run Probes Continuously

Monitors health via three probe types (see below).

5
Report Status

Reports Pod status back to the API server every few seconds.

6
Restart on Crash

If a Pod crashes → goes back to step 3 (restart the container).

💓
livenessProbe

"Is the app alive?"
If fails → RESTART container.
Detects deadlocks, hung processes.

🚦
readinessProbe

"Can the app serve traffic?"
If fails → REMOVE from Service endpoints. Pod keeps running but gets no traffic.

🐢
startupProbe

"Has the app finished starting?"
Protects slow-starting apps. Until it succeeds, liveness/readiness are disabled.

📦
CRI

Container Runtime Interface
Talks to containerd / CRI-O.
Pull image, create/start/stop containers.

🌐
CNI

Container Network Interface
Sets up Pod networking.
Assigns IP, creates veth pairs, configures routes.

💾
CSI

Container Storage Interface
Mounts volumes into Pods.
EBS, EFS, FSx — any CSI driver.

💡 If kubelet stops — the failure cascade
  • Node → NotReady — scheduler stops placing new Pods on this node
  • Existing Pods keep running — the container runtime (containerd) is independent. Containers don't stop just because kubelet died.
  • After ~5 minutes — controller-manager detects the node is gone and starts evicting Pods, rescheduling them to healthy nodes.
🚦 DEEP DIVE: kube-proxy — iptables vs IPVS Modes

kube-proxy maintains network rules so traffic reaches the right Pods. It has TWO modes — understanding the difference matters for the exam:

⚡ IPTABLES MODE

Default in most clusters

  • Writes iptables rules on every node
  • For each Service: creates a chain of rules
  • Traffic to Service IP → random DNAT to a backend Pod IP

✅ PRO: Simple, battle-tested, works everywhere

❌ CON: O(n) lookup — slow with 1000+ Services

❌ CON: Random LB only (no least-connection)

🚀 IPVS MODE

Better for large clusters

  • Uses Linux IP Virtual Server (kernel-level L4 load balancer)
  • O(1) lookup — constant time regardless of Service count
  • Multiple scheduling algorithms: rr (round-robin), lc (least-connection), dh (destination hashing)

✅ PRO: Much faster at scale, more scheduling options

❌ CON: Requires IPVS kernel modules on every node

🎯 EXAM TIP

EKS defaults to iptables. IPVS requires extra setup (kernel modules + kube-proxy config change). For production at scale (>1000 Services), prefer IPVS. Know the tradeoffs: iptables = simple but O(n), IPVS = fast O(1) but needs kernel modules.

🐋 DEEP DIVE: CRI — Why dockershim Was Removed

Kubernetes doesn't care WHICH container runtime you use — as long as it speaks CRI (Container Runtime Interface) via gRPC.

kubelet
│
CRI (gRPC API — standard interface)
containerd
default on EKS
CRI-O
Red Hat alternative
││
└──────┴──────┘
runc (low-level OCI runtime)
│
Linux process
🗑️ DOCKERSHIM — REMOVED in Kubernetes v1.24

Docker's original runtime wasn't CRI-compatible, so Kubernetes had to include "dockershim" — a built-in translation layer between the kubelet and Docker's engine. This created three problems:

🔗
Unnecessary Complexity

An extra hop in the chain: kubelet → dockershim → dockerd → containerd → runc. Every layer adds latency and failure points.

🔧
Maintenance Burden

Kubernetes maintainers had to support Docker-specific code in-tree. Every Docker version change risked breaking K8s.

🔄
Docker Uses containerd Anyway

Docker itself calls containerd under the hood. Dockershim was just a middleman. Cutting it out simplified the stack.

💡 What this means for YOU
  • Your Docker images STILL WORK on Kubernetes! OCI images are universal — containerd and CRI-O can run them natively.
  • docker build still works fine. docker run for local dev is unchanged.
  • The only change: Kubernetes no longer uses dockershim as the intermediary. It now talks CRI directly to containerd.
  • On EKS: containerd is the default runtime, zero configuration needed.
💡 What this means for YOU
  • Your Docker images STILL WORK on Kubernetes! OCI images are universal — containerd and CRI-O can run them natively.
  • docker build still works fine. docker run for local dev is unchanged.
  • The only change: Kubernetes no longer uses dockershim as the intermediary. It now talks CRI directly to containerd.
  • On EKS: containerd is the default runtime, zero configuration needed.

4.7 How a Pod Gets Created — The Full Journey

This is the most important diagram in this chapter. Trace it until you can explain it from memory:

1
kubectl apply

You declare desired state in YAML

2
API Server

AuthN → AuthZ → Admission → etcd write

3
etcd

Desired state persisted with RAFT consensus

4
Deploy Ctrl

Creates ReplicaSet object

5
RS Ctrl

Creates 3 Pod objects (Pending)

6
Scheduler

Filter → Score → Assign to best node

7
kubelet

CRI pull image → Create container → Start

8
Report

Pod status Running → back to etcd

9
kube-proxy

Update iptables → traffic flows!

🚀 9-Step Pod Creation Journey — kubectl apply → Running Container

⏱️ ~2 seconds to ~60 seconds (depending on image pull). Every kubectl apply triggers this exact chain — understanding it is the foundation of K8s debugging.

STEP 1 👨‍💻 kubectl apply

kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml — "I want 3 anihpj Pods with image anihpj:v1" — sent over HTTPS to the API server.

STEP 2 📡 kube-apiserver

AuthN (WHO are you?) → AuthZ (CAN you?) → Validates YAML → Writes to etcd. The API server is the ONLY component that talks directly to etcd.

STEP 3 💾 etcd

"Deployment anihpj: 3 replicas DESIRED" — DESIRED STATE persisted. RAFT consensus saved across the etcd cluster. A watch event fires, notifying controllers.

STEP 4 🔄 Deployment Controller

"New Deployment! Creating ReplicaSet..." — The Deployment controller sees the new Deployment object and creates a ReplicaSet.

STEP 5 🔄 ReplicaSet Controller

"RS wants 3 Pods, currently 0 → create 3 Pod objects" — Pods created with status: Pending (no node assigned yet). A watch event alerts the scheduler.

STEP 6 🎯 kube-scheduler

FILTER: Remove nodes with taints, insufficient resources. SCORE: Rank remaining candidates → Node 3 wins (best fit). Sets Pod.spec.nodeName = "node-3".

STEP 7 🏗️ kubelet (on Node 3)

1. CRI: Pull image anihpj:v1 from ECR (or local cache). 2. Create container from image. 3. Start container (gunicorn, port 8000).

STEP 8 📡 kubelet → API Server → etcd

"Pod anihpj-7d8f9-x1 is now RUNNING on Node 3!" — Status reported back via the API server, persisted in etcd.

STEP 9 🚦 kube-proxy (ALL nodes)

"New Pod 10.0.3.45:8000 → updating iptables rules" — Now users hitting ALB → Service → Pod get a response!

⚡ Repeat Steps 6-9 for EACH of the 3 replicas!

If you set replicas: 3, the scheduler assigns each Pod independently (possibly to different nodes), and each goes through its own pull-create-start cycle on its assigned node.

🧩 How K8s Components Connect

etcd
💾
📡
API Server

Only component that writes to etcd. All others read/write through it.

↔ writes/reads
🎯
Scheduler

Watches API for unscheduled Pods. Assigns node, writes back via API.

↔ via API
🔄
Controller Mgr

Watches API for state drift. Takes corrective action via API.

↔ via API
🏗️
kubelet

Watches API for Pods on its node. Reports status back via API.

↔ via API
🚦
kube-proxy

Watches API for Services/Endpoints. Updates local iptables rules.

↔ via API
🐋
Container Runtime

Actually runs containers. kubelet tells it what to do via CRI.

← told by kubelet
~2s
Pod Start (cached img)
40s
Node NotReady Timeout
5min
Pod Eviction After Node Death
N/2+1
etcd Quorum Formula
3-5
etcd Nodes for HA

📊 Chapter 4 — Recap: Kubernetes Architecture

🌊Shipping PortControl Plane = Harbor, Workers = Docks, Pods = Ships
🧠Control PlaneAPI server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager
💪Worker Nodeskubelet, kube-proxy, container runtime (containerd)
