🏋️ Drill 4: Trace the Pod Creation Journey

Deploy anihpj to your cluster and trace every step a Pod takes from kubectl apply to running container. (1) Create a Deployment with 3 replicas, (2) Watch the events with kubectl get events --watch, (3) Identify which component handled each step (scheduler, kubelet, etc.), (4) Intentionally crash a Pod and observe the self-healing response.

  1. Write the Deployment YAML for anihpj with 3 replicas
  2. Apply it and list the sequence of events in order
  3. Map each event to the K8s component responsible
  4. Delete one Pod and time how long recovery takes
💡 Hint: kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml && kubectl get events --watch. You'll see: Scheduled → Pulling → Pulled → Created → Started. Delete a Pod: kubectl delete pod <name> → watch ReplicaSet recreate it.

📝 Chapter 4 — AWS DevOps Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

You deploy anihpj on EKS. The Deployment specifies replicas: 3. One of the worker nodes suddenly fails. What happens to the Pods on that node?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

The controller-manager detects the node failure, marks the Pods for eviction, and the ReplicaSet controller creates replacement Pods on healthy nodes. Within minutes, 3 Pods are running again — the desired state is restored automatically.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Node Failure Recovery — Step by Step
# 1. kubelet on failed node stops reporting (heartbeat lost)
# 2. Node controller: marks node as NotReady after 40s
# 3. After 5 min: Pods on dead node marked for eviction
# 4. ReplicaSet controller: "Actual=0, Desired=3 → create 3"
# 5. Scheduler assigns new Pods to healthy nodes
# 6. kubelet on healthy nodes starts new containers
#
# RESULT: Zero human intervention. Self-healing in action!
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# BEFORE FAILURE:          AFTER RECOVERY:
# Node 1: [Pod A✓][Pod B] Node 1: [Pod A✓][Pod B][Pod D✓ NEW]
# Node 2: [Pod C✓]        Node 2: [Pod C✓][Pod E✓ NEW]
# Node 3: DEAD 💀          Node 3: (empty—drained)
Q2

Which component is responsible for deciding which node a new Pod should run on?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kube-scheduler. It watches for Pods with no nodeName, filters out unsuitable nodes (insufficient resources, taints), scores the remaining candidates, and binds the Pod to the best node.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Scheduler Decision Process
# FILTER Phase (remove unfit):
#   Node 1: Tainted NoSchedule → REMOVED
#   Node 2: Only 128Mi free (needs 256Mi) → REMOVED
#   Node 3: 4Gi free, no taints → CANDIDATE
#   Node 4: 2Gi free, no taints → CANDIDATE
#
# SCORE Phase (rank best):
#   Node 3: LeastRequestedPriority=9.2 → WINNER!
#   Node 4: LeastRequestedPriority=7.1
#
# Pod bound to Node 3: pod.spec.nodeName = "node-3"
Q3

What is the role of etcd in a Kubernetes cluster?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

etcd is the distributed key-value store that holds ALL cluster state — every Pod, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, and Node definition. It's the "source of truth" for the entire cluster. All cluster data lives in etcd. Losing etcd without a backup = losing the entire cluster.

📖 EXPLANATION
# etcd in EKS: AWS manages etcd for you (HA, encrypted, backed up)
# For self-managed clusters, you MUST:
# 1. Run etcd as a 3-5 node cluster (RAFT quorum = N/2+1)
# 2. Backup regularly: etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd.db
# 3. Store backups off-cluster (S3 with cross-region replication)
# 4. Test restore: etcdctl snapshot restore /backup/etcd.db
#
# ON EKS: kubectl get all --all-namespaces
# All of this data? Stored in etcd (AWS-managed).
Q4

What happens when the kube-apiserver goes down in an EKS cluster?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Existing workloads continue running unaffected. However, you cannot deploy new Pods, modify resources, or query the cluster with kubectl until the API server recovers. The cluster is "headless" — the body (worker nodes) keeps running, but the brain's front door is closed.

📖 EXPLANATION
# API Server Down — What STOPS and What KEEPS RUNNING
# STOPS:                      KEEPS RUNNING:
# kubectl commands            Existing Pods (already scheduled)
# New deployments             kubelet (node agent)
# Scheduler decisions         kube-proxy (networking)
# API queries                 Container runtime
#                             Auto-healing (kubelet restarts local Pods)
#
# EKS: AWS manages HA API server (multi-AZ, auto-recovery)
# Self-managed: Run 3 API server replicas behind a load balancer
Q5

Which component is responsible for actually pulling the container image and starting the container on a worker node?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubelet. The kubelet receives Pod specs from the API server, calls the container runtime (containerd) via CRI to pull the image and create/start the container. It also runs health probes and reports status back to the API server.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Container Startup Flow
# kubelet (on Node-3)
#   │
#   ├─ 1. Watch API server: "Pod X assigned to me?"
#   ├─ 2. Call CRI (containerd): "Pull anihpj:v1 from ECR"
#   ├─ 3. Create container from image
#   ├─ 4. Start container (runs CMD: gunicorn on :8000)
#   ├─ 5. Run liveness probe: GET /healthz every 10s
#   ├─ 6. Run readiness probe: GET /ready every 5s
#   └─ 7. Report Pod status → API server → etcd
#
# Container runtime (containerd) does the actual work.
# kubelet is the ORCHESTRATOR on the node.
Q6

A Deployment specifies replicas: 5. Due to a bug, 2 Pods crash. What Kubernetes component ensures 5 Pods are running again?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

The ReplicaSet controller (part of kube-controller-manager). It continuously compares desired replicas (5) vs actual (3 after crash) and creates 2 new Pods to restore the desired state.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Reconciliation Loop — ReplicaSet Controller
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# LOOP (runs continuously):
#   1. OBSERVE: "Deployment anihpj, replicas: 5"
#              "Currently running: Pod-1✓ Pod-2✓ Pod-3✓"
#              "Pod-4💀 crashed, Pod-5💀 crashed"
#   2. DIFF:    Desired=5, Actual=3, Gap=2
#   3. ACT:     Create Pod-6, Pod-7
#
# This is the RECONCILIATION LOOP — the heart of K8s.
# Every controller (Deployment, ReplicaSet, Node, Job...)
# runs the same: OBSERVE → DIFF → ACT → REPEAT
Q7

In the shipping port analogy, what does the "Harbor Master" represent?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kube-controller-manager. Just as a Harbor Master ensures the port operates according to plan — ships are where they should be, missing ships are replaced — the controller-manager runs loops that maintain the desired state of the cluster.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Shipping Port → K8s Analogy Map
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# HARBOR ELEMENT         K8s COMPONENT
# Port Authority         Control Plane (overall)
# Signal Tower           kube-apiserver (all comms)
# Manifest Office        etcd (records everything)
# Dispatch Tower         kube-scheduler (pod placement)
# Harbor Master          kube-controller-manager (maintainer)
# Docks                  Worker Nodes
# Cranes                 kubelet (loads/unloads containers)
# Traffic Control        kube-proxy (network routing)
# Ships (berths)         Pods
# Cargo containers       Docker Containers
Q8

On EKS, who manages the Kubernetes control plane (API server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager)?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

AWS manages the control plane. EKS runs the API server, etcd, scheduler, and controller-manager across multiple AZs for high availability. You cannot SSH into control plane nodes — they're fully managed. You only manage worker nodes and what runs on them.

📖 EXPLANATION
# EKS Shared Responsibility
# AWS MANAGES:                     YOU MANAGE:
# • API server (HA, multi-AZ)      • Worker nodes (EC2 or Fargate)
# • etcd (encrypted, backed up)    • Pods, Deployments, Services
# • Scheduler                      • IAM roles (IRSA)
# • Controller-manager             • Network policies
# • Control plane upgrades         • Worker node upgrades
# • Control plane security         • Pod security
#
# EKS control plane = ~$0.10/hour (flat fee, not per use)
Q9

What does "declarative management" mean in Kubernetes?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

You declare WHAT you want (desired state in YAML), and Kubernetes continuously ensures the cluster matches that state. You don't give step-by-step instructions ("start this container on node 2"). You say "I want 3 copies of anihpj" and K8s figures out where and how.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Imperative vs Declarative
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# IMPERATIVE (step-by-step):
#   "SSH into node-2, run docker run anihpj:v1"
#   "SSH into node-3, run docker run anihpj:v1"
#   Problem: You must track WHERE everything is
#
# DECLARATIVE (desired state):
#   kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
#   ┌──────────────────────────┐
#   │ replicas: 3              │  ← You declare THIS
#   │ image: anihpj:v1         │
#   └──────────────────────────┘
#   K8s ensures: 3 Pods running, on best-fit nodes.
#   If a Pod dies: K8s replaces it. You don't lift a finger.
Q10

You run kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml for anihpj. List the components involved in order from the command to the running container.

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl → kube-apiserver → etcd → Deployment controller → ReplicaSet controller → Scheduler → kubelet → containerd → Running container → kube-proxy. This 9-step journey is the single most important concept in Kubernetes — trace it until you can explain it from memory.

📖 EXPLANATION
# The 9-Step Journey — Summary
# 1. kubectl apply → sends YAML over HTTPS
# 2. API server → auth, authorize, validate
# 3. etcd → stores desired state
# 4. Deployment controller → creates ReplicaSet
# 5. ReplicaSet controller → creates Pod objects
# 6. Scheduler → assigns Pod to best node
# 7. kubelet → pulls image, starts container
# 8. kubelet → reports "Running" back to API server
# 9. kube-proxy → updates network rules for new Pod
#
# Total time: ~2 seconds (cached image) to ~60 seconds (large pull)
# Watch it live: kubectl get events --watch

Chapter 5: Core Kubernetes Objects — Pods, Deployments, Services

You only need to understand 5 K8s objects to be productive: Pod (runs your container), Deployment (manages Pods), Service (network endpoint), ConfigMap (settings), and Secret (passwords). Everything else builds on these five. By the end, you'll be able to write YAML for deploying anihpj on any K8s cluster.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~4 hours ☸️ Hands-On K8s 🎯 DOP-C02: EKS Core

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Pods — the smallest deployable unit, shared networking, lifecycle phases
  • ✅ ReplicaSets — maintaining desired Pod count, self-healing
  • ✅ Deployments — rolling updates, rollbacks, zero-downtime
  • ✅ Services — stable networking (ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer)
  • ✅ Namespaces — virtual clusters for dev/staging/prod isolation
  • ✅ ConfigMaps & Secrets — configuration separate from code

5.1 Kubernetes Objects — What Are They?

Every Kubernetes resource is an object defined by 4 required YAML fields. K8s objects are persistent entities that represent the desired state of your cluster:

📋 Every K8s Object Has These 4 Fields (+1)

Every Kubernetes resource is a persistent entity defined by 4 required YAML fields — plus a 5th that K8s manages automatically:

1️⃣
apiVersion

WHICH API version?
apps/v1 · v1 · networking.k8s.io/v1
Determines the API group and version schema.

2️⃣
kind

WHAT are you creating?
Deployment · Pod · Service · Ingress
The resource type — must match apiVersion.

3️⃣
metadata

WHAT is it called?
name, labels, namespace, annotations
Uniquely identifies the object in the cluster.

4️⃣
spec

WHAT should it do?
replicas, selector, template, containers
DESIRED STATE — you declare it, K8s ensures it.

🔄 +1: status — The Reconciliation Target

While apiVersion, kind, metadata, and spec are the 4 you write, status is the 5th — managed entirely by K8s. It reports the ACTUAL STATE: how many replicas are really running, conditions, and other runtime info.

1
You declare spec

replicas: 3 — "I want 3 Pods"

2
Controller watches

Deployment controller sees the desired state

3
K8s reconciles

Creates/deletes Pods to match spec

4
K8s reports status

availableReplicas: 2 → actual Pods running

Controllers continuously reconcile spec → status in a loop: observe → diff → act. This is the heart of Kubernetes' declarative model.

YAML — Every K8s Object Template
apiVersion: apps/v1        # WHICH API version?
kind: Deployment           # WHAT are you creating?
metadata:                  # WHAT is it called?
  name: anihpj-deployment
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:                      # WHAT should it do? (DESIRED)
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: anihpj
        image: anihpj:v1
# status:                   # ← K8s writes this (ACTUAL)
#   availableReplicas: 2    #   YOU never set it!

5.2 Pods — The Smallest Deployable Unit

A Pod is the smallest deployable unit in Kubernetes. It's a group of one or more containers that share the same network namespace (localhost), IPC namespace, and volumes. Pods are ephemeral — they can die and be replaced at any time.

Pod Anatomy — Shared Resources + Containers
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  📦 Pod: anihpj-7d8f9-x1  (IP: 10.0.3.45)                 │
│  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  SHARED RESOURCES (all containers in this Pod):       │  │
│  │  • Network namespace (localhost works between them!)  │  │
│  │  • IPC namespace (shared memory)                      │  │
│  │  • Volumes (same filesystem)                           │  │
│  │  • Pod-level cgroups (CPU/Memory limits)              │  │
│  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
│  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐            │  │
│  │  │ Container 1     │  │ Container 2     │            │  │
│  │  │ anihpj (Django) │  │ nginx (proxy)   │            │  │
│  │  │ Port: 8000      │  │ Port: 80        │            │  │
│  │  └─────────────────┘  └─────────────────┘            │  │
│  │  They communicate via localhost:8000 ←→ localhost:80│  │
│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                                                            │
│  Pod Lifecycle: Pending → Running → Succeeded/Failed      │
│  RestartPolicy: Always (default) | OnFailure | Never      │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Pod YAML — anihpj
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj-pod
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:
  containers:
  - name: anihpj
    image: 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1
    ports:
    - containerPort: 8000
    resources:
      requests: {memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m"}
      limits:   {memory: "512Mi", cpu: "500m"}
📦 DEEP DIVE: Pod Lifecycle — Every State Explained

A Pod goes through a well-defined lifecycle. Understanding each phase helps you debug issues faster:

⏳
Pending
🏃
Running
✅
Succeeded
❌
Failed
⏳
PENDING

Pod accepted by API server but not running yet. Image being pulled, waiting for scheduler, waiting for volume provisioning, or init containers still running. Stuck? Run kubectl describe pod → check Events: "0/3 nodes available: insufficient memory".

🏃
RUNNING

All containers created, at least one running. Does NOT mean healthy! Container could be running but app still starting. Use readinessProbe to verify the app is actually serving traffic.

✅
SUCCEEDED

All containers terminated successfully (exit code 0). Only for Pods with restartPolicy: Never or OnFailure. Common for Jobs — run once, exit clean. The Pod stays around for log inspection.

❌
FAILED

All containers terminated, at least one with non-zero exit. Check kubectl logs <pod> --previous for the crash reason. The Pod may restart depending on restartPolicy.

🔄 CrashLoopBackOff — NOT a Phase, It's a Reason
  • K8s detects a container is crashing repeatedly. Instead of restarting immediately, it exponentially backs off: 10s → 20s → 40s → 80s → ... → max 5 minutes between restarts.
  • This prevents infinite crash-restart loops from consuming all cluster resources. The Pod shows STATUS: CrashLoopBackOff but is technically in the Running phase — just constantly restarting.
  • Debug: kubectl logs <pod> --previous to see the crash logs, then kubectl describe pod for the event timeline.
Restart Policies — spec.restartPolicy
spec:
  restartPolicy: Always       # Default. Restart on ANY exit. For long-running apps.
  restartPolicy: OnFailure     # Restart only if exit code ≠ 0. For Jobs/batch.
  restartPolicy: Never         # Pod exits and stays terminated. For one-shot tasks.
📦 DEEP DIVE: Init Containers & Sidecar Pattern

Pods can hold three types of containers, each serving a distinct purpose:

1️⃣
Init Containers

spec.initContainers

Run BEFORE app containers, run to COMPLETION. Sequential (init-1 → init-2 → ...). If any init fails, the whole Pod restarts.

2️⃣
App Containers

spec.containers

The main application. Runs for the Pod's entire lifetime. Supports liveness, readiness, and startup probes.

3️⃣
Sidecar Containers

K8s 1.29+: initContainers with restartPolicy: Always

Runs ALONGSIDE the main container for the Pod's life. Never stops unless Pod dies.

🔧 Init Container Use Cases
  • Wait for database: nc -z db 5432
  • Run migrations: python manage.py migrate
  • Download from S3 before app starts
  • Generate config from templates
  • Set file permissions on shared volumes
📎 Sidecar Use Cases
  • Log shipper: Fluentd → CloudWatch
  • Service mesh: Istio envoy proxy
  • Config reloader: watch ConfigMap changes
  • Metrics exporter: Prometheus sidecar
  • TLS termination proxy
Init Container Example — anihpj Pod
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj
spec:
  initContainers:
  - name: wait-for-db            # Runs FIRST — blocks until DB is ready
    image: busybox
    command: ['sh', '-c', 'until nc -z anihpj-db 5432; do sleep 2; done']
  - name: migrate-db             # Runs SECOND — applies Django migrations
    image: anihpj:v1
    command: ['python', 'manage.py', 'migrate']
  containers:                    # Runs AFTER all inits succeed
  - name: anihpj
    image: anihpj:v1
    ports:
    - containerPort: 8000

5.3 ReplicaSets — Ensuring Pod Availability

A ReplicaSet ensures a specified number of identical Pods are always running. If a Pod crashes or a node fails, the ReplicaSet creates replacements automatically. You rarely create ReplicaSets directly — Deployments manage them for you. But understanding how they work is essential for debugging.

🎯
spec.replicas

The desired count of Pods. "I want exactly 3 Pods at all times." The ReplicaSet controller continuously compares this with the actual count.

🏷️
spec.selector

Which Pods belong to this RS? Uses matchLabels (equality) or matchExpressions (set-based). Must match the template labels.

📋
spec.template

The Pod blueprint. When the RS needs more Pods, it stamps out copies using this template. Contains metadata.labels + spec.containers.

🔄 The Reconciliation Loop — How ReplicaSets Self-Heal

The ReplicaSet controller runs a continuous loop — this is the engine of Kubernetes' self-healing:

1
Observe

Controller checks: "How many Pods match my selector?" It counts all Pods whose labels match spec.selector.matchLabels.

2
Compare

"I want 3 (spec.replicas), but I have 2 → missing 1 Pod." Or "I have 5 → need to delete 2."

3
Act

If too few: create new Pods from spec.template. If too many: delete excess Pods (oldest first). Then go back to step 1.

This loop runs continuously, not just when you make changes. If a node dies, the controller sees missing Pods and recreates them on healthy nodes — automatically, within seconds.

Standalone ReplicaSet YAML (you'd normally let a Deployment create this)
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: ReplicaSet
metadata:
  name: anihpj-rs
spec:
  replicas: 3                        # Desired: 3 Pods
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj                     # RS owns Pods with this label
  template:                           # Pod blueprint
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj                   # MUST match selector!
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: anihpj
        image: anihpj:v2
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8000
🔍 Inspecting ReplicaSets
  • kubectl get rs — list all ReplicaSets
  • kubectl describe rs anihpj-rs — full details including events ("Created pod: anihpj-rs-x1k2m")
  • kubectl get rs -o wide — see which Pods belong to each RS
  • kubectl get pods --selector app=anihpj — find Pods by label (matches RS selector)
📊 ReplicaSet vs Deployment
  • ReplicaSet: Only ensures Pod count. No rolling updates, no rollbacks, no revision history.
  • Deployment: Creates ReplicaSets, manages rolling updates (new RS spun up, old scaled down), keeps revision history for rollbacks.
  • Rule: Always use Deployments. Only create ReplicaSets directly if you're writing a custom controller.

5.4 Deployments — Managing Application Lifecycle

A Deployment is the most common way to run applications on Kubernetes. It manages ReplicaSets and provides rolling updates (gradually replace old Pods with new ones, zero downtime) and rollbacks (kubectl rollout undo — instant!).

Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pod Hierarchy
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Deployment: anihpj (replicas: 3, strategy: RollingUpdate)  │
│  │                                                         │
│  ├── ReplicaSet: anihpj-v1-abc123  (revision 1, scaled 0) │
│  │   └── Pod × 0 (old version, no longer active)           │
│  │                                                         │
│  └── ReplicaSet: anihpj-v2-def456  (revision 2, active)    │
│      ├── Pod-1 (anihpj:v2, Node-1) ✅                      │
│      ├── Pod-2 (anihpj:v2, Node-2) ✅                      │
│      └── Pod-3 (anihpj:v2, Node-3) ✅                      │
│                                                            │
│  UPDATE: kubectl set image deploy/anihpj anihpj=v3         │
│  → Creates new RS, gradually replaces Pods, 0 downtime!    │
│  ROLLBACK: kubectl rollout undo deploy/anihpj              │
│  → Scales old RS back up, new RS down — instant!           │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Deployment YAML — anihpj
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: anihpj
        image: 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8000
        resources:
          requests: {memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m"}
          limits:   {memory: "512Mi", cpu: "500m"}
🚀 DEEP DIVE: Deployment Strategies — RollingUpdate vs Recreate
Deployments support TWO strategies for updating Pods:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  STRATEGY 1: RollingUpdate (default, recommended)                     │
│  ─────────────────────────────────────────────                        │
│                                                                       │
│  "Gradually replace old Pods with new ones — zero downtime!"          │
│                                                                       │
│  Timeline: anihpj v1 → v2 (3 replicas)                               │
│                                                                       │
│  Step 0: [v1] [v1] [v1]     ← all v1                                 │
│  Step 1: [v1] [v1] [v1] [v2]← v2 created (maxSurge=1)                │
│  Step 2: [v1] [v1]     [v2]← v1 removed (maxUnavailable=0)            │
│  Step 3: [v1] [v1] [v2] [v2]← another v2 created                     │
│  Step 4: [v1]     [v2] [v2]← another v1 removed                      │
│  Step 5: [v1] [v2] [v2] [v2]← last v2 created                        │
│  Step 6:      [v2] [v2] [v2]← ALL v2, zero downtime! ✅              │
│                                                                       │
│  Key parameters:                                                      │
│  • maxSurge: How many EXTRA Pods can exist during update (default 25%)│
│    → maxSurge=1 means at most 4 Pods during 3-replica update          │
│  • maxUnavailable: How many Pods can be UNAVAILABLE (default 25%)    │
│    → maxUnavailable=0 means NEVER drop below 3 serving Pods!         │
│                                                                       │
│  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
│  STRATEGY 2: Recreate                                                 │
│  ───────────────────                                                  │
│                                                                       │
│  "Kill ALL old Pods FIRST, then create new ones — DOWNTIME!"          │
│                                                                       │
│  Step 1: [v1] [v1] [v1] → ALL deleted → ❌ SERVICE DOWN              │
│  Step 2:                → [v2] [v2] [v2] created → ✅ BACK UP         │
│                                                                       │
│  ⚠️ Only use when:                                                    │
│     • You cannot have two versions running simultaneously             │
│       (e.g., database schema incompatible between versions)           │
│     • Downtime is acceptable (dev environment)                        │
│     • You need a full cleanup before starting fresh                   │
│                                                                       │
│  🎯 EXAM TIP: Default is RollingUpdate. If the question asks about    │
│     "zero-downtime deployment" → RollingUpdate is the answer.         │
│     maxUnavailable: 0 + maxSurge: 1 = guaranteed zero-downtime.       │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔄 DEEP DIVE: ReplicaSet Self-Healing — How It Detects Failure
How does the ReplicaSet controller KNOW a Pod died?

The ReplicaSet controller doesn't actively "ping" Pods. Instead, it
uses a WATCH MECHANISM on the API server:

1. RS Controller watches Pod events via the API server
2. kubelet on each node reports Pod status to API server
3. When kubelet detects container crash → reports "Pod-2 status: Failed"
4. API server updates etcd → fires watch event
5. RS Controller receives event: "Pod-2 Failed"
6. RS checks: "I need 3 running, Pod-2 is dead → create Pod-4"

The BEAUTY of this design:
  • RS controller does NOT connect directly to Pods
  • It does NOT ping containers
  • It ONLY watches the API server
  • If RS controller itself crashes → kubelet restarts it
  • When RS restarts, it reads DESIRED state from etcd:
    "Oh, I'm supposed to have 3 replicas!"
  • Then checks ACTUAL state: "Only 2 running"
  • Creates the 3rd — back to desired state!

💡 This is LEVEL-TRIGGERED (not edge-triggered): the controller
   doesn't care about HOW we got here — it just ensures current
   state matches desired state. This is the core of K8s resilience.

5.5 Services — Exposing Your Application

Pod IPs are ephemeral — they change when Pods restart. Services provide a stable IP address and DNS name. This is why Services exist: Pods come and go, but the Service IP stays the same.

Kubernetes offers 4 Service types, each for different use cases:

🏠
ClusterIP

Default. Internal-only IP accessible within the cluster. Used for Pod-to-Pod communication.

🚪
NodePort

Exposes the Service on each Node's IP at a static port (30000-32767). Good for dev/testing.

☁️
LoadBalancer

Creates an AWS ALB/NLB that routes external traffic to the Service. Production standard on EKS.

🔗
ExternalName

Maps a Service to an external DNS name (CNAME). Used for external services like RDS.

Service Types Traffic Flow
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ClusterIP (Internal Only):                                          │
│    Pod-A (10.0.1.5) ──▶ anihpj-svc:8000 (ClusterIP 10.100.50.2)    │
│                         ──▶ Backend Pod (10.0.3.45:8000)             │
│                                                                      │
│  NodePort (Dev/Test):                                                │
│    curl http://<node-ip>:30080 ──▶ NodePort 30080                  │
│                                      ──▶ ClusterIP ──▶ Backend Pod  │
│                                                                      │
│  LoadBalancer (Production — EKS):                                    │
│    User ──▶ ALB (anihpj-alb-123.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com)         │
│              ──▶ NodePort ──▶ ClusterIP ──▶ Backend Pod              │
│                                                                      │
│  ExternalName (External Services):                                   │
│    Pod ──▶ rds-svc ──▶ CNAME ──▶ anihpj-db.abc123.rds.amazonaws.com │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

5.5c Service YAML for anihpj

Here's the complete Service YAML for exposing the anihpj Django app. Note how selector: app: anihpj connects this Service to all Pods with that label:

Service YAML — anihpj
# Internal (ClusterIP) — for pod-to-pod communication
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-svc
  namespace: anihpj
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector:
    app: anihpj          # Must match Pod template labels!
  ports:
  - name: http
    port: 80             # Service port (others see this)
    targetPort: 8000     # Container port (where Django listens)
    protocol: TCP
---
# Production (LoadBalancer) — ALB integration
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-lb
  namespace: anihpj
  annotations:
    # AWS Load Balancer Controller annotations
    service.beta.kubernetes.io/aws-load-balancer-type: "external"
    service.beta.kubernetes.io/aws-load-balancer-nlb-target-type: "ip"
    service.beta.kubernetes.io/aws-load-balancer-scheme: "internet-facing"
spec:
  type: LoadBalancer
  selector:
    app: anihpj
  ports:
  - port: 443
    targetPort: 8000
    protocol: TCP

5.5d Ingress — Advanced Routing

While Services handle simple Layer 4 (TCP) routing, Ingress provides Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS) routing: path-based routing, host-based routing, TLS termination, and more. On EKS, the AWS Load Balancer Controller automatically provisions an ALB when you create an Ingress resource.

Ingress YAML — anihpj with ALB
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: anihpj-ingress
  namespace: anihpj
  annotations:
    kubernetes.io/ingress.class: "alb"
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/scheme: "internet-facing"
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/target-type: "ip"
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/listen-ports: '[{"HTTPS":443}]'
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/certificate-arn: "arn:aws:acm:..."
    alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/healthcheck-path: "/health/"
spec:
  rules:
  - host: anihpj.example.com
    http:
      paths:
      - path: /api/
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: anihpj-api-svc
            port:
              number: 80
      - path: /
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: anihpj-svc
            port:
              number: 80
💡
Service vs Ingress — Quick Decision Guide

Use Service (ClusterIP/NodePort/LoadBalancer): Simple TCP exposure, one service per port, internal communication. Use Ingress: Need path-based routing (/api vs /), host-based routing (api.example.com vs app.example.com), TLS termination at the edge, or multiple services behind one ALB. In production on EKS, you typically use BOTH: ClusterIP Service internally + Ingress/ALB externally.

5.6 Namespaces — Organizing Resources

Namespaces are virtual clusters within a physical cluster. They isolate resources — anihpj-dev, anihpj-staging, and anihpj-prod can coexist in the same cluster without interfering with each other. Use ResourceQuotas to limit namespace resource consumption.

🏠
default

Your home namespace. If you don't specify a namespace, everything lands here. Don't use it for production — it offers zero isolation.

⚙️
kube-system

System components: CoreDNS, kube-proxy, metrics-server, AWS Load Balancer Controller. Never touch this unless you know exactly what you're doing.

📢
kube-public

Publicly readable data (even without auth). Contains the cluster-info ConfigMap used by kubeadm join. Rarely used beyond cluster bootstrap.

💓
kube-node-lease

Node heartbeat leases (K8s 1.14+). Each node has a Lease object here. Lightweight — improves performance over the old node-status heartbeat method.

Namespace Boundaries — dev/staging/prod in One Cluster
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  KUBERNETES CLUSTER                                                 │
│  ┌───────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐ │
│  │ Namespace: anihpj │ │ Namespace: anihpj │ │ Namespace: anihpj │ │
│  │        -dev       │ │     -staging      │ │       -prod       │ │
│  │                   │ │                   │ │                   │ │
│  │ 📦 Pod (2 replic) │ │ 📦 Pod (3 replic) │ │ 📦 Pod (5 replic) │ │
│  │ 🔧 Service (int)  │ │ 🔧 Service (int)  │ │ 🔧 Service (LB)   │ │
│  │ 🗂️ ConfigMap      │ │ 🗂️ ConfigMap      │ │ 🗂️ ConfigMap      │ │
│  │ 🔐 Secret         │ │ 🔐 Secret         │ │ 🔐 Secret         │ │
│  │ 📊 ResourceQuota  │ │ 📊 ResourceQuota  │ │ 📊 ResourceQuota  │ │
│  │    CPU: 2, Mem:4Gi│ │    CPU: 4, Mem:8Gi│ │   CPU: 16, Mem:32G│ │
│  └───────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘ │
│                                                                     │
│  Cross-namespace DNS: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local      │
│  Example: anihpj-svc.anihpj-dev.svc.cluster.local:8000              │
│                                                                     │
│  Default namespaces: default (your stuff), kube-system (system),    │
│  kube-public (public data), kube-node-lease (node heartbeats)       │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📋 Essential Namespace Commands
  • kubectl create ns anihpj-dev — create a namespace
  • kubectl get namespaces — list all namespaces
  • kubectl get pods -n anihpj-dev — list pods in a specific namespace
  • kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj-dev — switch default namespace
  • kubectl get pods --all-namespaces — see pods across ALL namespaces
  • kubectl delete ns anihpj-dev — delete namespace (cascades — deletes everything inside!)
⚠️ What Namespaces Do NOT Isolate
  • Network: Pods in different namespaces CAN communicate by default (via DNS). Use NetworkPolicies to block cross-namespace traffic.
  • Nodes: Namespaces don't separate workloads onto different nodes. Use taints/tolerations or nodeSelectors for that.
  • CRDs: Most Custom Resource Definitions are cluster-scoped, not namespaced.

💡 Rule: Namespaces = logical grouping. NetworkPolicy = actual isolation. Always use BOTH for multi-tenant clusters.

ResourceQuota — Limit Namespace Consumption
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata:
  name: anihpj-dev-quota
  namespace: anihpj-dev
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "2"
    requests.memory: "4Gi"
    limits.cpu: "4"
    limits.memory: "8Gi"
    pods: "10"
    services: "5"
    persistentvolumeclaims: "3"

5.7 Labels, Selectors & Annotations — The Glue

Labels are key-value pairs attached to objects for organization and selection. Selectors filter objects by labels (the core mechanism connecting Services to Pods, Deployments to ReplicaSets). Annotations store non-identifying metadata for tools.

🏷️
Labels

Key-value pairs for identification and grouping. Used by selectors to connect objects. Examples: app: anihpj, env: prod, tier: backend. Limited to 63 chars per key/value, must start/end with alphanumeric.

🎯
Selectors

Filter objects by labels. Two types: equality-based (=, !=) and set-based (in, notin, exists). This is how a Service finds its Pods and a Deployment finds its ReplicaSets.

📝
Annotations

Key-value pairs for non-identifying metadata. NOT used by selectors. Store tool-specific info: ALB controller config, Prometheus scrape settings, build IDs, contact info. Much larger (256KB vs 63 chars).

📖 RECOMMENDED LABELS (app.kubernetes.io/*)
metadata:
  labels:
    app.kubernetes.io/name: anihpj        # Application name
    app.kubernetes.io/instance: anihpj-prod # Unique instance ID
    app.kubernetes.io/version: "v1.2.3"     # Current version
    app.kubernetes.io/component: backend    # Role in architecture
    app.kubernetes.io/part-of: anihpj       # Higher-level app name
    app.kubernetes.io/managed-by: helm      # Tool managing the app
    app.kubernetes.io/created-by: devops-team # Team responsible
= Equality-Based Selectors

Simple = / != matching. Used by Services and ReplicaSets.

# Service selects Pods with app=anihpj AND env=prod
selector:
  app: anihpj
  env: prod

# kubectl filter
kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj,env=prod
kubectl get pods -l 'env!=production'
∈ Set-Based Selectors

More flexible: in, notin, exists. Used by Deployments, NetworkPolicies, Node affinity.

# Deployment selects env in (staging,prod)
selector:
  matchExpressions:
  - {key: env, operator: In, values: [staging, prod]}
  - {key: critical, operator: Exists}

# kubectl filter
kubectl get pods -l 'env in (dev,staging)'
kubectl get pods -l '!critical'
📝 Real-World Annotation Examples
⚖️
AWS Load Balancer Controller
annotations:
  alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/scheme: internet-facing
  alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/target-type: ip
  alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/healthcheck-path: /health
📊
Prometheus Scrape Config
annotations:
  prometheus.io/scrape: "true"
  prometheus.io/port: "8000"
  prometheus.io/path: "/metrics"

5.8 ConfigMaps & Secrets — Configuration Management

ConfigMaps store non-sensitive configuration (environment variables, config files). Secrets store sensitive data (passwords, tokens). Both can be mounted as environment variables or volumes. On AWS, integrate with AWS Secrets Manager via the External Secrets Operator for automatic rotation.

ConfigMap + Secret — anihpj
# ConfigMap — non-sensitive settings
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: anihpj-config
data:
  DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE: "anihpj.settings"
  ALLOWED_HOSTS: "anihpj.example.com"
  LOG_LEVEL: "INFO"
---
# Secret — sensitive data (base64-encoded)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: anihpj-secrets
type: Opaque
data:
  DATABASE_URL: cG9zdGdyZXM6Ly8uLi4=  # base64 encoded
  SECRET_KEY: YW5paHBqLXNlY3JldC1rZXk=
External Secrets Operator — AWS Secrets Manager → K8s
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  AWS Secrets Manager  ────▶  External Secrets Operator  ────▶  K8s  │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌─────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐  │
│  │ AWS Secrets Mgr │     │ ExternalSecret   │     │ K8s Secret   │  │
│  │                 │     │ (Custom Resource)│     │              │  │
│  │ anihpj/db-pass  │────▶│ "Sync secret     │────▶│ db-password  │  │
│  │ anihpj/api-key  │     │  anihpj/db-pass  │     │ api-key      │  │
│  │                 │     │  every 5 min"    │     │              │  │
│  │ ✅ Automatic    │     │                  │     │ ✅ Synced    │  │
│  │    Rotation     │     │ IAM Role (IRSA)  │     │ ✅ Auto-updt │  │
│  └─────────────────┘     └──────────────────┘     └──────────────┘  │
│                                                                      │
│  Pod mounts K8s Secret via env var or volume — app code unchanged!  │
│  Benefits: Central secret mgmt, auto-rotation, audit trail, no      │
│  secrets in Git (not even base64!)                                    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Mount as Env Var vs Volume
# METHOD 1: Mount as environment variables (simplest)
env:
- name: DB_PASSWORD
  valueFrom:
    secretKeyRef:
      name: anihpj-secrets
      key: DATABASE_URL

# METHOD 2: Mount as files (auto-updating!)
volumes:
- name: secrets
  secret:
    secretName: anihpj-secrets
# Files created: /etc/secrets/DATABASE_URL, /etc/secrets/SECRET_KEY
# ⚡ Key advantage: Volume-mounted Secrets auto-update when the
# Secret changes. Env vars do NOT update — requires Pod restart.

🧩 How Core K8s Objects Connect

Pod
📦
🚀
Deployment

Creates & manages ReplicaSets. Handles rolling updates & rollbacks.

creates → RS
🔄
ReplicaSet

Ensures correct number of Pods. Auto-heals by creating replacements.

creates → Pod
🌐
Service

Stable IP + DNS. Routes traffic to Pods matching its label selector.

selects → Pod
⚙️
ConfigMap

Injects non-sensitive config as env vars or mounted files into Pods.

injects → Pod
🔐
Secret

Injects sensitive data (passwords, tokens) into Pods as env vars/files.

injects → Pod
🏠
Namespace

Virtual cluster boundaries. All objects above live inside a namespace.

contains → all
1
Deployment

You declare: replicas=3, strategy=RollingUpdate

2
ReplicaSet

Deployment creates RS. RS watches Pod count.

3
Pod

RS creates 3 Pods. Each gets unique IP.

4
Service

Service selects Pods by label. Gives stable IP.

5
Traffic

Users → ALB → Service → Pod (container:8000)

📊 Chapter 5 — Recap: Core K8s Objects

📦PodSmallest unit, shared network + storage, ephemeral IP
🔄ReplicaSetMaintains desired Pod count, auto-healing
🚀DeploymentRolling updates, rollbacks, versioned ReplicaSets
🌐ServiceStable IP/DNS, load-balances to Pods by label
🏠NamespaceVirtual clusters, dev/staging/prod isolation
⚙️Config+SecretConfiguration separate from container image
⏱️ 20 minutes
Scenario: Deploy anihpj on Kubernetes

You have the anihpj Docker image in ECR. Your task: (1) Write a Deployment YAML with 3 replicas, resource limits, and health checks; (2) Write a Service YAML to expose it internally; (3) Apply both to a namespace called "anihpj"; (4) Scale to 5 replicas; (5) Roll back after a bad update.

  1. Create namespace: kubectl create ns anihpj
  2. Write deployment.yaml (replicas: 3, image from ECR, requests: 256Mi/250m)
  3. Write service.yaml (ClusterIP, port 80 → targetPort 8000)
  4. Apply: kubectl apply -f . -n anihpj
  5. Simulate bad update: kubectl set image deploy/anihpj anihpj=anihpj:broken
  6. Rollback: kubectl rollout undo deploy/anihpj
💡 Hint: Use --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML skeletons. Service selector must match Pod template labels. Resources are required for production — never skip them!
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
# STEP 1: Create namespace
kubectl create ns anihpj
kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj

# STEP 2: Deploy
kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
kubectl apply -f service.yaml

# STEP 3: Verify
kubectl get pods -o wide
kubectl get svc
curl http://anihpj-svc.anihpj.svc.cluster.local

# STEP 4: Scale
kubectl scale deploy anihpj --replicas=5
kubectl get pods -w  # Watch pods spin up

# STEP 5: Bad update → Rollback
kubectl set image deploy/anihpj anihpj=anihpj:broken
kubectl rollout status deploy/anihpj  # Oops, failing!
kubectl rollout undo deploy/anihpj     # Instant recovery! 🎉

📝 Chapter 5 — AWS DevOps Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

A Pod running anihpj on EKS crashes. The Deployment specifies replicas: 3. What ensures that 3 Pods are running again?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

The ReplicaSet controller (managed by the Deployment). It continuously monitors running Pods. When actual count (2) ≠ desired (3), it creates a new Pod. This is the reconciliation loop at work.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pod hierarchy
# Deployment creates and manages ReplicaSets
# ReplicaSet ensures Pod count matches desired
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# Q1 Flow:
# 1. Pod crashes (container exits non-zero)
# 2. kubelet reports Pod status → API server
# 3. ReplicaSet controller: Actual=2, Desired=3
# 4. Creates new Pod via API server
# 5. Scheduler assigns Pod to node
# 6. kubelet starts new container
# Result: 3 Pods running again, no human needed!
Q2

You update the anihpj Deployment image to v2. Which strategy ensures zero downtime during the update?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

RollingUpdate (the default Deployment strategy). It gradually replaces old Pods with new ones — one at a time — ensuring that the desired number of Pods are always available to serve traffic.

📖 EXPLANATION
# RollingUpdate in action
kubectl set image deploy/anihpj anihpj=anihpj:v2
# K8s creates new ReplicaSet (v2), gradually:
# Step 1: New Pod-v2 created → becomes Ready
# Step 2: One old Pod-v1 terminated
# Step 3: Repeat until all Pods are v2
#
# kubectl rollout status deploy/anihpj  # Watch progress
# kubectl rollout undo deploy/anihpj     # Instant rollback!
Q3

What type of Kubernetes Service should you use to expose anihpj to the internet on EKS?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

LoadBalancer Service (or Ingress with AWS Load Balancer Controller). A LoadBalancer Service provisions an AWS ALB/NLB that routes external traffic to the Service, which load-balances across healthy Pods.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Service Types — External Access
# ClusterIP:   Internal only (Pod-to-Pod)
# NodePort:    NodeIP:30000-32767 (dev only)
# LoadBalancer: AWS ALB/NLB (PRODUCTION) ← Q3 answer
#
# On EKS, AWS Load Balancer Controller creates:
# ALB (Layer 7) for HTTP/HTTPS with path routing
# NLB (Layer 4) for TCP/UDP with high throughput
Q4

Why do Pods have ephemeral IPs and why are Services necessary?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Pods are mortal — they die and are replaced with NEW IPs. Services provide a stable, unchanging IP/DNS name that load-balances traffic to the current set of healthy Pods matched by label selectors.

📖 EXPLANATION
# The Problem Services Solve
# Pod-1 starts: IP 10.0.1.5 (users connect here)
# Pod-1 dies, replaced by Pod-4: IP 10.0.1.8 (different!)
# Without Service: users need to know new IP → BROKEN
#
# WITH Service:
# Service anihpj-svc: ClusterIP 10.100.50.2 (NEVER changes)
# DNS: anihpj-svc.default.svc.cluster.local
# Selector: app=anihpj → routes to Pod-1, Pod-2, Pod-3
# Pods come and go — Service IP stays the same forever
Q5

Where should you store the anihpj database password in Kubernetes?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

In a Kubernetes Secret, never in a ConfigMap or Pod spec. Secrets are specifically designed for sensitive data. On AWS, use the External Secrets Operator to sync from AWS Secrets Manager for automatic rotation.

📖 EXPLANATION
# ConfigMap vs Secret — KNOW THE DIFFERENCE
# ConfigMap: Non-sensitive data
#   • Environment variables (DEBUG, LOG_LEVEL)
#   • Config files (nginx.conf, Django settings)
#   • Plain text, no encryption by default
#
# Secret: Sensitive data
#   • Passwords, API keys, tokens, certificates
#   • Base64 encoded (NOT encrypted — use etcd encryption!)
#   • On EKS: integrate with AWS KMS for envelope encryption
#
# AWS Best Practice: External Secrets Operator
#   K8s Secret ← sync ← AWS Secrets Manager (auto-rotates!)
Q6

You deploy anihpj-dev in namespace "dev" and anihpj-prod in namespace "prod" on the same EKS cluster. Can a Pod in "dev" reach a Service in "prod"?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Yes, by default. Namespaces provide logical isolation, not network isolation. A Pod in "dev" can reach a Service in "prod" using the fully qualified DNS name: anihpj-svc.prod.svc.cluster.local. Use NetworkPolicies to restrict cross-namespace traffic.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Cross-Namespace DNS Format
# <service-name>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local
#
# Dev Pod → Prod Service:
#   curl http://anihpj-svc.prod.svc.cluster.local:8000
#   → WORKS by default! (no network isolation)
#
# To BLOCK cross-namespace traffic, use NetworkPolicy:
#   podSelector: {}
#   ingress:
#   - from:
#     - namespaceSelector:
#         matchLabels:
#           name: prod  # Only allow from prod namespace
Q7

What command would you use to immediately roll back an anihpj Deployment to the previous version?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl rollout undo deployment/anihpj. This instantly scales the previous ReplicaSet back up and scales the current one down. No downtime. No manual steps. One command.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Rollback commands — memorize these!
kubectl rollout history deployment/anihpj    # See revision history
kubectl rollout undo deployment/anihpj       # Rollback to previous
kubectl rollout undo deployment/anihpj \
  --to-revision=2                             # Rollback to specific revision
kubectl rollout status deployment/anihpj     # Watch rollback progress

# What happens internally:
# Revision 1: anihpj-v1-abc (scaled to 0, now scales UP)
# Revision 2: anihpj-v2-def (scaled to 3, now scales DOWN)
# Service continues routing to healthy Pods throughout
Q8

Two containers in the same Pod need to communicate. What address should they use?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

localhost. Containers in the same Pod share the network namespace. Container-1 on port 8000 can reach Container-2 on port 80 via localhost:80. They share the same IP address and port space.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Pod Networking — Shared Namespace
# Pod IP: 10.0.3.45 (shared by ALL containers in the Pod)
# ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
# │ Container A (anihpj)            │
# │ Listens on :8000                │
# │ Can reach Container B at        │
# │   localhost:80 ← SAME NETWORK!  │
# ├─────────────────────────────────┤
# │ Container B (nginx sidecar)     │
# │ Listens on :80                  │
# │ Proxies to localhost:8000       │
# └─────────────────────────────────┘
# Pod-to-Pod: Use Service DNS
# Container-to-Container (same Pod): Use localhost
Q9

What is the relationship between a Deployment, ReplicaSet, and Pod?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Deployment → manages → ReplicaSet → manages → Pods. Deployment defines the desired state (replicas, image, update strategy). ReplicaSet ensures the correct number of Pods are running. Pods run the actual containers.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Object Hierarchy
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────
# Deployment (anihpj)
#   │  replicas: 3, strategy: RollingUpdate
#   │  Records revision history (v1, v2, v3...)
#   │
#   ├── ReplicaSet (anihpj-v1-abc123)
#   │   │  revision: 1, replicas: 0 (scaled down)
#   │   └── Pod × 0
#   │
#   └── ReplicaSet (anihpj-v2-def456)
#       │  revision: 2 (current), replicas: 3
#       ├── Pod anihpj-v2-def456-x1 (Running)
#       ├── Pod anihpj-v2-def456-x2 (Running)
#       └── Pod anihpj-v2-def456-x3 (Running)
#
# You create a Deployment. K8s creates RS + Pods automatically.
Q10

A ConfigMap is updated with new settings. Will the running anihpj Pods automatically pick up the changes?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

No, not by default. Environment variables from ConfigMaps are set at Pod startup and don't update. Files mounted from ConfigMaps DO update (with a delay of ~60 seconds via kubelet sync). For immediate updates, use kubectl rollout restart deployment/anihpj to restart Pods.

📖 EXPLANATION
# ConfigMap Update Behavior
# Env vars from ConfigMap:  ❌ NOT auto-updated
#   Pod must be restarted (kubectl rollout restart)
#
# Volume mounts from ConfigMap: ✅ Auto-updated
#   kubelet syncs every ~60 seconds
#   App must watch file changes (inotify)
#
# Best Practice: Use volume mounts + file watchers
# OR use immutable ConfigMaps with new names per version
#   → Rolling update replaces old Pods with new ones

Chapter 6: kubectl — Your Kubernetes Command Center

kubectl is your primary tool for interacting with Kubernetes. Every command you run — deploying anihpj, scaling Pods, debugging crashes — goes through kubectl → kubeconfig → API server. Master these commands and you'll be productive on any K8s cluster.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours ☸️ CLI Tool 🎯 DOP-C02: Essential

6.1 kubectl Basics — The Universal K8s Tool

Syntax: kubectl <verb> <resource> [flags]. Connects to clusters via kubeconfig file (~/.kube/config). Multiple contexts for multiple clusters.

kubectl → kubeconfig → API Server Flow
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  🖥️  YOUR TERMINAL                ☸️  KUBERNETES CLUSTER     │
│  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐     │
│  │   kubectl    │─▶│  kubeconfig   │─▶│ kube-        │     │
│  │   command    │  │  (~/.kube/    │  │ apiserver    │     │
│  │ kubectl get  │  │   config)     │  │ Authenticate │     │
│  │ pods         │  │ • cluster URL │  │ + Authorize  │     │
│  │ kubectl      │  │ • certificate │  │ + Validate   │     │
│  │ apply -f     │  │ • user creds  │  │ Execute!     │     │
│  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘     │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔌 DEEP DIVE: kubeconfig File — Clusters, Users, Contexts
The kubeconfig file (~/.kube/config) has THREE key sections:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  clusters:          "WHERE am I connecting?"                          │
│    - name: eks-prod                                                   │
│      cluster:                                                         │
│        server: https://ABC123.gr7.us-east-1.eks.amazonaws.com        │
│        certificate-authority-data: LS0tLS... (base64 CA cert)        │
│                                                                       │
│  users:             "WHO am I?"                                       │
│    - name: admin                                                     │
│      user:                                                            │
│        exec:                                                          │
│          apiVersion: client.authentication.k8s.io/v1beta1            │
│          command: aws                                                 │
│          args: ["eks", "get-token", "--cluster-name", "eks-prod"]    │
│          # On EKS: kubectl calls "aws eks get-token" to get IAM creds│
│                                                                       │
│  contexts:          "WHICH cluster + WHICH user + WHICH namespace?"   │
│    - name: eks-prod-admin                                             │
│      context:                                                         │
│        cluster: eks-prod                                              │
│        user: admin                                                    │
│        namespace: anihpj-prod                                         │
│                                                                       │
│  current-context: eks-prod-admin  ← what "kubectl" uses by default   │
│                                                                       │
│  🎯 ESSENTIAL COMMANDS:                                               │
│     kubectl config view                    # See full config          │
│     kubectl config get-contexts            # List all contexts        │
│     kubectl config use-context NAME        # Switch context           │
│     kubectl config current-context         # Show current             │
│     kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj-dev      │
│                                                                       │
│  ⚠️ DANGER: Running kubectl against the wrong context is the #1 cause│
│     of production incidents. Always verify:                           │
│     kubectl config current-context  BEFORE  kubectl delete -f .      │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

6.2 Imperative vs Declarative — When to Use Each

This is one of the most important conceptual distinctions in Kubernetes. Mastering it separates beginners from professionals:

🏃 IMPERATIVE — "Do THIS now"

You tell K8s exactly what to do step by step. K8s executes your commands immediately. Think of it like a recipe: "chop onions, heat oil, add onions, stir 5 min..."

  • kubectl run nginx --image=nginx
  • kubectl create deploy web --image=app:v1
  • kubectl expose deploy web --port=80
  • kubectl scale deploy web --replicas=5
  • kubectl label pod app=web

✅ BEST FOR: Exam speed, quick debugging, one-off tasks, learning/exploration

❌ WORST FOR: Production, team collaboration, audit trails, reproducibility

📝 DECLARATIVE — "Make it LIKE this"

You describe the desired end state in YAML/JSON. K8s figures out HOW to get there. Think of it like a blueprint: "build a 3-bedroom house with these specs on this lot."

  • kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
  • kubectl apply -f service.yaml
  • kubectl apply -k ./overlays/prod (Kustomize)
  • Git commit → PR review → CI/CD applies
  • ArgoCD/Flux watches Git → auto-syncs cluster

✅ BEST FOR: Production, GitOps, team collaboration, audit compliance, disaster recovery

❌ WORST FOR: Exam speed (too slow), exploratory debugging, quick temporary fixes

Same Result, Two Approaches — Create a Deployment with 3 replicas
# IMPERATIVE: 2 commands, 5 seconds. Great for exam. Not in Git.
kubectl create deploy anihpj --image=anihpj:v1
kubectl scale deploy anihpj --replicas=3

# DECLARATIVE: Write YAML, git add, kubectl apply. In Git forever.
# deploy.yaml:
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: anihpj
        image: anihpj:v1
# kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml  ← one command applies everything
🔄 GitOps — Declarative, Automated, Auditable

GitOps takes declarative to its logical conclusion: Git is the single source of truth. Instead of running kubectl apply manually, a controller (ArgoCD/Flux) watches your Git repo and automatically syncs the cluster to match. If someone changes a Deployment directly via kubectl edit, the GitOps controller reverts it within minutes — enforcing declarative state at all times.

  • Git repo = desired state. Every change is a PR with review, approval, and audit trail.
  • Controller = enforcer. Continuously reconciles: "Does the cluster match Git? If not, fix it."
  • Rollback = git revert + push. No kubectl needed. Your entire infrastructure history is in Git.
🎯 EXAM STRATEGY

Use imperative commands with --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML quickly during the exam, then kubectl apply the generated files. This gives you the speed of imperative with the reliability of declarative. Example: kubectl create deploy web --image=nginx --dry-run=client -o yaml > deploy.yaml && kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml. The $do alias (Section 6.3) automates this.

6.3 Essential kubectl Commands Reference

CategoryCommands
Viewkubectl get pods -o wide, kubectl describe pod <name>, kubectl logs -f <pod>, kubectl exec -it <pod> -- bash
Createkubectl apply -f manifest.yaml, kubectl create deploy anihpj --image=anihpj:v1
Modifykubectl edit deploy anihpj, kubectl scale deploy anihpj --replicas=5, kubectl set image deploy/anihpj anihpj=v2
Deletekubectl delete pod <name>, kubectl delete -f manifest.yaml, kubectl delete deploy,svc -l app=anihpj
Debugkubectl describe pod, kubectl get events --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp, kubectl top pods, kubectl port-forward pod/anihpj 8080:8000
Adminkubectl get nodes -o wide, kubectl cordon/uncordon/drain <node>
💡 RESOURCE SHORTCUTS (Memorize These!)

po=pods, svc=services, deploy=deployments, rs=replicasets, ns=namespaces, no=nodes, cm=configmaps, pv=persistentvolumes, ing=ingresses, sa=serviceaccounts. Pro tip: export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml" then k create deploy web --image=nginx $do > deploy.yaml

6.4 Output Formats & Filtering

FlagWhat It DoesExample
-o wideExtra columns (NODE, IP, NOMINATED NODE)kubectl get pods -o wide
-o yamlFull object as YAML — every field, great for debuggingkubectl get pod anihpj-x1 -o yaml
-o jsonFull object as JSON — for scripting (jq, Python)kubectl get deploy anihpj -o json
-o nameOnly resource names — useful for piping to deletekubectl get pods -o name
-o jsonpathExtract specific fields with JSONPath expressionskubectl get pods -o jsonpath='{.items[*].status.podIP}'
-o custom-columnsCustom table columns — cleaner than JSONPathkubectl get pods -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,IP:.status.podIP
🏷️ Label Selector (-l)

Filter by labels: kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj,env=prod. Supports =, !=, in, notin. The primary way to connect objects in K8s.

🔍 Field Selector

Filter by object fields: kubectl get pods --field-selector status.phase=Running. Use for spec.nodeName, metadata.namespace. Different from labels — these are built-in fields.

📊 Sorting

Sort by any field path: kubectl get pods --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp. Find crash-looping Pods with: --sort-by=.status.containerStatuses[0].restartCount.

👀 Watch Mode (-w)

Live-update output: kubectl get pods -w. See Pods transition Pending→Running in real-time. Perfect for watching rollouts or scaling events. Ctrl+C to stop.

🔬 Pro Filtering Patterns

Combine everything: kubectl get pods -A -l app=anihpj --field-selector status.phase=Running --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp -o wide — this shows all running anihpj Pods across all namespaces, sorted by age, with node info. Master the pipeline: | grep, | wc -l, | awk for power filtering.

6.5 Shortcuts & Time-Savers

⚡ The Two Commands That Will Save You Hours
  • 1. alias k=kubectl — saves 6 keystrokes × 100s of commands per day. Add to ~/.bashrc or ~/.zshrc permanently.
  • 2. export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml" — turns k create deploy web --image=nginx --dry-run=client -o yaml into k create deploy web --image=nginx $do > deploy.yaml. This is the #1 exam time-saver.
🗑️ Force Delete

k delete pod stuck-pod --force --grace-period=0

Instantly kills a Pod that's stuck in Terminating. Use with caution — bypasses graceful shutdown hooks. Add --wait=false to not even wait for confirmation.

🌐 All Namespaces

k get pods -A or k get pods --all-namespaces

See every Pod in the cluster at once. Combine: k get pods -A -l app=anihpj — find all anihpj instances regardless of where they live.

The Dry-Run YAML Generation Pipeline
💡Imperativek create deploy web --image=nginx $do
→
📄YAML File> deploy.yaml
→
✏️EditAdd resources, probes, env vars
→
✅Applykubectl apply -f deploy.yaml
🔑 THE $do PATTERN — Master This Before Your Exam

Set export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml" at the START of your exam terminal. Then generate ANY resource YAML in seconds:

k create deploy X --image=Y $do > file.yaml
k create service clusterip SVC --tcp=80:8000 $do > svc.yaml
k create configmap CFG --from-literal=KEY=val $do > cm.yaml
k run POD --image=IMG --restart=Never $do > pod.yaml

This pattern saves 25+ keystrokes per command and eliminates typos. Combined with alias k=kubectl, you'll generate YAML 3x faster than anyone typing commands manually.

Pro Shortcuts — Save These!
# Alias setup (add to ~/.bashrc or ~/.zshrc)
alias k=kubectl
export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml"

# Generate YAML without creating
k create deploy myapp --image=nginx $do > deploy.yaml
k create service clusterip my-svc --tcp=80:8000 $do > svc.yaml
k create configmap settings --from-literal=DEBUG=true $do > cm.yaml

# Force operations
k delete pod stuck-pod --force --grace-period=0
k delete pod stuck-pod --force --grace-period=0 --wait=false  # Don't even wait

# Watch resources live
k get pods -w                    # Watch all Pods
k get deployments -w             # Watch deployments
k rollout status deploy/anihpj   # Watch rollout progress

# Namespace shortcuts
k get pods -A                              # All namespaces
k get pods -n kube-system                  # Specific namespace
k config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj  # Set default ns
💡 EXAM TIP: The $do Pattern

Set export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml" at the START of your exam. Then every YAML generation becomes k create deploy X --image=Y $do > file.yaml. This saves you typing 25+ characters per command and prevents typos. Combine with alias k=kubectl for maximum speed.

🔍 DEEP DIVE: JSONPath — Extract Exactly What You Need

JSONPath lets you query Kubernetes objects like a database. Instead of parsing YAML with grep, you extract exactly the fields you need:

Basic Patterns — Single Field Extraction
# Get ALL Pod names
kubectl get pods -o jsonpath='{.items[*].metadata.name}'
# → anihpj-7d8f9-x1 anihpj-7d8f9-x2 anihpj-7d8f9-x3

# Get ALL Pod IPs
kubectl get pods -o jsonpath='{.items[*].status.podIP}'
# → 10.0.1.5 10.0.2.8 10.0.3.12

# Get node OS images (check what's running where)
kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{.items[*].status.nodeInfo.osImage}'
# → Bottlerocket OS 1.15.0 Amazon Linux 2
Advanced — Range Loops with Tabs & Newlines (Table Output)
kubectl get pods -o jsonpath='
  {range .items[*]}
  {.metadata.name}{"\t"}{.status.phase}{"\t"}{.status.podIP}{"\n"}
  {end}'

# OUTPUT (clean table!):
# anihpj-7d8f9-x1    Running    10.0.1.5
# anihpj-7d8f9-x2    Running    10.0.2.8
# anihpj-7d8f9-x3    Pending    <none>
📋 JSONPath

Powerful, flexible. Can extract nested fields, build custom output. Syntax: {.items[*].status.podIP}. Supports range, @, filters. Slightly harder to remember.

📊 Custom Columns

Simpler, more readable. Syntax: -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,IP:.status.podIP. No curly braces, no range loops. Use this for quick tables.

Sorting — Find What Matters Most
# Sort by creation time (newest last)
kubectl get pods --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp

# Find crash-looping Pods (highest restart count first)
kubectl get pods --sort-by=.status.containerStatuses[0].restartCount

# Sort nodes by CPU capacity
kubectl get nodes --sort-by=.status.capacity.cpu
🎯 EXAM: JSONPath + custom-columns appear frequently

Practice extracting Pod IPs, node names, and image versions. The difference: JSONPath is more powerful (range loops, nested fields), custom-columns is simpler for quick tables. Know both — the exam may ask for either.

🐛 DEEP DIVE: Systematic kubectl Debugging Workflow

When a Pod isn't working, follow this 7-step systematic flow. These 7 steps solve 95% of all Pod debugging scenarios:

1
Get Pods

Check STATUS column: Pending? CrashLoop? ErrImagePull?

2
Describe

Events at bottom reveal WHY it failed

3
Logs

Current logs, crashed logs (--previous), follow (-f)

4
Exec

Interactive shell inside the running container

5
Network

netshoot pod: nslookup, curl, traceroute

6
Port-Forward

Map localhost:8080 → Pod:8000 for browser access

7
Events

Cluster-wide event log, sorted by time

STEP 1: Is the Pod even running?
kubectl get pods -o wide
# Check STATUS: Pending? Running? CrashLoopBackOff? ErrImagePull?
# Each status tells a different story — diagnose accordingly.
STEP 2: What happened? (Events are gold!)
kubectl describe pod <name>
# Scroll to EVENTS at the bottom:
#   "Failed to pull image: authentication required" → ECR Auth issue
#   "0/3 nodes available: insufficient memory" → Resource issue
#   "FailedMount: volume already mounted" → PV issue
STEP 3: Check the logs
kubectl logs <pod>                        # Current container logs
kubectl logs <pod> -c <container>         # Multi-container Pods
kubectl logs <pod> --previous             # CRASHED container logs!
kubectl logs <pod> --tail=50 -f           # Last 50 lines + follow
STEP 4: Exec inside the container
kubectl exec -it <pod> -- bash                    # Interactive shell
kubectl exec -it <pod> -- python manage.py check  # Run Django checks
kubectl exec <pod> -- env                          # See environment vars
kubectl exec <pod> -- cat /etc/resolv.conf         # Check DNS config
STEP 5: Network debugging with netshoot
kubectl run debug --image=nicolaka/netshoot --rm -it -- bash
# Inside netshoot:
nslookup anihpj-svc.anihpj.svc.cluster.local  # DNS resolution
curl http://anihpj-svc:80/health/               # HTTP connectivity
traceroute 10.0.3.45                             # Network path
STEP 6: Port-forward for local debugging
kubectl port-forward pod/anihpj-7d8f9-x1 8080:8000
# Now open http://localhost:8080 in your browser!
# The Pod's port 8000 is mapped to your local port 8080.
STEP 7: Cluster-level events
kubectl get events --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp   # Sorted timeline
kubectl get events -w                                        # Watch live!
kubectl get events --field-selector involvedObject.name=anihpj-xxx
🎯 MEMORIZE: describe → logs → exec → port-forward

This 4-step core flow solves 95% of Pod debugging without needing to touch the cluster infrastructure. Start with describe (what happened?), then logs (what did the app say?), then exec (what's happening inside?), then port-forward (can I reach it locally?).

📊 Chapter 6 — Recap

🔌kubectl→kubeconfigCLI → config → API server
