Scenario: kubectl Speed Challenge

You're debugging an anihpj deployment. Complete these tasks using ONLY imperative kubectl commands: (1) List all Pods with their node names; (2) Describe the failing Pod; (3) View logs from the crashed container; (4) Exec into a running Pod; (5) Generate a new Deployment YAML for a test app without creating it.

💡 Hint: -o wide for node info, --previous for crash logs, -it for interactive exec, --dry-run=client -o yaml for YAML generation.
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
# 1. Pods with node names
kubectl get pods -o wide

# 2. Describe failing Pod
kubectl describe pod anihpj-7d8f9-x1

# 3. Crash logs
kubectl logs anihpj-7d8f9-x1 --previous

# 4. Exec into Pod
kubectl exec -it anihpj-7d8f9-x2 -- bash

# 5. Generate YAML without creating
kubectl create deploy test-app --image=nginx \
  --dry-run=client -o yaml > test-deploy.yaml

📝 Chapter 6 — Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

How do you generate a Deployment YAML file without actually creating the Deployment?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl create deployment anihpj --image=anihpj:v1 --dry-run=client -o yaml > deploy.yaml

📖 EXPLANATION
# --dry-run=client: Validate without sending to API server
# -o yaml: Output as YAML instead of creating
# Pipe to file for editing and version control
# This is THE exam speed technique!
Q2

How do you view the logs of a crashed Pod's previous instance?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl logs <pod> --previous (or -p). This shows logs from the PREVIOUS container instance — critical for debugging crash-looping Pods.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Debug crash-looping Pods
kubectl logs anihpj-7d8f9-x1 --previous  # Why did it crash?
kubectl describe pod anihpj-7d8f9-x1     # Check events
kubectl get events --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp
Q3

You need to run a one-time command inside a running anihpj Pod. How?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl exec -it <pod> -- python manage.py migrate. The -it flags give you interactive terminal. Double dash -- separates kubectl flags from the command.

📖 EXPLANATION
# kubectl exec examples
kubectl exec anihpj-7d8f9-x1 -- python manage.py migrate
kubectl exec -it anihpj-7d8f9-x1 -- bash    # Interactive shell
kubectl exec anihpj-7d8f9-x1 -- ls /app     # List files
Q4

What's the difference between kubectl create and kubectl apply?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

create is imperative — it creates the resource once and errors if it already exists. apply is declarative — it creates OR updates the resource to match the YAML. Use apply for production; create for one-off tasks.

📖 EXPLANATION
# create: Fails if resource already exists
kubectl create -f deploy.yaml  # Error: AlreadyExists

# apply: Creates OR updates (idempotent)
kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml   # Creates if new, updates if exists
# apply uses last-applied-configuration annotation to track changes
Q5

How do you scale an anihpj Deployment to 10 replicas?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl scale deployment anihpj --replicas=10 (imperative) OR update replicas: 10 in the YAML and kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml (declarative).

📖 EXPLANATION
# Imperative (fast, one-off):
kubectl scale deployment anihpj --replicas=10

# Declarative (version-controlled):
# Edit deploy.yaml: replicas: 10
kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml

# Verify:
kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj  # Should show 10 Pods
Q6

What is the difference between kubectl exec and kubectl logs?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl exec runs a command INSIDE a running container (e.g., kubectl exec -it pod/anihpj -- bash). kubectl logs prints the stdout/stderr output of a container (e.g., kubectl logs pod/anihpj). Use exec for interactive debugging, logs for viewing application output.

Q7

How do you view the logs of a crashed Pod's previous instance?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl logs pod/anihpj --previous. The --previous flag retrieves logs from the PREVIOUS container instance — critical for debugging CrashLoopBackOff, where the current container may not have produced useful logs before crashing.

Q8

What does kubectl get pods -o wide show that kubectl get pods does not?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

-o wide adds columns: IP address (Pod IP), NODE (which worker node), NOMINATED NODE, and READINESS GATES. Use kubectl get pods -o wide when you need to know which node a Pod is running on or its cluster IP for network debugging.

Q9

How do you edit a running Deployment's image without modifying the YAML file?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl set image deployment/anihpj anihpj=anihpj:v2. This imperatively updates the container image and triggers a rolling update. Combine with --record to save the command in rollout history for later rollback reference.

Q10

What command shows the rollout history of an anihpj Deployment?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl rollout history deployment/anihpj. Shows all revisions with CHANGE-CAUSE (if --record was used). Use kubectl rollout undo deployment/anihpj --to-revision=2 to rollback to a specific revision.

Chapter 7: YAML Configuration Files — The Language of Kubernetes

YAML is the configuration language of Kubernetes. Every resource — Pod, Deployment, Service — is defined in YAML. Master the 4 required fields (apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec) and you can write ANY Kubernetes manifest.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📝 YAML 🎯 DOP-C02: Essential

7.1 Why YAML? — The Configuration Language

YAML is human-readable, whitespace-sensitive (2-space indentation), and supports comments with #. It's the K8s standard format (JSON is also accepted but rarely used for manifests).

📝 YAML — The K8s Standard
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:
  containers:
  - name: web
    image: nginx

✅ Human-readable
✅ Comments with #
✅ No closing braces
✅ Multi-document with ---
✅ K8s native format

📊 JSON — Also Accepted (but rarely used)
{
  "apiVersion": "v1",
  "kind": "Pod",
  "metadata": {
    "name": "anihpj",
    "labels": {
      "app": "anihpj"
    }
  },
  "spec": {
    "containers": [{
      "name": "web",
      "image": "nginx"
    }]
  }
}

❌ Noisy braces & commas
❌ No comments allowed
❌ Need closing braces/ brackets
❌ One document per file
⚠️ K8s accepts both

📝 DEEP DIVE: YAML Syntax — Beyond the Basics

YAML has features that make Kubernetes manifests cleaner and more powerful. Here's everything beyond basic key-value pairs:

TypeSyntaxExampleNotes
stringkey: valuename: anihpjNo quotes needed usually
string (forced)key: "value"version: "1.0"Quotes force string for numbers
integerkey: 3replicas: 3Plain digits, no quotes
floatkey: 0.5cpuLimit: 0.5Decimal point makes it float
booleankey: trueenabled: truetrue/false (lowercase)
nullkey: ~oldValue: ~Also null or just empty
list- item- name: webItems start with dash + space
mapkey: valueapp: anihpjNested key-value pairs
📜 Literal Block (|)

Preserves ALL newlines exactly as written. Perfect for scripts, certificates, and multi-line configs.

script: |
  #!/bin/bash
  echo "Line 1"
  echo "Line 2"
📝 Folded Block (>)

Converts newlines to spaces — produces a single long line. Perfect for descriptions and long text.

description: >
  This is a very long
  description that will
  be folded into one line.
YAML Anchors & Aliases — DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself)
# Define a reusable "template" with &anchor
containers:
- &default-container          # ← Anchor: save this block as "default-container"
  name: web
  image: nginx:1.25
  resources:
    requests: { memory: "128Mi", cpu: "100m" }

- <<: *default-container      # ← Alias: reuse the saved block
  name: worker                 # ← Override just the name
  # All other fields (image, resources) inherited from default-container!
⚠️ YAML GOTCHAS — These Will Bite You
  • Tabs are FORBIDDEN. Use spaces only. VS Code's "Convert Indentation to Spaces" is your friend.
  • "no" and "yes" are booleans! In YAML 1.1, country: no means country: false — not Norway! Always quote: country: "no".
  • "0123" becomes 83! Leading zeros make YAML treat it as octal. Always quote version strings: version: "0123".
  • Indentation must be CONSISTENT. 2 spaces is the K8s standard. Mixing 2 and 4 spaces = parse error.
  • Kubernetes uses YAML 1.1 (not 1.2). Some features differ between versions — stick to the K8s subset for manifests.

7.2 The Four Required Fields — Every Manifest

Every K8s YAML file has 4 required top-level fields. Memorize them:

1️⃣
apiVersion

Which API group/version: v1, apps/v1, networking.k8s.io/v1

2️⃣
kind

What type: Pod, Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, Ingress...

3️⃣
metadata

Name, namespace, labels, annotations — identifying information

4️⃣
spec

The DESIRED STATE — what you want Kubernetes to ensure

🧠 DEEP DIVE: Why These 4 (+1) Fields Exist

Kubernetes uses a universal object model. Every object — Pod, Deployment, Service, Ingress — follows the exact same structure. This consistency is what makes kubectl get/describe/apply work identically for all resource types:

✍️ YOU Write These 4
  • apiVersion — tells K8s which API server endpoint to use (/apis/apps/v1/namespaces/default/deployments). Wrong apiVersion = "resource not found".
  • kind — which resource type. Must be valid for that apiVersion (e.g., Deployment only exists in apps/v1, not v1).
  • metadata — unique identity. name is required (unique within namespace+kind). namespace determines where it lives.
  • spec — desired state. The "what" of your declaration. Structure varies by kind (Pod.spec.containers vs Service.spec.ports).
🤖 K8s Adds the 5th
  • status — the ACTUAL state. You NEVER write this field. K8s populates it automatically as controllers reconcile.
  • For a Deployment: status.availableReplicas, status.conditions, status.observedGeneration.
  • For a Pod: status.phase (Pending/Running/Succeeded/Failed), status.podIP, status.containerStatuses.
  • The reconciliation loop: Controller reads spec (what you want), reads status (what is), computes the difference, takes action to close the gap.
⚠️ What Happens If You Omit a Field?
  • Missing apiVersion: API server rejects with error: object does not have apiVersion set. Can't even be validated.
  • Missing kind: Rejected with Object 'Kind' is missing. API server doesn't know what to create.
  • Missing metadata.name: Rejected — every object needs a unique identifier.
  • Missing spec: Accepted! Some resources have optional specs (e.g., a ServiceAccount with no secrets). But most controllers will do nothing without it.

7.3 Common apiVersion Quick Reference

ResourceapiVersion
Pod, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, PV, PVCv1
Deployment, ReplicaSet, StatefulSet, DaemonSetapps/v1
Job, CronJobbatch/v1
Ingress, NetworkPolicynetworking.k8s.io/v1
HPAautoscaling/v2
🧬 DEEP DIVE: How apiVersion Works

The apiVersion field follows a strict format: apiGroup/version. Understanding this prevents the most common YAML error — wrong apiVersion:

🔬
alpha

batch/v1alpha1
Experimental, may be removed. Disabled by default. Never use in production. Features graduate from alpha → beta → stable.

🧪
beta

batch/v1beta1
Well-tested, enabled by default. API may change. OK for testing, avoid production. Most betas graduate to stable within 2-3 releases.

✅
stable (GA)

batch/v1
Production-ready. Guaranteed backward compatibility. Always use this for production manifests. The v1 means "version 1 of this stable API".

Discover apiVersions — kubectl api-resources
# List ALL available resources with their apiVersions
kubectl api-resources

# Filter by api group
kubectl api-resources --api-group=apps

# Show specific resource details
kubectl explain deployment
kubectl explain deployment.apiVersion

# Check which API versions your cluster supports
kubectl api-versions

# Example output:
# NAME         SHORTNAMES   APIVERSION   NAMESPACED   KIND
# deployments  deploy       apps/v1      true         Deployment
# services     svc          v1           true         Service
# pods         po           v1           true         Pod
# jobs                      batch/v1     true         Job
# ingresses    ing          networking.k8s.io/v1  true  Ingress
⚠️ Deprecated apiVersions — A Common Exam Trap

Kubernetes deprecates old API versions on a schedule. Example: extensions/v1beta1 for Ingress was removed in v1.22 — manifests using it break. Always check: kubectl api-resources | grep Ingress. If a manifest fails with "no matches for kind," the apiVersion is likely wrong. This is tested on CKA/CKAD — the exam may give you manifests with deprecated apiVersions you must fix.

7.4 YAML Templates for anihpj — Complete Set

Below is a complete YAML template library for deploying the anihpj Django app on Kubernetes. These templates cover every resource you'll need. Use them as starting points for any project.

Complete anihpj YAML Suite
# ============================================================
# 1. POD (Single-instance, quick test)
# ============================================================
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj-test
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:
  restartPolicy: Never  # Always | OnFailure | Never
  containers:
  - name: anihpj
    image: 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1
    ports:
    - containerPort: 8000
    resources:
      requests: { memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m" }
      limits:   { memory: "512Mi", cpu: "500m" }
    env:
    - name: DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE
      valueFrom:
        configMapKeyRef:
          name: anihpj-config
          key: DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE
    - name: DB_PASSWORD
      valueFrom:
        secretKeyRef:
          name: anihpj-secrets
          key: DATABASE_URL
    livenessProbe:
      httpGet:
        path: /health/
        port: 8000
      initialDelaySeconds: 30
      periodSeconds: 10

---
# ============================================================
# 2. DEPLOYMENT (Production — Rolling Updates)
# ============================================================
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  labels:
    app: anihpj
    app.kubernetes.io/name: anihpj
    app.kubernetes.io/version: "v1"
spec:
  replicas: 3
  revisionHistoryLimit: 5
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxSurge: 1
      maxUnavailable: 0  # Zero-downtime!
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
        version: v1
    spec:
      serviceAccountName: anihpj-sa
      containers:
      - name: anihpj
        image: 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8000
        resources:
          requests: { memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m" }
          limits:   { memory: "512Mi", cpu: "500m" }

---
# ============================================================
# 3. SERVICE (Internal ClusterIP)
# ============================================================
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-svc
  labels:
    app: anihpj
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector:
    app: anihpj
  ports:
  - name: http
    port: 80
    targetPort: 8000
    protocol: TCP

---
# ============================================================
# 4. HPA (Auto-scale based on CPU/Memory)
# ============================================================
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: anihpj-hpa
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: anihpj
  minReplicas: 2
  maxReplicas: 10
  metrics:
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: cpu
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: 70
  - type: Resource
    resource:
      name: memory
      target:
        type: Utilization
        averageUtilization: 80

7.5 kubectl explain — Your Built-in Documentation

kubectl explain is your in-terminal API reference. It shows the schema, description, and valid values for any Kubernetes field. Think of it as man pages for K8s resources. Essential for the exam — you don't need to memorize every field, just know how to look them up!

kubectl explain — Essential Commands
# Basic: explain a resource
kubectl explain pod
kubectl explain deployment

# Specific field (dot-notation path)
kubectl explain deployment.spec
kubectl explain deployment.spec.strategy
kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.resources
kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.livenessProbe

# Recursive: show ALL nested fields
kubectl explain deployment --recursive
kubectl explain pod.spec --recursive | grep -i "limit"

# Quick check: is this field integer or string?
kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.ports.containerPort
# OUTPUT: containerPort <integer> -required-
#         Number of port to expose on the pod's IP address.
🎯
EXAM TIP: kubectl explain is Your Best Friend

During the exam, if you forget the exact field name for a resource limit or what goes under strategy, just run kubectl explain deployment.spec. The exam environment has full kubectl access — use explain instead of guessing!

7.6 Multi-Document YAML Files (--- Separator)

A single YAML file can contain multiple Kubernetes resources separated by --- (triple dash). This is the standard pattern for deploying an entire application stack with one kubectl apply command.

Multi-Document YAML — Full anihpj Stack
# ===== multi-resource.yaml =====
# Apply the entire anihpj stack with ONE command:
#   kubectl apply -f multi-resource.yaml
# Delete it all with ONE command:
#   kubectl delete -f multi-resource.yaml
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: anihpj
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: anihpj-config
  namespace: anihpj
data:
  DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE: "anihpj.settings"
  LOG_LEVEL: "INFO"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: anihpj-secrets
  namespace: anihpj
type: Opaque
stringData:
  DATABASE_URL: "postgres://user:pass@anihpj-db.rds.amazonaws.com:5432/anihpj"
  SECRET_KEY: "django-insecure-abc123xyz"
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: anihpj
        image: 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj:v1
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8000
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj-svc
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  selector:
    app: anihpj
  ports:
  - port: 80
    targetPort: 8000
# Apply ALL resources, in order, with one command!
# kubectl apply -f multi-resource.yaml
⚡ How Multi-Doc Processing Works
  • Resources are applied in order from top to bottom. Namespace first, then ConfigMap, then Secret, then Deployment, then Service.
  • If doc 3 fails (e.g., invalid Secret), docs 1-2 are already applied. Multi-doc is NOT atomic — there's no rollback if a later doc fails.
  • kubectl apply is idempotent: running it again won't duplicate resources. It creates if missing, updates if exists.
  • kubectl delete -f file.yaml deletes ALL resources in the file, in reverse order.
📦 Best Practices
  • Group related resources in one file: all resources for one microservice, or all RBAC rules for one team.
  • Order by dependency: Namespace → RBAC → ConfigMaps/Secrets → Deployments/StatefulSets → Services → Ingresses.
  • Use Kustomize instead of raw multi-doc files for production. Kustomize applies resources in the correct order automatically.
  • Test with --dry-run=server before applying: kubectl apply -f multi-resource.yaml --dry-run=server.
  • Split large files if one file exceeds ~1000 lines. Use kubectl apply -f ./k8s/ to apply an entire directory.

7.7 Validating YAML Before Applying

Always validate before you apply! A syntax error in YAML can silently fail or cause unexpected behavior. Use these tools to catch issues early:

MethodWhat It DoesCommand
--dry-run=serverSends YAML to API server for full validation (RBAC, schema, admission webhooks) — doesn't persist itkubectl apply -f deploy.yaml --dry-run=server
--dry-run=clientClient-side validation only (YAML syntax + basic schema)kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml --dry-run=client
kubectl diffShows what WOULD change if you applied the filekubectl diff -f deploy.yaml
kubeconformCLI tool — validates YAML against K8s JSON schemas offlinekubeconform deploy.yaml
VS Code ExtensionRed squiggles for YAML errors + autocomplete for K8s resourcesInstall: Kubernetes extension
💡 GOLDEN RULE FOR YAML

Before applying any YAML in production: (1) kubectl diff -f file.yaml — see what changes, (2) kubectl apply --dry-run=server -f file.yaml — validate against the server, (3) Peer review the diff, (4) kubectl apply -f file.yaml. These 3 steps prevent 90% of production incidents caused by bad YAML.

🔧 DEEP DIVE: How kubectl apply Actually Works
kubectl apply is NOT the same as kubectl create. Here's the difference:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  kubectl create -f deploy.yaml                                       │
│  ─────────────────────────────                                        │
│  • Sends the FULL object to API server                                │
│  • If object already exists → ERROR "already exists"                 │
│  • Use for: ONE-TIME creation (namespaces, secrets)                   │
│                                                                       │
│  kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml                                         │
│  ─────────────────────────────                                        │
│  • Stores a "last-applied-configuration" annotation on the object    │
│  • Calculates a THREE-WAY MERGE PATCH:                                │
│                                                                       │
│    1. ORIGINAL (last-applied-configuration): what YOU last sent      │
│    2. CURRENT (live object in etcd): what EXISTS now                 │
│    3. MODIFIED (your new YAML): what you WANT now                    │
│                                                                       │
│  THREE-WAY MERGE LOGIC:                                               │
│  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │  Field in ORIGINAL | Field in MODIFIED | Field in CURRENT    │    │
│  │  ─────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────── │    │
│  │  app: web         │ app: api          │ app: web → CHANGE   │    │
│  │                   │                   │ to "api" (you said) │    │
│  │  replicas: 3      │ replicas: 3       │ replicas: 5 → KEEP  │    │
│  │                   │                   │ "5" (someone else   │    │
│  │                   │                   │ changed it — and    │    │
│  │                   │                   │ you didn't touch it)│    │
│  │  memory: 256Mi    │ (removed)         │ memory: 256Mi       │    │
│  │                   │                   │ → REMOVE (you      │    │
│  │                   │                   │ deleted the field)  │    │
│  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                                       │
│  🎯 THIS is why apply is safe for teams:                              │
│     • If someone manually scaled replicas to 5, and you run apply    │
│       with replicas: 3 → it does NOT scale down! (you didn't change  │
│       it from the original)                                           │
│     • kubectl create would error. kubectl replace would overwrite.   │
│     • kubectl apply does the RIGHT thing — merge intelligently.      │
│                                                                       │
│  STRATEGIC MERGE PATCH vs JSON MERGE PATCH:                           │
│  • Strategic merge (default for apply): understands K8s semantics —  │
│    for lists of containers, uses "name" as merge key. If you add a   │
│    container with name: "sidecar", it MERGES it into the list. If    │
│    you remove a container, it removes ONLY that one by name.         │
│  • JSON merge patch (RFC 7396): simple replace. Entire list replaced │
│    as one unit. Would delete ALL containers if you sent just one.    │
│                                                                       │
│  💡 kubectl apply is the STANDARD for production. kubectl create     │
│     is for one-time setup. kubectl replace is rarely used.            │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🛡️ DEEP DIVE: Admission Webhooks — The YAML Gatekeepers
Before ANY YAML is persisted to etcd, it passes through Admission
Webhooks. This is where you enforce organizational policies:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  MUTATING WEBHOOKS (run FIRST — can MODIFY the YAML):                │
│                                                                       │
│  Example: "Every Pod MUST have resource limits"                       │
│                                                                       │
│  YOU write:                          WEBHOOK transforms to:           │
│  ┌──────────────────┐               ┌──────────────────────────┐    │
│  │ apiVersion: v1   │               │ apiVersion: v1           │    │
│  │ kind: Pod         │    ──────▶    │ kind: Pod                 │    │
│  │ spec:             │               │ spec:                     │    │
│  │   containers:     │               │   containers:             │    │
│  │   - name: web     │               │   - name: web             │    │
│  │     image: nginx  │               │     image: nginx          │    │
│  │                   │               │     resources:            │    │
│  │                   │               │       limits:             │    │
│  │                   │               │         memory: "256Mi" ← │    │
│  │                   │               │         cpu: "200m"   ←   │    │
│  └──────────────────┘               └──────────────────────────┘    │
│                                                                       │
│  Real mutating webhook examples:                                      │
│  • Istio: injects envoy sidecar into every Pod                       │
│  • Kyverno: adds default resource limits, securityContext            │
│  • AWS Load Balancer Controller: adds ALB annotations                 │
│                                                                       │
│  VALIDATING WEBHOOKS (run SECOND — can REJECT the YAML):             │
│                                                                       │
│  Example: "All images MUST come from our ECR registry"                │
│                                                                       │
│  Pod with image: docker.io/nginx:latest → ❌ REJECTED:               │
│    "Image must be from 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/*"  │
│                                                                       │
│  Real validating webhook examples:                                    │
│  • OPA/Gatekeeper: "No pods without resource limits"                 │
│  • Kyverno: "All containers must run as non-root"                    │
│  • Custom: "All Ingresses must have TLS enabled"                     │
│                                                                       │
│  🎯 EXAM: The flow is:                                                │
│     kubectl apply → AuthN → AuthZ → Mutating → Validating → etcd    │
│     If ANY validating webhook says NO → request is REJECTED          │
│     (and you see the error in kubectl output)                         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Required YAML Fields
2
Space Indentation
#
YAML Comments (JSON can't!)

Multi-Doc Separator

📊 Chapter 7 — Recap

📝YAMLHuman-readable, comments, K8s standard
4️⃣4 FieldsapiVersion, kind, metadata, spec
📋apiVersionKnow which version per resource type
🔍kubectl explainBuilt-in API documentation
✂️Multi-Doc--- separator for multiple resources
✅Validate--dry-run=server before applying
⏱️ 15 minutes
Scenario: Write YAML from Memory

Without looking at references, write: (1) A complete Deployment YAML for nginx with 2 replicas, port 80, resource requests/limits, and labels; (2) A Service to expose it; (3) Validate with --dry-run=server; (4) Combine both into a multi-doc YAML with --- separator.

💡 Hint: The 4 required fields are apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec. apiVersion for Deployment is apps/v1. Service selector must match Pod labels. Always indent 2 spaces.
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: nginx
  labels:
    app: nginx
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: nginx
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: nginx
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: nginx
        image: nginx:1.25
        ports:
        - containerPort: 80
        resources:
          requests:
            memory: "128Mi"
            cpu: "100m"
          limits:
            memory: "256Mi"
            cpu: "200m"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: nginx-svc
spec:
  selector:
    app: nginx
  ports:
  - port: 80
    targetPort: 80
  type: ClusterIP

# Validate:
# kubectl apply --dry-run=server -f nginx.yaml
# kubectl apply -f nginx.yaml

📝 Chapter 7 — Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

What are the 4 required top-level fields in every Kubernetes YAML manifest?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec. These appear in EVERY K8s YAML file regardless of resource type. status is added by K8s — you don't write it.

📖 EXPLANATION
# The 4 required fields — memorize these!
apiVersion: v1        # WHICH API version?
kind: Pod             # WHAT are you creating?
metadata:             # WHAT is it called?
  name: my-pod
spec:                 # WHAT should it do? (DESIRED STATE)
  containers: [...]
# status: {}          # K8s writes this — you don't
Q2

How do you find the correct apiVersion for a Kubernetes resource?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl api-resources lists all resources and their API versions. Or use kubectl explain <resource> to see detailed field documentation.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Find apiVersion for any resource
kubectl api-resources | grep -i deploy
# deployments  deploy  apps/v1  true  Deployment

# Get detailed field docs
kubectl explain deployment.spec
kubectl explain pod.spec.containers --recursive
Q3

How do you validate a YAML file before applying it to the cluster?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

kubectl apply --dry-run=server -f manifest.yaml. This sends the YAML to the API server for full validation but doesn't persist it. --dry-run=client only validates syntax locally.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Validate before applying
kubectl apply --dry-run=server -f deploy.yaml  # Full server validation
kubectl apply --dry-run=client -f deploy.yaml  # Client-side only

# If validation passes, apply for real:
kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml
Q4

What's the correct apiVersion for a Deployment?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

apps/v1. Deployments moved from extensions/v1beta1 to apps/v1 in Kubernetes 1.16. Always use apps/v1 — the old versions are removed.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Correct (K8s 1.16+):
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment

# DEPRECATED — do not use:
apiVersion: extensions/v1beta1  # Removed in 1.16
apiVersion: apps/v1beta1        # Removed in 1.16
apiVersion: apps/v1beta2        # Removed in 1.19
Q5

Can you define multiple Kubernetes resources in a single YAML file?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Yes — use --- (triple dash) as a document separator. Each section between --- markers is a separate YAML document. kubectl apply -f processes all documents in order.

📖 EXPLANATION
# multi-resource.yaml — apply with one command
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: anihpj
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 3
# ...
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: anihpj
# ...

# Apply ALL at once:
kubectl apply -f multi-resource.yaml
Q6

What is the difference between a ConfigMap and a Secret in Kubernetes?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

ConfigMaps store non-sensitive data (env vars, config files) in plain text. Secrets store sensitive data (passwords, tokens, keys) — base64 encoded by default, but should be encrypted at rest with KMS. Secrets are not encrypted by default in etcd — enable encryption at rest for production.

Q7

How do you inject a ConfigMap value as an environment variable in a Pod?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Use valueFrom.configMapKeyRef in the container's env section. Reference the ConfigMap name and key. Example: env: [{name: LOG_LEVEL, valueFrom: {configMapKeyRef: {name: anihpj-config, key: LOG_LEVEL}}}]. Alternatively, mount the entire ConfigMap as a volume.

Q8

What does kubectl explain deployment.spec.strategy do?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

It shows the API documentation for the Deployment strategy field directly in the terminal. You can drill down: kubectl explain deployment.spec.strategy.rollingUpdate. This is the fastest way to check valid fields, types, and descriptions without leaving the CLI — essential for the exam.

Q9

How can you validate a YAML file before applying it to the cluster?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Three methods: (1) kubectl apply --dry-run=client -f file.yaml — client-side validation. (2) kubectl apply --dry-run=server -f file.yaml — full server-side validation including admission webhooks. (3) kubectl diff -f file.yaml — shows what would change without applying. Always validate before applying to production.

Q10

What happens if you omit the --- separator between documents in a multi-document YAML file?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Error: "mapping values are not allowed in this context." Without ---, YAML tries to parse the entire file as a single document, but multiple top-level apiVersion keys conflict. The --- separator tells YAML parsers "this is a new document" — each document is processed independently by kubectl.

Chapter 8: AWS Compute Services — Where Your Code Runs

AWS offers five compute options — EC2, ECS, EKS, Lambda, and Fargate. Each solves a different problem. By the end of this chapter, you'll know exactly which one to use for any workload, and you'll deploy anihpj on EC2 from scratch using a user-data bootstrap script.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~3 hours 🎯 DOP-C02: SDLC + Resiliency

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Understand all 5 AWS compute services and when to use each
  • ✅ Deploy anihpj on EC2 with Auto Scaling Groups
  • ✅ Know the difference between ECS and EKS — and when to choose which
  • ✅ Understand Lambda serverless model and its use cases
  • ✅ Navigate the compute decision tree for any workload

8.1 EC2 — Elastic Compute Cloud (Virtual Servers)

EC2 is AWS's foundational compute service — virtual machines in the cloud. You get full root access, can install any software, and it behaves exactly like a physical server. For DevOps engineers, EC2 is where you run self-managed workloads: build servers, monitoring tools, jump hosts, or applications that need specific OS-level configurations.

🤔 THE COMPUTE DILEMMA — Which Service for anihpj?
  • 🎯 EC2: "I need full control. Custom kernel module. Specific OS version. I'll manage it."
  • 📦 ECS: "Just run my containers. I don't want to learn Kubernetes. AWS-native is fine."
  • ☸️ EKS: "I need Kubernetes. Helm charts, Istio, multi-cloud. Give me the standard."
  • ⚡ Lambda: "Just run this function when a resume is uploaded. No server, please."
  • ☁️ Fargate: "Run my container. I don't care HOW. No instances, no nodes."
🖥️
Launch
⏳
Pending
✅
Running
🛑
Stopping
⏸️
Stopped
💀
Terminated
🖥️
Instance Types

General purpose (t3, m6g), compute-optimized (c7g), memory-optimized (r7g), GPU (p4d), storage (i4i). Choose based on workload profile.

💿
AMI (Amazon Machine Image)

A pre-configured OS template. Amazon Linux 2, Ubuntu, Windows, or your own Golden AMI built with Image Builder.

📈
Auto Scaling Group (ASG)

Automatically replaces failed instances and scales in/out based on demand. This is the DevOps essential — never run a single EC2 instance in production.

🔑
Key Pairs & Security

SSH key pairs for login. Security Groups act as stateful firewalls. IAM Roles grant permissions — NO hardcoded AWS credentials on instances!

🧬 DEEP DIVE: How EC2 Actually Works — From Physical Server to Your VM

When you launch an EC2 instance, you get a VIRTUAL MACHINE slice of a physical server. Here's how it actually works under the hood:

⚡ THE AWS NITRO SYSTEM — The Secret to Near Bare-Metal Performance

🖥️
Dedicated Hardware Offload

Nitro cards are physical PCIe cards in every EC2 host. They offload networking (VPC), storage (EBS), and monitoring (CloudWatch) from the CPU — your vCPUs run YOUR code only, not AWS overhead.

🧵
vCPU = One Hyperthread

A "vCPU" is one hyperthread on a physical core. t3.xlarge (4 vCPU) = 2 physical cores × 2 threads each. No oversubscription — you get what you pay for.

⏫
Burstable Instances (t3/t4g)

Accumulate CPU credits when idle, spend them during bursts. Baseline: 20-40% of a core. Run out of credits? Throttled to baseline. Ideal for spiky workloads like web apps with fluctuating traffic.

🔄 WHAT HAPPENS WHEN YOU CLICK "LAUNCH"

🔍1. Find CapacityLocate host with enough CPU, memory & Nitro cards
→
💿2. Create Root DiskCopy AMI snapshot → new EBS volume (/dev/xvda)
→
🌐3. Create ENIVPC private IP from subnet CIDR + Security Groups
→
⚡4. Attach & BootEBS + ENI → KVM-based VM via Nitro hypervisor
→
✅5. Run & ReportUser Data executes → pending → running

The VM boots, User Data (cloud-init on Linux) executes any initialization scripts, the instance state transitions from pending → running, and EC2 begins reporting metrics to CloudWatch. The entire process typically completes in 30–90 seconds.

🔒 Dedicated Host
  • YOU control placement — choose exactly which physical server. Critical for licensing compliance (Oracle, SQL Server per-core).
  • You see and manage the physical host. Can launch multiple instances on the same host.
  • More expensive. Billed per host, not per instance.
  • Use when: software licensing requires physical core visibility, or regulatory compliance demands dedicated hardware.
🔐 Dedicated Instance
  • AWS chooses the host — but you're the ONLY tenant on it. Single-tenant physical isolation guaranteed.
  • You don't see or manage the host. AWS places your instance on a dedicated physical server.
  • Less expensive than Dedicated Hosts. Billed per instance, not per host.
  • Use when: you need physical isolation (compliance/security) but don't need host-level control.
💡 THE KEY INSIGHT

EC2 is NOT a computer in a rack somewhere. It's a slice of a massive, multi-tenant server managed by the Nitro hypervisor. When you stop an instance: the VM is destroyed but the EBS volume persists (you keep paying for EBS). When you terminate: both VM and EBS are gone (unless DeleteOnTermination is false). This distinction is critical for cost management and data protection.

EC2 + Auto Scaling Group Architecture
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  AWS REGION: us-east-1                                               │
│  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  AUTO SCALING GROUP (min:2, desired:3, max:6)                  │  │
│  │                                                                  │  │
│  │  us-east-1a          us-east-1b          us-east-1c             │  │
│  │  ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐           │  │
│  │  │ EC2 #1   │       │ EC2 #2   │       │ EC2 #3   │           │  │
│  │  │ anihpj   │       │ anihpj   │       │ anihpj   │           │  │
│  │  │ :8000    │       │ :8000    │       │ :8000    │           │  │
│  │  └──────────┘       └──────────┘       └──────────┘           │  │
│  │                                                                  │  │
│  │  CloudWatch Alarm: CPU > 70% → Scale Out (+1 instance)          │  │
│  │  CloudWatch Alarm: CPU < 30% → Scale In  (-1 instance)          │  │
│  │  Health Check failed → Terminate & replace automatically        │  │
│  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                                                                      │
│  ┌──────────────┐                                                   │
│  │ Application  │ ──▶ Users hit the ALB, not individual instances  │
│  │ Load Balancer│     ALB distributes traffic across healthy EC2s   │
│  └──────────────┘                                                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 EC2 Image Builder — Golden AMIs

Instead of configuring each EC2 instance manually (install Docker, pull code, set up nginx), use EC2 Image Builder to create a Golden AMI with everything pre-installed. New instances launch fully configured in seconds. This is the DevOps way — immutable infrastructure.

8.2 ECS — Elastic Container Service (AWS-Native Containers)

ECS is AWS's own container orchestration service. Unlike EKS (which runs Kubernetes), ECS is fully AWS-native — no Kubernetes knowledge needed. You define Task Definitions (container specs) and Services (how many copies, networking), and ECS runs them across your cluster.

FeatureECSEKS
OrchestratorAWS-native (no K8s)Kubernetes (open-source)
Learning CurveLower — AWS-only conceptsHigher — must learn K8s
PortabilityAWS-onlyMulti-cloud (any K8s cluster)
EcosystemAWS integrations onlyMassive CNCF ecosystem (Helm, Istio, Argo)
Control Plane CostFREE$0.10/hour (~$73/month)
Best ForSimple container workloads, AWS-only shopsComplex microservices, multi-cloud, large teams
📋
Task Definition

Like a Docker Compose file — defines which containers to run, CPU/memory, environment variables, logging. Task Definitions are versioned (like Docker images).

⚙️
Launch Types

EC2 launch type: You manage EC2 instances, ECS places tasks. Fargate launch type: Serverless — no EC2 management, pay per task.

ECS Architecture — Task Definition → Service → Cluster
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ECS CLUSTER: anihpj-cluster                                          │
│                                                                       │
│  CAPACITY PROVIDER (decides WHERE tasks run):                         │
│  ┌─────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────┐                  │
│  │ Fargate Capacity    │    │ EC2 Capacity         │                  │
│  │ "Just run the task  │    │ "Place on EC2        │                  │
│  │  — I handle infra"  │    │  instances in ASG"   │                  │
│  └─────────────────────┘    └─────────────────────┘                  │
│                                                                       │
│  ECS SERVICE: anihpj-service (desired: 3 tasks)                      │
│  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐              │
│  │ TASK 1       │  │ TASK 2       │  │ TASK 3       │              │
│  │ anihpj :8000 │  │ anihpj :8000 │  │ anihpj :8000 │              │
│  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘              │
│                                                                       │
│  Service Auto Scaling → ALB registers/deregisters task IPs           │
│  Task Definitions are VERSIONED (anihpj-task:1 → :2 → :3)          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

8.3 Lambda — Serverless Compute

AWS Lambda runs your code without provisioning servers. You upload code (Python, Node.js, Java, Go, etc.), set a trigger (S3 upload, API Gateway request, EventBridge event), and Lambda executes it. You pay only for compute time — no idle costs.

⚡
Event-Driven

Lambda runs in response to events: S3 upload, API call, DynamoDB stream, CloudWatch scheduled event, SNS message.

💰
Pay-Per-Use

Charged per millisecond of execution + number of invocations. First 1M requests/month FREE. No idle costs.

⏱️
15-Minute Limit

Max execution time: 15 minutes (900 seconds). Max memory: 10GB. Not for long-running tasks.

❄️
Cold Starts

First invocation after idle period takes ~1-3 seconds to initialize. Provisioned Concurrency keeps functions warm.

📤S3 UploadObjectCreated
→
🌐API GatewayHTTP Request
→
⏰CloudWatchScheduled Event
→
📊DynamoDBStream
→
⚡LambdaFunction
→
📦S3 / DynamoDBWrite Result
🎯 Lambda Use Cases for anihpj DevOps
  • ✅ Image Processing: User uploads resume → S3 trigger → Lambda resizes/watermarks → save back to S3
  • ✅ Scheduled Tasks: CloudWatch cron → Lambda cleans up old job posts daily
  • ✅ API Backend: API Gateway → Lambda → DynamoDB (simple CRUD for jobpost)
  • ✅ Webhook Handler: GitHub webhook → Lambda validates PR → posts comment
  • ❌ NOT for: Long-running Django server (use EC2/ECS/EKS), database (use RDS)
⚡ DEEP DIVE: Lambda Concurrency, Limits & CodeDeploy

Lambda concurrency controls how many invocations can run simultaneously. Understanding the two types is critical for both the exam and production architecture:

🔒
Reserved Concurrency

"This function gets at MOST X concurrent slots."

  • Guarantees capacity — other functions CANNOT steal from this pool
  • Also acts as a throttle: function can never exceed X concurrent executions
  • Use for: critical functions that must always be available (payment processing, auth)
  • Use for: preventing a runaway function from consuming the entire account limit
🔥
Provisioned Concurrency

"Keep X execution environments WARM at all times."

  • No cold starts — execution environments are pre-initialized
  • You pay for warm environments even if idle (provisioned concurrency pricing)
  • Use for: latency-sensitive APIs where p99 < 100ms is required
  • Can be combined with Application Auto Scaling (schedule-based warmup for peak hours)

📊 Account Limit: 1,000 concurrent executions per region (soft limit, can be increased via AWS Support request). This is shared across ALL functions in the account.

📏 EXECUTION LIMITS — Exam Must-Know

⏱️
Max Runtime

15 minutes (900s). For longer: use Step Functions to chain Lambdas, or EC2/ECS for long-running tasks.

🧠
Max Memory

10,240 MB (10 GB). vCPUs scale proportionally — more memory = more CPU allocated. 1,769 MB = 1 vCPU, up to 6 vCPUs at 10 GB.

📦
Max Package Size

50 MB (zipped direct upload), 250 MB (unzipped including layers). For larger: store code in S3 and load at runtime.

💾
Storage & Env Vars

/tmp: 10 GB ephemeral (persists across warm invocations). Env vars: 4 KB total across all variables.

🚦 LAMBDA + CODEPLOY — Gradual Traffic Shifting

CodeDeploy can shift traffic gradually between Lambda versions, enabling safe deployments with automatic rollback:

🕊️
Canary

Send 10% traffic to new version → monitor for X minutes → if healthy, shift remaining 90%. If error rate spikes, auto-rollback.

📈
Linear

Shift 10% every N minutes (e.g., 10% every 1 min → 100% after 9 minutes). Gradual, predictable, easy to monitor at each step.

⚡
All-at-Once

Instant switch: 100% traffic to new version immediately. Fastest, but no bake time. Use only for non-critical functions with thorough pre-deploy testing.

🎯 How It Works Under the Hood

CodeDeploy uses Lambda aliases with weighted routing. An alias like PROD points to two versions: v1 (90%) and v2 (10%). CloudWatch alarms monitor the canary — if Errors metric exceeds threshold, CodeDeploy shifts weight back to v1 (100%). This gives you zero-downtime deployments with automatic rollback — a DOP-C02 tested pattern for serverless CI/CD.

🔥 DEEP DIVE: Firecracker MicroVM — How Lambda Actually Runs

Lambda doesn't use Docker containers. It uses AWS's own Firecracker — a lightweight virtual machine manager written in Rust that boots in ~125ms.

Firecracker Sandbox — Your Function's Execution Environment
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ PHYSICAL HOST (bare metal)                                   │
│ ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│ │ FIREWALL (seccomp + cgroups)                             │ │
│ │ ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │
│ │ │ FIRECRACKER MicroVM (your sandbox)                   │ │ │
│ │ │                                                      │ │ │
│ │ │ • Tiny Linux kernel (~4 MB)                          │ │ │
│ │ │ • Your code + runtime (Python/Node/Java/Go)          │ │ │
│ │ │ • /tmp (10 GB ephemeral)                             │ │ │
│ │ │ • ENI-like network (VPC connectivity)                │ │ │
│ │ │                                                      │ │ │
│ │ │ Starts in ~125ms | Up to 10 GB RAM                   │ │ │
│ │ │ 1.8 GB RAM = 1 vCPU allocated                        │ │ │
│ │ └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
│ └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🐋 Docker Containers (NOT used by Lambda)

Process-level isolation (cgroups + namespaces). Shared kernel with host. Container escape CVEs exist. Good for: your own infrastructure.

🔥 Firecracker MicroVM (Lambda's engine)

Hardware-level isolation (KVM). Each Lambda = separate VM kernel. Escape virtually impossible. ~1000s per host. Sub-second boot.

❄️ Cold Start — What Actually Happens
  • Step 1: AWS receives request (API Gateway, S3 event, etc.)
  • Step 2: Worker checks: "Do I have a warm Firecracker for this function?"
  • IF COLD: Download code from S3 → Boot microVM (~125ms) → Initialize runtime (Python imports, DB connections) ← THIS is the slow part → Run handler
  • IF WARM: Skip all init → Run handler directly (~1ms overhead)
💡 THE TRICK: Move heavy init OUTSIDE the handler

Cold starts are NOT caused by "booting Linux." They're caused by runtime initialization — loading libraries, compiling regex, establishing DB connections. Put this code at the global scope (outside the handler function) — it runs once during cold start, then every warm invocation reuses it. This turns 3-second cold starts into 50ms warm invocations.

💰 DEEP DIVE: The Economics of Compute — When Does Each Service Pay Off?

Choosing compute isn't just technical — it's economic. Here's the math for anihpj: 10,000 requests/day, 50ms avg response time.

🖥️ EC2 — t3.medium (2 vCPU, 4 GB, 24/7)
$30.37/month
  • $0.0416/hr × 730 hrs/month
  • You pay for 24/7 — even at 3 AM with zero traffic
  • Predictable cost, but you pay for idle
⚡ Lambda — 256 MB, 50ms avg
$0.31/month
97× cheaper!
  • 300,000 req/month × $0.20/million = $0.06
  • 15,000 GB-seconds × $0.0000166667 = $0.25
  • You pay ONLY for actual usage
📈 THE BREAKEVEN — When Lambda Becomes More Expensive
🥉
Low Traffic

10K req/day
Lambda: $0.31/mo
EC2: $30.37/mo
Lambda wins 97×

🥈
Medium Traffic

1M req/day
Lambda: ~$31/mo
EC2: $30.37/mo
Roughly equal

🥇
Heavy Traffic

100M req/day
Lambda: ~$290/mo
EC2 (c5.xlarge): ~$120/mo
EC2 wins 2.4×

Breakeven point: ~30 million requests/day at 50ms. Below that, Lambda wins. Above that, EC2/Fargate become more cost-effective for steady-state workloads.

🧭 THE DECISION FRAMEWORK

📊
Lambda Wins When:
  • Sporadic / bursty traffic — pay only when used
  • No ops team — serverless, zero infrastructure management
  • Event-driven workloads — S3 uploads, API Gateway, DynamoDB streams
  • Low-to-medium throughput — below ~30M req/day breakeven
  • Variable load — unpredictable spikes, long idle periods
🏗️
EC2 / Fargate Wins When:
  • Steady high volume — reserved capacity is cheaper at scale
  • Long-running tasks — >15 minutes (Lambda's max runtime)
  • Predictable workload — consistent 24/7 utilization
  • Container orchestration needed — ECS/EKS for K8s ecosystem
  • GPU / custom hardware — Lambda doesn't support GPU instances
ScenarioEC2 (t3.medium)Lambda (256MB)Fargate
10K req/day (light)$30.37/mo$0.31/mo (97x less!)~$12/mo
1M req/day (medium)$30.37/mo~$31/mo~$12/mo
100M req/day (heavy)~$120/mo~$290/mo~$200/mo
💡 Fargate — The Middle Ground

A 0.25 vCPU + 0.5 GB RAM Fargate task running 24/7 costs ~$12/month. Lambda at equivalent GB-seconds with 50% idle time: ~$8/month. Fargate is competitive for steady, predictable workloads where you still want serverless operations. It bridges the gap between Lambda's pay-per-use model and EC2's reserved capacity pricing — you get serverless ops with predictable billing for always-on workloads.

📉
Lambda vs EC2 Break-Even

~30M req/day at 50ms. Below: Lambda wins. Above: EC2/Fargate wins.

🎯
Rule of Thumb

Bursty → Lambda. Steady → EC2/Fargate. K8s needed → EKS. No ops → serverless.

8.4 Fargate — Serverless Containers

Fargate is the serverless compute engine for containers. You define your container (image, CPU, memory), and AWS runs it — no EC2 instances to manage. Fargate works with both ECS and EKS. It's the middle ground between EC2 (full control) and Lambda (fully managed).

🖥️ EC2 Launch Type

You manage the EC2 instances (patching, scaling, security). Containers are placed on YOUR instances. More control, more ops burden.

Pricing: Pay for EC2 instances (even if idle)

☁️ Fargate Launch Type

AWS manages everything below the container. You specify CPU/memory per task. No instances to patch or scale.

Pricing: Pay per task per second (no idle costs)
