🎯 API Gateway Use Cases for anihpj
  • ✅ Public Job API: REST API → Lambda → DynamoDB — simple CRUD for jobpost listings with API keys for third-party integrators
  • ✅ Internal Microservices: HTTP API → VPC Link → NLB → EKS — cheaper, lower latency for service-to-service calls
  • ✅ WebSocket API: Real-time job posting notifications — WebSocket API → Lambda → DynamoDB Streams
  • ✅ Canary Deployments: Stage variables + deployment canaries — 10% traffic to new API version, monitor, roll forward or back

📊 Chapter 9 — Recap: AWS Networking

🌐VPCYour private cloud network, CIDR blocks, subnets
🔒SecuritySG (stateful) + NACL (stateless) — layered defense
☸️EKS VPCPublic+Private subnets, VPC CNI for Pod IPs
⚖️ALB vs NLBLayer 7 routing vs Layer 4 performance
🌍Route 53DNS + weighted/failover/latency routing
⚡CloudFront600+ edge locations, static + dynamic content
🏢 REAL-WORLD NETWORKING — How Companies Connect on AWS
🏢 Netflix — VPC + Transit Gateway

100,000+ VPC instances, 1000s of VPCs

Stack: VPC (per-service isolation) → Transit Gateway (inter-VPC routing) → Direct Connect (hybrid on-prem)

Why: Each microservice gets its own VPC for blast-radius isolation. Transit Gateway connects them without a mesh of peering connections.

🛒 Shopify — CloudFront + ALB

Sub-50ms page loads worldwide

Stack: CloudFront (600+ edge locations) → ALB (path routing) → EKS (dynamic content) + S3 (static)

Why: Every merchant's storefront cached at nearest edge. Dynamic cart/checkout routes through ALB to EKS. Static theme assets never touch origin servers.

🏦 Stripe — VPC Endpoints Only

Zero internet-exposed traffic

Stack: VPC Endpoints (all AWS services) → PrivateLink → No NAT Gateway → No IGW for internal services

Why: PCI DSS compliance demands NO traffic over public internet. Interface Endpoints for every AWS service. S3/DynamoDB via Gateway Endpoints.

📱 Tinder — Route 53 + NLB

Millions of concurrent WebSocket connections

Stack: Route 53 (latency-based routing) → NLB (Layer 4, WebSockets) → EKS (real-time matching engine)

Why: WebSockets need Layer 4 (NLB), not Layer 7 (ALB). Latency routing sends users to nearest region. NLB handles millions of persistent connections.

🧠 Chapter 9 — Core Takeaways

  • ✅ VPC = your private network. Everything runs inside one. Master public vs private subnets.
  • ✅ SGs are STATEFUL, NACLs are STATELESS. SGs handle 99% of firewall needs.
  • ✅ ALB for web apps (Layer 7), NLB for performance (Layer 4). GWLB for virtual appliances.
  • ✅ VPC Endpoints keep traffic on AWS backbone. Gateway (free, S3/DDB) vs Interface ($/hr, 100+ services).
  • ✅ Route 53 weighted routing = canary deployments. CloudFront = global edge caching.
⚠️ NETWORKING EXAM TRAP: Never expose RDS/DB to the internet!

Databases go in PRIVATE subnets. Only ALB (in public subnet) should be internet-facing. Use Security Groups to restrict: ALB SG → Worker SG → RDS SG. Each layer only trusts the layer above it.

⏱️ 20 minutes
Scenario: Design anihpj Network on AWS

Design a VPC architecture for anihpj running on EKS. Requirements: (1) Highly available across 3 AZs, (2) Worker nodes must not be directly accessible from the internet, (3) Pods must be able to pull images from ECR and reach external APIs, (4) Users access anihpj via HTTPS at anihpj.example.com, (5) Static files served via CDN.

💡 Hint: Public subnets for ALB + NAT Gateways. Private subnets for worker nodes. Route 53 → CloudFront → ALB (dynamic) + S3 (static). VPC CNI for Pod networking.
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
# NETWORK ARCHITECTURE — anihpj on EKS
# ─────────────────────────────────────
# VPC: 10.0.0.0/16
# 3 AZs × (1 public + 1 private subnet) = 6 subnets
#
# PUBLIC SUBNETS (each AZ):
#   • ALB (internet-facing) — terminates HTTPS
#   • NAT Gateway — outbound for private subnets
#   • Bastion host (optional, for SSH access)
#
# PRIVATE SUBNETS (each AZ):
#   • EKS Worker Nodes (managed node group)
#   • RDS PostgreSQL (Multi-AZ)
#   • ElastiCache Redis
#   • Pods get VPC IPs via CNI
#
# DNS + CDN:
#   Route 53: anihpj.example.com → A Alias → CloudFront
#   CloudFront:
#     /static/* → S3 bucket (cached)
#     /api/*    → ALB (not cached, or short TTL)
#     /*        → ALB (dynamic)
#
# SECURITY:
#   ALB SG: allow 443 from 0.0.0.0/0
#   Worker SG: allow 8000 from ALB SG only
#   RDS SG: allow 5432 from Worker SG only
#   NAT Gateway: outbound only (no inbound rules)

📝 Chapter 9 — AWS DevOps Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

Your EKS worker nodes are in private subnets. How do they pull container images from Amazon ECR (which is an AWS service on the internet)?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Via a NAT Gateway in a public subnet, or via VPC Endpoints for ECR (interface endpoints for ECR API + gateway endpoint for S3).

📖 EXPLANATION

Private subnets have no direct internet access. NAT Gateway (in public subnet) provides outbound-only internet access. Better: Use VPC Endpoints — traffic stays within AWS network, no NAT Gateway costs. ECR requires TWO endpoints: com.amazonaws.region.ecr.dkr (interface) for image pulls and com.amazonaws.region.ecr.api (interface) for API calls, plus S3 gateway endpoint for image layers.

Q2

What's the difference between a Security Group and a Network ACL (NACL)?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Security Groups are STATEFUL — allow inbound automatically allows return traffic. Attached to ENI/instance. ALLOW rules only. NACLs are STATELESS — must explicitly allow inbound AND outbound. Attached to subnet. ALLOW + DENY rules. Evaluated in rule number order.

📖 EXPLANATION

Use SGs as your primary firewall (instance-level, stateful). Use NACLs as a backup subnet-level defense. Common pattern: SG allows port 443 from anywhere; NACL blocks known bad IP ranges. Remember: NACLs evaluate rules by NUMBER (lowest first), and the first match (allow OR deny) wins.

Q3

You need to expose the anihpj EKS application with path-based routing: /api/* goes to backend service, /* goes to frontend service. Which AWS load balancer should you use?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Application Load Balancer (ALB). ALB supports Layer 7 routing — path-based, host-based, and header-based. NLB only supports Layer 4 (IP + Port) routing.

📖 EXPLANATION

On EKS, use the AWS Load Balancer Controller with a K8s Ingress resource. The controller automatically creates an ALB, Target Groups, and Listener Rules based on your Ingress YAML annotations. Path-based routing is configured in the Ingress spec.

Q4

You're deploying a new version of anihpj. You want to send 10% of users to v2 and 90% to v1. Which Route 53 routing policy enables this?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Weighted Routing. Create two records — one for v1 (weight 90) and one for v2 (weight 10) — both pointing to different ALBs or target groups.

📖 EXPLANATION

Weighted routing is perfect for canary deployments. Start with v2 at weight 1, monitor for errors, gradually increase to 100. If v2 has issues, set weight to 0 — all traffic instantly returns to v1. Combine with Route 53 health checks: if v2's endpoint fails health checks, Route 53 automatically removes it from rotation.

Q5

How does the VPC CNI plugin for EKS differ from traditional Kubernetes overlay networks like Flannel or Calico?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

The VPC CNI plugin assigns actual VPC IP addresses to Pods — not overlay IPs. Pods appear as first-class citizens on the VPC network, directly routable from other VPC resources.

📖 EXPLANATION

With overlay networks (Flannel, Calico), Pods get virtual IPs (e.g., 10.244.x.x) encapsulated in VXLAN packets. With VPC CNI, Pods get real IPs from the subnet (e.g., 10.0.16.45). This means: Security Groups apply to individual Pods, VPC Flow Logs show Pod traffic, other VPC resources (RDS, Lambda) can reach Pods directly without NAT.

Q6

Your EKS cluster endpoint is set to "Private only." How do you run kubectl commands from your laptop?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Connect via VPN, AWS Direct Connect, or a bastion host in the VPC. Your laptop needs network reachability to the private endpoint IP address. Use aws eks update-kubeconfig to generate the kubeconfig.

📖 EXPLANATION

Private endpoint = control plane is only accessible from within the VPC. Options: (1) AWS Client VPN to VPC, (2) Direct Connect from corporate network, (3) SSH tunnel through a bastion host in public subnet. For development, use "Public and Private" endpoint setting — kubectl works from anywhere with IAM auth.

Q7

What's the difference between an Alias record and a CNAME record in Route 53?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Alias records are AWS-specific and can point to AWS resources (ALB, CloudFront, S3) at no charge. They work at the zone apex (root domain). CNAME records are standard DNS and cannot be used at the zone apex.

📖 EXPLANATION

RFC requires that a CNAME not coexist with other records at the zone apex (e.g., example.com). Alias records solve this — you can point example.com directly to your ALB. Always use Alias for AWS resources; CNAME for external domains.

Q8

How does CloudFront handle dynamic content that shouldn't be cached?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Set the Cache Behavior TTL to 0 (or use Cache-Control: no-cache headers). CloudFront forwards requests to the origin but still provides DDoS protection, TLS termination, and edge networking benefits.

📖 EXPLANATION

Even without caching, CloudFront adds value: AWS Shield Standard (free DDoS protection), TLS termination at the edge, persistent connections to origin (faster than user→origin directly), and geographic restriction capabilities. You're trading slightly higher latency for significant security and reliability gains.

Q9

You need to connect an API Gateway to an internal service running on EKS in a private subnet. How do you do this?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Use API Gateway VPC Link with a Network Load Balancer (NLB) in front of your EKS service. VPC Link allows API Gateway to reach private resources inside your VPC.

📖 EXPLANATION

VPC Link creates an elastic network interface in your VPC, giving API Gateway private connectivity. Architecture: API Gateway → VPC Link → NLB (internal, in private subnet) → EKS Pods. The NLB must be internal (not internet-facing). The EKS service type should be LoadBalancer with NLB annotations.

Q10

Your EKS cluster spans 3 AZs. You notice that in us-east-1a, the private subnet is running out of IP addresses for Pods. What can you do?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

(1) Add a secondary CIDR block to the VPC and create new subnets, (2) Use custom networking with the VPC CNI to assign Pods IPs from a different subnet than the node, or (3) Enable prefix delegation to assign /28 prefixes instead of individual IPs.

📖 EXPLANATION

The VPC CNI assigns one IP per Pod from the node's subnet. If subnet is /24 (256 IPs) shared with nodes + Pods, you can quickly exhaust IPs. Solutions: (1) Larger initial subnets (/20 = 4096 IPs), (2) Custom networking (Pod subnets separate from node subnets), (3) Prefix delegation (each ENI gets a /28 = 16 IPs, reducing ENI attachment count). Plan subnet sizes BEFORE creating the cluster!

Chapter 10: AWS IAM — Identity & Access Management

IAM is the #1 security topic on the AWS DevOps exam. If you don't understand IAM, you can't secure anything. This chapter covers IAM from the ground up — Users, Groups, Roles, Policies, and the EKS-specific IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) that lets individual Pods have their own AWS permissions.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~3 hours 🎯 DOP-C02: Security (17%)

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ IAM Users, Groups, and Roles — the holy trinity
  • ✅ IAM Policy structure (Effect, Action, Resource, Condition)
  • ✅ Policy evaluation logic — why explicit DENY always wins
  • ✅ EC2 Instance Roles — no more hardcoded credentials
  • ✅ IRSA — per-Pod IAM roles on EKS
  • ✅ AWS Organizations & SCPs
Before IAM Roles (Pre-2012)

Hardcoded access keys on EC2. Rotating keys was manual. Keys leaked in GitHub repos. Compliance nightmare.

PAST
EC2 Instance Roles (2012)

Attach IAM Role to EC2. SDK auto-retrieves temporary credentials from metadata service. No keys on disk.

REVOLUTION
IRSA for EKS (2019)

Per-Pod IAM roles via OIDC. Pod → JWT → STS → temporary creds. Least privilege at the Pod level.

MODERN
IAM Roles Anywhere (2022)

On-premises servers get temporary AWS creds via X.509 certificates. No more long-lived access keys anywhere.

HYBRID

10.1 IAM Fundamentals — Users, Groups & Roles

IAM is GLOBAL (not regional) — a user created in any region works everywhere. The three core entities:

👤
IAM Users

Individual people or services. Has long-term credentials (password + access keys). DevOps rule: Use Roles, not Users, for applications.

👥
IAM Groups

Collections of Users. Attach policies to Groups, users inherit them. Example: "DevOps-Engineers" group gets CodePipeline, EKS, CloudWatch permissions.

🎭
IAM Roles

Temporary credentials, assumed by services (EC2, Lambda) or users. NO passwords or long-lived keys. The DevOps way: always use Roles for AWS access.

IAM Entity Relationships
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  AWS ACCOUNT                                                         │
│  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  IAM USERS                     IAM GROUPS                      │  │
│  │  ┌──────────┐                 ┌──────────────┐                │  │
│  │  │  alice   │────────────────▶│ DevOps-Engineers│              │  │
│  │  │ (human)  │     member of   │               │              │  │
│  │  └──────────┘                 │ Policies:     │              │  │
│  │  ┌──────────┐                 │  • EKSFullAccess│             │  │
│  │  │  bob     │────────────────▶│  • CodePipeline│              │  │
│  │  │ (human)  │     member of   │  • CloudWatch │              │  │
│  │  └──────────┘                 └──────────────┘                │  │
│  │                                                                  │  │
│  │  IAM ROLES (temporary, assumed)                                  │  │
│  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐                    │  │
│  │  │ EC2 Instance Role │  │ Lambda Exec Role  │                    │  │
│  │  │ Assumed by: EC2   │  │ Assumed by: Lambda│                    │  │
│  │  │ Grants: S3, ECR   │  │ Grants: DynamoDB  │                    │  │
│  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘                    │  │
│  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🧬 DEEP DIVE: Why IAM is Global — The Architecture Behind It

IAM is GLOBAL (not regional). This confuses beginners — here's WHY:

🧠 Control Plane (Global)

The "brain" — where you manage things

  • IAM — users, roles, policies
  • Route 53 — DNS management
  • CloudFront — CDN configuration
  • Billing / Organizations — account management

These services don't run your workloads — they govern them. They're global because permissions and DNS need to work everywhere.

💪 Data Plane (Regional)

The "muscles" — where things actually run

  • EC2 — virtual machines
  • S3 buckets — object storage (bucket is regional)
  • RDS — managed databases
  • EKS clusters — Kubernetes

These services run your workloads. They're regional so data stays close to users and meets residency requirements.

🌍 How IAM's Global Architecture Works
  • IAM data lives in a global database replicated across all AWS regions. The authoritative copy is in us-east-1.
  • When EC2 in ap-southeast-1 calls S3 in eu-west-2, the IAM policy check happens at the global IAM service — not in either region.
  • This is WHY IAM is eventually consistent — writes propagate to the global replicas and may take a few seconds to be visible everywhere. If you create a role and immediately try to use it, you might get an AccessDenied for a few seconds.

🔑 CREDENTIALS — The Three Forms

👤
Username + Password

Console login — for humans. Used to sign into the AWS Management Console. Always enable MFA on these accounts. Never use for programmatic access.

🔑
Access Key ID + Secret

API/CLI — long-lived, 2 parts. AKIA... (public ID) + secret key (shown ONCE at creation). Used by aws configure. Rotate every 90 days.

⏳
Temporary Credentials (STS)

3 parts: AccessKeyId + SecretAccessKey + SessionToken. All three required. Automatically rotated by SDK. Valid 1–12 hours. The DevOps standard.

🔄 HOW STS GENERATES TEMPORARY CREDENTIALS

1. EC2 Requests Credentials

EC2 instance calls the Instance Metadata Service (IMDS): http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/role-name. This is a link-local address — only accessible from within the instance itself.

2. IMDS Forwards to STS

IMDS calls STS: "EC2 i-abc123 needs credentials for role anihpj-s3-read." IMDS acts as a proxy — the instance never talks directly to STS.

3. STS Verifies Trust

STS checks the role's TRUST POLICY: "Is EC2 i-abc123 trusted to assume anihpj-s3-read?" The trust policy is the gatekeeper — it defines WHO can assume the role.

4. STS Returns Temporary Credentials

STS generates: AccessKeyId + SecretAccessKey + SessionToken. Valid for 1–12 hours. The AWS SDK automatically refreshes these before they expire — your code never handles credential rotation.

5. SDK Caches & Uses

The SDK caches the credentials in memory and attaches them to every subsequent API call. You never see or manage these credentials — they're invisible, automatic, and secure by default.

💡 THE REVOLUTION — Why IAM Roles Changed Everything

Before IAM Roles (pre-2012), DevOps teams had to manually create IAM Users with access keys, copy those keys to every EC2 instance, and rotate them manually. Keys leaked in GitHub repos, sat in ~/.aws/credentials files, and were a compliance nightmare. With Roles, this entire process is automated, invisible, and secure by default — no keys on disk, no manual rotation, no credential leakage. This is why the DOP-C02 exam always prefers Roles over Users for any AWS-to-AWS communication.

10.2 IAM Policies — Deep Dive

Every IAM policy is a JSON document with four key elements:

IAM Policy Structure
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",           // Allow or Deny
    "Action": [                  // WHAT you can do
      "s3:GetObject",
      "s3:ListBucket"
    ],
    "Resource": [                // WHICH resources
      "arn:aws:s3:::anihpj-bucket",
      "arn:aws:s3:::anihpj-bucket/*"
    ],
    "Condition": {               // WHEN (optional)
      "IpAddress": {
        "aws:SourceIp": "10.0.0.0/8"
      }
    }
  }]
}
🧠 DEEP DIVE: IAM Policy Evaluation Logic
The evaluation order is CRITICAL for the exam:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  POLICY EVALUATION FLOW (for every API call):                         │
│                                                                       │
│  1. EXPLICIT DENY? ──▶ YES ──▶ ❌ DENIED (stop!)                     │
│     │                                                                  │
│     ▼ NO                                                               │
│  2. SCP (Organizations) DENY? ──▶ YES ──▶ ❌ DENIED                  │
│     │                                                                  │
│     ▼ NO                                                               │
│  3. Resource-based policy ALLOW? ──▶ YES ──▶ ✅ ALLOWED              │
│     │                                                                  │
│     ▼ NO                                                               │
│  4. Identity-based policy ALLOW? ──▶ YES ──▶ ✅ ALLOWED              │
│     │                                                                  │
│     ▼ NO                                                               │
│  5. Permission boundary DENY? ──▶ YES ──▶ ❌ DENIED                  │
│     │                                                                  │
│     ▼ NO                                                               │
│  ❌ IMPLICIT DENY (default — everything is denied unless allowed)     │
│                                                                       │
│  🎯 GOLDEN RULE: Explicit DENY > ALLOW > Implicit DENY               │
│     One DENY anywhere in the chain = request is DENIED.               │
│     You can't "allow away" a deny.                                     │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

10.3 IAM Roles — The DevOps Essential

Roles are how AWS services get permissions without hardcoded credentials. This is one of the most important DevOps patterns — never put AWS access keys in code, config files, or environment variables.

🔴 BEFORE IAM ROLES — The Old "Hardcoded Keys" Nightmare
  • Create IAM User → generate Access Key + Secret Key → copy to server → store in ~/.aws/credentials → HOPE nobody leaks it
  • Keys committed to GitHub repos → AWS bill: $50,000 in crypto mining overnight
  • Rotating keys = manual process, often forgotten → expired keys break production
  • One key per server → all apps on that server share the same permissions → "why can the web server delete S3 buckets?!"
🟢 WITH IAM ROLES — The DevOps Way
  • ✅ Attach IAM Role to EC2 instance → SDK auto-retrieves temporary credentials from 169.254.169.254
  • ✅ No keys on disk. No keys in code. No keys in environment variables.
  • ✅ STS auto-rotates credentials every 1-12 hours. Application never knows the keys changed.
  • ✅ Each service gets its OWN role → Lambda has DynamoDB access, EC2 has S3 access, EKS Pod has Secrets Manager access
🖥️
EC2 Instance Role

EC2 instances assume a role to access S3, ECR, CloudWatch. Credentials auto-rotated, available at 169.254.169.254.

⚡
Lambda Execution Role

Lambda functions assume a role to access DynamoDB, S3, SNS. Defined in function configuration.

🔧
CodeBuild Service Role

CodeBuild assumes a role during builds to pull from ECR, push to S3, call AWS APIs.

🖥️EC2 InstanceSDK needs S3 access
→
🔗IMDS169.254.169.254
→
🔐STSAssumeRole
→
🔑Temp CredsValid 1-12 hrs
→
📦S3 API CallSigned with creds
💡 NEVER Put AWS Credentials in Your Code

The old (wrong) way: AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=AKIA... in environment variables. The DevOps way: Attach an IAM Role to the EC2/EKS/Lambda resource. The SDK automatically retrieves temporary credentials. No keys to leak, no keys to rotate. This alone prevents 90% of AWS credential breaches.

10.4 IRSA — IAM Roles for Service Accounts (EKS)

IRSA is the EKS-specific feature that lets individual Kubernetes Pods have their own IAM roles. Without IRSA, all Pods on a node share the node's IAM role — if one Pod needs S3 access, ALL Pods get S3 access. IRSA solves this with per-Pod, least-privilege IAM.

IRSA — Pod → IAM Role Token Exchange
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  HOW IRSA WORKS — Pod Gets Temporary AWS Credentials                  │
│                                                                       │
│  SETUP (one-time per cluster):                                        │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │ 1. Create OIDC Provider in IAM (trusts EKS cluster)             │  │
│  │ 2. Create IAM Role with Trust Policy:                           │  │
│  │    "Allow sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity for anihpj-sa"         │  │
│  │ 3. Annotate K8s Service Account with IAM Role ARN              │  │
│  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│                                                                       │
│  RUNTIME (automatic, every Pod start):                                │
│  ┌──────────┐       ① JWT Token         ┌──────────┐               │
│  │ anihpj   │ ────────────────────────▶ │ AWS STS  │               │
│  │ Pod      │   "I am anihpj-sa"        │          │               │
│  │          │ ◀──────────────────────── │          │               │
│  │ K8s SA:  │   ② Temp Creds            └──────────┘               │
│  │ anihpj-sa│   (AccessKey, SecretKey,                               │
│  │          │    SessionToken)            ┌──────────┐               │
│  │          │ ────────────────────────▶  │ Amazon S3│               │
│  │          │   ③ Call S3 with temp creds│          │               │
│  └──────────┘                            └──────────┘               │
│                                                                       │
│  RESULT: Pod accesses S3 with its OWN IAM role —                      │
│          no credentials in the Pod, no shared node role!              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
IRSA Setup — Service Account + IAM Role
# 1. K8s Service Account (annotated with IAM Role ARN)
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: anihpj-sa
  namespace: anihpj
  annotations:
    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam::123456789:role/anihpj-s3-read

---
# 2. Pod using that Service Account
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj
spec:
  serviceAccountName: anihpj-sa  # ← This triggers IRSA!
  containers:
  - name: anihpj
    image: anihpj:v1
    # ENV vars automatically injected:
    # AWS_WEB_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE
    # AWS_ROLE_ARN

# 3. IAM Role Trust Policy (in AWS, not K8s)
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": {
      "Federated": "arn:aws:iam::123456789:oidc-provider/oidc.eks.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/id/EXAMPLED..."
    },
    "Action": "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity",
    "Condition": {
      "StringEquals": {
        "oidc.eks.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/id/EXAMPLED...:sub": "system:serviceaccount:anihpj:anihpj-sa"
      }
    }
  }]
}

10.5 AWS Organizations & SCPs

AWS Organizations lets you manage multiple AWS accounts centrally. SCPs (Service Control Policies) are guardrails — they define what accounts CAN'T do, even if IAM allows it.

AWS Organization Structure with SCP Inheritance
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ORGANIZATION ROOT                                                    │
│  │ SCP: Deny leaving organization                                     │
│  │                                                                    │
│  ├── 🏢 Management Account (billing only, no workloads)               │
│  │                                                                    │
│  ├── 📂 OU: Security                                                  │
│  │   ├── 🔐 Audit Account (CloudTrail, Config)                       │
│  │   └── 🔐 Log Archive Account (S3 for all logs)                    │
│  │   SCP: Deny deletion of CloudTrail logs                            │
│  │                                                                    │
│  ├── 📂 OU: Workloads (Dev/Staging)                                   │
│  │   ├── 🟢 anihpj-dev                                                │
│  │   └── 🟡 anihpj-staging                                            │
│  │   SCP: Deny creation of resources outside us-east-1               │
│  │                                                                    │
│  └── 📂 OU: Workloads (Production)                                    │
│      └── 🔴 anihpj-prod                                               │
│      SCP: Deny EC2 instance types > large (cost control)             │
│                                                                       │
│  💡 SCPs are INHERITED down the OU tree. They AFFECT all IAM         │
│     users/roles in the account but do NOT grant permissions —        │
│     they only RESTRICT. You still need IAM policies to ALLOW.        │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

10.6 IAM Best Practices for DevOps

These are the non-negotiable IAM rules every DevOps engineer must follow. They directly map to exam questions and real-world security practices:

🔐
Principle of Least Privilege

Start with NOTHING, add only what's needed. Never use "Resource": "*" or AdministratorAccess for applications. Scope to specific resources.

🎭
Use Roles, Not Users

Applications (EC2, Lambda, EKS) use Roles with temporary credentials. Humans use IAM Identity Center (SSO) with temporary credentials. No long-lived access keys.

🔑
Enable MFA

ALL human IAM users must have MFA. Use virtual MFA devices or hardware security keys (U2F/FIDO2). Enforce with an SCP or IAM policy condition.

🔄
Rotate Credentials

Rotate access keys every 90 days. Use IAM Credential Report to audit. For Roles, temporary credentials auto-rotate — no action needed.

🔍
Use IAM Access Analyzer

Identifies resources shared with external entities. Generates least-privilege policies based on CloudTrail activity. Free to use.

📊
Monitor with Credential Report

Generates CSV report of all IAM users: password age, access key age, MFA status. Audit weekly. Automate with AWS Config rules.

🚨 COMMON IAM MISTAKES ON THE EXAM

1. Putting AWS access keys in code/environment variables → Use IAM Roles. 2. Using root user for daily tasks → Create IAM users with MFA, lock away root credentials. 3. Using "Resource": "*" in policies → Always scope to specific ARNs. 4. Forgetting that IAM is GLOBAL → A user in any region can access resources in any region. 5. Not understanding the policy evaluation order → Explicit Deny ALWAYS wins.

📊 Chapter 10 — Recap: AWS IAM

👤Users/GroupsHumans get users, attach to groups with policies
🎭RolesTemporary, for services — no hardcoded keys
📜PoliciesJSON: Effect + Action + Resource + Condition
🚫Explicit DENYDeny always wins — SCP → Resource → Identity
☸️IRSAPer-Pod IAM via OIDC — Pod → JWT → STS → AWS
🏢OrganizationsMulti-account strategy, SCPs as guardrails
🏢 REAL-WORLD IAM — How Companies Secure AWS
🏦 Capital One — IRSA + Organizations

Zero standing credentials, 200+ accounts

Stack: IRSA (per-Pod IAM) → AWS Organizations → SCPs (block public S3) → IAM Access Analyzer

Why: Every EKS Pod gets its OWN role. SCPs prevent any account from creating public buckets. No long-lived access keys exist in production.

🛒 Amazon — IAM Roles Only

Zero access keys in production

Stack: IAM Roles (EC2/Lambda/EKS) → STS (hourly rotation) → IMDSv2 (secure metadata)

Why: No service uses long-lived credentials. STS auto-rotates every hour. IMDSv2 prevents SSRF attacks from stealing instance credentials.

🏥 Cerner — SCPs + Permission Boundaries

HIPAA compliance at scale

Stack: SCPs (org-wide guardrails) → Permission Boundaries (per-user limits) → CloudTrail (audit every action)

Why: SCPs enforce HIPAA: no PHI outside approved regions. Permission Boundaries prevent privilege escalation. No developer can bypass compliance controls.

🎮 Riot Games — Cross-Account Roles

Secure multi-account for League of Legends

Stack: Cross-account IAM Roles → AWS Organizations → Centralized CloudTrail → SCPs per environment

Why: Ops team assumes roles in game accounts — no shared credentials. Each game region is a separate account. SCPs prevent production changes from dev accounts.

🧠 Chapter 10 — Core Takeaways

  • ✅ Roles > Users. No long-lived credentials. EC2/Lambda/EKS all use Roles with temp creds.
  • ✅ Explicit DENY always wins. SCP → Resource Policy → Identity Policy → Permission Boundary.
  • ✅ IRSA = per-Pod IAM. Pod → JWT → STS → AWS API. No shared node roles. Least privilege.
  • ✅ MFA for ALL humans. SCP to enforce. Root user locked away. IAM Identity Center (SSO) for federation.
  • ✅ Multi-account = security isolation. Management, Security, Dev, Prod. SCPs as guardrails.
⏱️ 20 minutes
Scenario: Set Up IRSA for anihpj

anihpj Pods on EKS need to: (1) Read resumes from an S3 bucket, (2) Write processed results to a DynamoDB table, (3) Access Secrets Manager for database credentials. Set up IRSA with least-privilege IAM roles — each Pod should only have the permissions it needs.

💡 Hint: Create separate Service Accounts for different access patterns. Use conditions to scope S3 access to specific bucket prefixes. The trust policy must match the Service Account namespace + name.
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
# 1. S3 READ ROLE (for resume processing)
# Trust: anihpj-sa in namespace anihpj
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:ListBucket"],
    "Resource": [
      "arn:aws:s3:::anihpj-resumes",
      "arn:aws:s3:::anihpj-resumes/*"
    ]
  }]
}

# 2. DYNAMODB WRITE ROLE (for jobpost app)
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": ["dynamodb:PutItem", "dynamodb:UpdateItem"],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:dynamodb:*:*:table/anihpj-jobpost"
  }]
}

# 3. Service Account + Pod
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: anihpj-sa
  namespace: anihpj
  annotations:
    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn: arn:aws:iam::123456789:role/anihpj-s3-read
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: anihpj-resume-processor
spec:
  serviceAccountName: anihpj-sa
  containers:
  - name: processor
    image: anihpj-processor:v1

📝 Chapter 10 — AWS DevOps Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

An EC2 instance needs to read from an S3 bucket. What's the secure way to grant this access?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Attach an IAM Role to the EC2 instance with an S3 read policy. The AWS SDK automatically retrieves temporary credentials from the instance metadata service.

📖 EXPLANATION

NEVER put AWS access keys on an EC2 instance. The IAM Role provides auto-rotating temporary credentials via http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/role-name. The SDK handles this transparently.

Q2

An IAM policy has both an Allow and a Deny for the same action on the same resource. Which takes precedence?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Deny always takes precedence. An explicit Deny overrides any Allow, anywhere in the policy evaluation chain.

📖 EXPLANATION

Evaluation order: (1) Explicit Deny → REJECT immediately, (2) SCP Deny → REJECT, (3) Resource-based Allow, (4) Identity-based Allow, (5) Permission Boundary Deny, (6) Implicit Deny (default). One Deny anywhere = final decision is Deny.

Q3

What problem does IRSA solve that the traditional node-level IAM role doesn't?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

IRSA provides per-Pod IAM roles. Without IRSA, all Pods on a node share the node's IAM role — broad permissions for all. With IRSA, each Pod gets its own role with least-privilege permissions.

📖 EXPLANATION

Without IRSA: Node has S3FullAccess → all 50 Pods on that node get S3FullAccess. With IRSA: Pod-A gets read-only access to bucket X. Pod-B gets write access to bucket Y. Pod-C has no S3 access. This is the principle of least privilege at the Pod level.

Q4

What is the purpose of an SCP (Service Control Policy) in AWS Organizations?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

SCPs are guardrails that define the maximum permissions for accounts in an organization. They restrict what IAM users/roles CAN do — even if IAM policies explicitly Allow it.

📖 EXPLANATION

SCPs do NOT grant permissions. They only LIMIT. Example: SCP denies ec2:TerminateInstances in production OU → no IAM user/role in those accounts can terminate instances, even with AdministratorAccess. SCPs are inherited down the OU tree.

Q5

How does an EKS Pod authenticate to AWS STS to assume an IRSA role?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

The Pod presents a JWT (JSON Web Token) projected by the kubelet from the Service Account. AWS STS validates the JWT against the OIDC provider configured for the EKS cluster.

📖 EXPLANATION

The flow: (1) kubelet projects a Service Account token into the Pod at /var/run/secrets/eks.amazonaws.com/serviceaccount/token, (2) AWS SDK reads this token and calls sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity, (3) STS validates the JWT signature against the OIDC provider, (4) STS returns temporary credentials scoped to the IAM role.

Q6

What's the difference between an IAM Role and an IAM User?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Users have long-term credentials (passwords, access keys). Roles provide temporary credentials and are assumed by services or users. Roles have no passwords or long-lived keys.

📖 EXPLANATION

DevOps best practice: Use Roles for everything. EC2 gets a Role. Lambda gets a Role. CodeBuild gets a Role. Humans use SSO/temporary credentials via Roles. The only long-lived Users should be break-glass emergency accounts with MFA.

Q7

You need to grant a Lambda function permission to write to a DynamoDB table. What must you configure?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Attach an IAM Execution Role to the Lambda function with a policy allowing dynamodb:PutItem on the specific table ARN. No access keys needed.

📖 EXPLANATION

Lambda execution roles are assumed by the Lambda service when your function runs. The temporary credentials are available via environment variables. The SDK uses them automatically. Least privilege: scope the Resource to the specific table ARN, not *.

Q8

What is the trust policy of an IAM Role, and why is it important?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

The trust policy defines WHO can assume the role (the Principal). It's the first check — if the principal isn't trusted, the role can't be assumed regardless of what permissions it grants.

📖 EXPLANATION

A role has TWO policies: (1) Trust Policy — "EC2 service can assume this role" or "OIDC identity anihpj-sa can assume this role," and (2) Permissions Policy — "Once assumed, the role can read S3 bucket X." For IRSA, the trust policy's Condition verifies the Service Account namespace + name.

Q9

You have an SCP denying s3:DeleteBucket at the organization root. Can an account administrator with AdministratorAccess delete an S3 bucket?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

No. SCPs act as guardrails — they restrict permissions even if IAM explicitly allows them. The SCP deny at root level applies to ALL accounts in the organization.

📖 EXPLANATION

SCPs define the maximum available permissions boundary. Even the root user (account owner) cannot exceed SCP restrictions. This makes SCPs the ultimate safety net — use them to prevent catastrophic actions (delete S3 buckets, leave the organization, disable CloudTrail).

Q10

What AWS services enable a multi-account strategy and why should DevOps engineers use one?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

AWS Organizations manages multiple accounts. Benefits: blast radius isolation (dev can't break prod), cost allocation per team, separate security boundaries, SCP guardrails per environment.

📖 EXPLANATION

Recommended structure: Management (billing only), Security (audit + log archive), Workloads OU with dev/staging/prod accounts. Each account has its own VPC, IAM, and resource limits. Use AWS SSO for centralized access. This is the enterprise DevOps standard.

Chapter 11: AWS Storage & Database Services

Every application needs data. The anihpj Django app stores job posts, user profiles, resumes, and needs a database. AWS offers purpose-built storage for every data type — from object storage (S3) to relational databases (RDS) to in-memory caches (ElastiCache). This chapter maps each anihpj data need to the right AWS service.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~3 hours 🎯 DOP-C02: SDLC + Resiliency

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ S3 — object storage with 11 9's durability for anihpj static files
  • ✅ EBS vs EFS — block vs file storage, when to use each
  • ✅ RDS — managed PostgreSQL for the anihpj database
  • ✅ DynamoDB — serverless NoSQL for jobpost application tracking
  • ✅ ElastiCache — Redis caching to accelerate anihpj responses
🏢 REAL-WORLD STORAGE ARCHITECTURES — Who Uses What
📺 Netflix — S3 Data Lake + DynamoDB

1 TRILLION+ objects

Stack: S3 (viewing analytics data lake) → DynamoDB (user state, 200M+ subscribers) → ElastiCache (recommendation cache)

Why: S3's infinite scale for analytics. DynamoDB's single-digit-ms latency for real-time personalization. No database can handle Netflix's scale — only purpose-built AWS services.

🛒 Shopify — S3 + CloudFront CDN

Petabytes of merchant data

Stack: S3 Standard (active assets) → S3 Glacier (archived data) → CloudFront (global CDN delivery)

Why: S3 Lifecycle Policies auto-transition old data, saving millions. CloudFront caches at 600+ edge locations. Merchants worldwide get sub-50ms page loads.

📱 Snapchat — DynamoDB Global Tables

Millions of writes/second

Stack: DynamoDB Global Tables (multi-region active-active) → DynamoDB Streams → Lambda (real-time processing)

Why: Messages must appear INSTANTLY worldwide. Global Tables replicate across continents in sub-second time. No manual sharding — DynamoDB auto-scales partitions.

🎮 Epic Games — EFS + EKS

1000s of Pods, 350M+ players

Stack: EKS (container orchestration) → EFS ReadWriteMany (shared assets) → DynamoDB (game state)

Why: Fortnite assets (textures, models) shared across ALL Pods. EFS is the ONLY AWS service that gives ReadWriteMany across AZs. Pods in any AZ see the same files.

🏦 Capital One — EBS + AWS Backup

99.999% uptime for banking

Stack: EBS io2 Block Express (SAN performance) → RDS Multi-AZ → AWS Backup (automated snapshots)

Why: Banking needs sub-millisecond disk latency. io2 delivers up to 256K IOPS with 99.999% durability. AWS Backup automates compliance snapshots across all services.

🚗 Airbnb — RDS + ElastiCache

1000s of PostgreSQL instances

Stack: RDS PostgreSQL (primary data) → ElastiCache Redis (sessions + cache) → RDS Proxy (connection pooling)

Why: RDS Multi-AZ for automatic failover. Redis cuts database load by 90%+ for repeated queries. RDS Proxy manages Lambda connection spikes without overwhelming PostgreSQL.

11.1 S3 — Simple Storage Service (Object Storage)

S3 stores any amount of data with 99.999999999% (11 9's) durability. It's not a filesystem — it's object storage (key → value). Each object has a unique key (path) and can range from 0 bytes to 5 TB.

📦
Storage Classes

S3 Standard (frequent access), Intelligent-Tiering (auto-move), IA (infrequent), Glacier (archive: minutes to hours retrieval).

🔄
Versioning

Keep multiple versions of objects. Delete doesn't actually delete — adds a delete marker. Protects against accidental deletion.

📋
Lifecycle Policies

Automatically transition objects between classes: Standard → IA after 30 days → Glacier after 90 days → delete after 365 days.

🔔
Event Notifications

Trigger Lambda/SQS/SNS on S3 events: object created, deleted, replicated. Perfect for resume upload → Lambda processing.

🧬 DEEP DIVE: How S3 Achieves 11 9's Durability

"99.999999999% durability" — here's how it actually works under the hood:

🔢 WHAT 11 9's ACTUALLY MEANS
99.999999999%

Store 10,000,000 objects. Expect to lose ONE every 10,000 YEARS.

For comparison: a typical hard drive has ~99.8% annual durability (lose ~2% of data per year without RAID). S3 is 100,000,000× more durable.

🧩 HOW S3 ACHIEVES THIS — Four Mechanisms

1. Erasure Coding (Reed-Solomon)

Objects are split into data shards + parity shards. S3 can lose ANY 3 shards and still reconstruct the object perfectly. More durable AND more storage-efficient than simple 3× replication — you get 11 9's durability with only ~1.3× storage overhead (vs 3× for replication).

2. Geographic Spread

Shards are distributed across 3+ Availability Zones. An entire AZ can fail — S3 still has enough shards in the remaining AZs to reconstruct every object. This is transparent to you — no failover, no downtime.

3. Continuous Checksumming

Every object is CRC-checked continuously. If corruption is detected (bit rot, disk error), S3 silently reconstructs the object from parity shards. Fully automatic — you never know it happened.

4. PUT Consistency Guarantee

S3 only returns HTTP 200 AFTER the data is durably written across multiple facilities. Your data is already multi-AZ durable the INSTANT the PUT succeeds. There's no "replication lag" window where your data is vulnerable.

🛡️ DURABILITY — 99.999999999%
11 9's
  • "Will my data be LOST?"
  • Essentially never. You're more likely to be struck by lightning twice on the same day.
  • Protects against: disk failures, AZ failures, bit rot, cosmic ray bit flips
📡 AVAILABILITY — 99.99%
4 9's
  • "Can I ACCESS it right now?"
  • ~53 minutes of downtime per year possible. Brief interruptions may occur.
  • Mitigated by: client retry logic, S3 cross-region replication for critical reads
💡 THE KEY INSIGHT

S3 is built so LOSING data is virtually impossible, but ACCESSING it might have brief interruptions. This is a deliberate design trade-off: prioritize durability (never lose your data) over perfect availability (occasional 503s, handled by retry). For DOP-C02: when a question asks about data protection, durability is the concern. When it asks about uptime SLAs, availability is the concern. Know the difference — it's tested frequently.

S3 Lifecycle — Object Transitions + Cross-Region Replication
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  S3 LIFECYCLE — anihpj Resume Bucket                                  │
│                                                                       │
│  ┌─────────────┐   30 days    ┌──────────────────┐                   │
│  │ S3 Standard  │────────────▶│ S3 Standard-IA    │                   │
│  │ $0.023/GB   │             │ $0.0125/GB        │                   │
│  │ Frequent     │             │ Infrequent access │                   │
│  └─────────────┘             └────────┬─────────┘                   │
│                                       │ 90 days                       │
│                                       ▼                               │
│                              ┌──────────────────┐                   │
│                              │ S3 Glacier        │                   │
│                              │ $0.004/GB         │                   │
│                              │ Archive (min-hrs) │                   │
│                              └────────┬─────────┘                   │
│                                       │ 180 days                      │
│                                       ▼                               │
│                              ┌──────────────────┐                   │
│                              │ Glacier Deep      │                   │
│                              │ Archive $0.00099  │                   │
│                              │ 12-48hr retrieval │                   │
│                              └──────────────────┘                   │
│                                                                       │
│  CROSS-REGION REPLICATION (CRR):                                      │
│  us-east-1 (Primary) ────▶ us-west-2 (DR)                            │
│  Every object uploaded automatically replicated for disaster recovery│
│  Also: Same-Region Replication (SRR) for compliance/log aggregation  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

11.2 EBS & EFS — Block & File Storage

FeatureEBS (Elastic Block Store)EFS (Elastic File System)
TypeBlock storage (like a hard drive)File storage (NFS v4.1)
AccessSingle EC2 instance (ReadWriteOnce)Multiple EC2/EKS across AZs (ReadWriteMany)
Use CaseDatabase storage, boot volumesShared files: user uploads, ML models, configs
EKS VolumePersistentVolume (gp3/io2)ReadWriteMany PV for shared Pod access
BackupSnapshots (incremental, S3-backed)EFS Backup (automated, point-in-time)
EBS Volume TypePerformancePriceBest For
gp3 (General Purpose SSD)3000 IOPS baseline, 125 MB/s$0.08/GB-monthBoot volumes, dev/test, most workloads
io2 (Provisioned IOPS)Up to 256K IOPS, 99.999% durability$0.125/GB + $0.065/IOPSHigh-performance databases, latency-sensitive
st1 (Throughput HDD)500 MB/s, frequent access$0.045/GB-monthBig data, data warehouses, log processing
sc1 (Cold HDD)250 MB/s, infrequent access$0.015/GB-monthArchive, lowest cost, infrequent access
💡 EBS vs Instance Store

EBS: Persistent storage — data survives instance stop/termination. Snapshots to S3. Instance Store: Ephemeral — data LOST when instance stops/terminates. BUT: physically attached to the host → lower latency, higher throughput. Free with instance. Use for buffers, caches, scratch data — never for databases!

11.3 RDS — Managed Relational Database

RDS manages relational databases so you don't have to. AWS handles: backups, patching, failover, replication. anihpj uses PostgreSQL via RDS.

🔄
Multi-AZ

Synchronous replication to standby in another AZ. Automatic failover (60-120 seconds). For production, ALWAYS enable Multi-AZ.

📖
Read Replicas

Asynchronous copies for read-heavy workloads. Up to 5 replicas. Offload reporting/queries from primary.

🔌
RDS Proxy

Connection pooling for serverless/Lambda. Reduces connection overhead, improves failover times by 66%.

🗄️ DEEP DIVE: RDS Engines, RDS Proxy & Backup Features
SUPPORTED RDS ENGINES:
  • PostgreSQL (anihpj uses this)
  • MySQL / MariaDB
  • Oracle (BYOL or License Included)
  • SQL Server
  • Amazon Aurora (MySQL/PostgreSQL-compatible, 5x faster, auto-scaling)

RDS PROXY — WHY IT MATTERS:
  Normally, each Lambda invocation opens a NEW database connection.
  With 1000 concurrent Lambdas → 1000 DB connections → RDS melts.

  RDS Proxy sits BETWEEN Lambda and RDS:
  Lambda → RDS Proxy (connection pool) → RDS
  • Pooled connections: 1000 Lambdas share 50 DB connections.
  • Failover: < 30 seconds (vs 60-120 without proxy).
  • IAM auth: Lambdas authenticate to Proxy via IAM, not DB passwords.
  • Required for: Lambda/ECS with RDS at scale.

AUTOMATED BACKUPS:
  • Daily snapshot + transaction logs (point-in-time recovery).
  • Retention: 0-35 days (0 = disable automated backups).
  • Restore to any point within retention window (to the second!).

MANUAL SNAPSHOTS:
  • User-initiated, persist until you delete them.
  • Can be shared across accounts and copied across regions.

11.4 DynamoDB — Serverless NoSQL

DynamoDB is a fully managed NoSQL key-value/document database with single-digit millisecond latency at any scale. Perfect for the jobpost application's high-throughput tracking needs.

⚡
On-Demand vs Provisioned

On-Demand: pay per request (unpredictable workloads). Provisioned: specify RCU/WCU (predictable workloads, cheaper at scale).

🌍
Global Tables

Multi-region, active-active replication. Users in any region read/write locally. Automatic conflict resolution (last-writer-wins).

🧬 DEEP DIVE: How DynamoDB Works Under the Hood

DynamoDB achieves single-digit millisecond latency at ANY scale. Here's the architecture that makes it possible:

Partition Key Hashing — How Data is Distributed
Partition Key "user123" → hash("user123") → Partition #7 (SSD Shard A)
Partition Key "user456" → hash("user456") → Partition #3 (SSD Shard B)

ALL items with the SAME Partition Key live on the SAME partition.
This is why Partition Key design is CRITICAL — a bad key creates
"hot partitions" where one shard handles all the traffic.
🔑
Partition Key (PK) Only

Single value. Must be unique OR evenly distributed. Bad: "status" (only 3 values → 3 hot partitions). Good: "userId" (millions of values → even spread).

🔑📋
PK + Sort Key (SK) — Composite

Same PK, different SK. Sorted within partition. Supports range queries: >, <, BETWEEN, begins_with. Example: PK=user123, SK=jobpost#2024-01-15.

ConceptWhat It MeansExample
1 RCU1 strongly consistent read of up to 4 KB/secItem is 8 KB → costs 2 RCU to read
1 WCU1 write of up to 1 KB/secItem is 500 bytes → costs 1 WCU to write
100 RCU provisionedCan read ~400 KB/sec consistently100 × 4 KB strongly consistent reads
Partition Limit~3000 RCU or ~1000 WCU per partitionExceed it → DynamoDB splits partition automatically
Automatic Partition Splitting — How DynamoDB Scales
BEFORE (1 partition):              AFTER SPLIT (2 partitions):
┌────────────────────┐            ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
│ Partition #7       │            │Part #7A  │ │Part #7B  │
│ 3000 RCU capacity  │   ────▶    │1500 RCU  │ │1500 RCU  │
│ All user123 data   │            │ users A-M│ │ users N-Z│
└────────────────────┘            └──────────┘ └──────────┘

• Data spreads across 1000s of partitions (physical SSDs)
• As your data grows, DynamoDB silently SPLITS partitions
• The SDK routes to the correct partition automatically
• You NEVER manage partitions — it's fully serverless
📡
DynamoDB Streams

Time-ordered sequence of item-level changes. Trigger Lambda on each change — like database CDC. Retention: 24 hours. Perfect for: "When jobpost created → Lambda → index in Elasticsearch."

🌍
Global Tables

Multi-region, active-active replication. Write in us-east-1, read in eu-west-2. Automatic conflict resolution (last-writer-wins). Sub-second replication across continents.

11.5 ElastiCache — In-Memory Caching

ElastiCache provides managed Redis or Memcached clusters. For anihpj, Redis caches frequent database queries (job listings, user sessions), reducing RDS load and improving response times.

How ElastiCache Accelerates anihpj — Cache-Aside Pattern
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  USER REQUESTS /jobs/ PAGE                                            │
│                                                                       │
│  ┌──────────┐   ① Request     ┌──────────┐                          │
│  │  Django  │───────────────▶│  Redis   │                          │
│  │  App     │                │ (Cache)  │                          │
│  │  (EKS)   │◀──────────────│          │                          │
│  └──────────┘  ② Cache MISS  └──────────┘                          │
│       │                                                              │
│       │ ③ Fallback to database                                       │
│       ▼                                                              │
│  ┌──────────┐   ④ Return data   ┌──────────┐                       │
│  │  RDS     │─────────────────▶│  Django  │                       │
│  │PostgreSQL│                   │  App     │                       │
│  └──────────┘                   └────┬─────┘                       │
│                                      │ ⑤ Store in cache for next time│
│                                      ▼                               │
│                                 ┌──────────┐                       │
│                                 │  Redis   │                       │
│                                 │ KEY: jobs│                       │
│                                 │ :latest  │                       │
│                                 │ TTL: 60s │                       │
│                                 └──────────┘                       │
│                                                                       │
│  RESULT:                                                              │
│  • Cache MISS: 15ms (RDS query) + populate cache                     │
│  • Cache HIT:  0.5ms (Redis) — 30x faster!                          │
│  • 95% cache hit rate → RDS handles 95% fewer queries               │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
FeatureRedisMemcached
Data TypesStrings, Lists, Sets, Hashes, Sorted Sets, Streams, GeospatialStrings only (key-value)
Persistence✅ Snapshots + Append-Only File (AOF)❌ Pure in-memory, lost on restart
Replication✅ Multi-AZ with automatic failover❌ No replication (sharded only)
Pub/Sub✅ Native publish/subscribe messaging❌ Not supported
Cluster Mode✅ Up to 500 shards, auto-partitioning✅ Up to 20 nodes, manual partitioning
Use CaseSessions, leaderboards, real-time analytics, cachingSimple caching, transient data, low overhead
💡 Redis Cluster Mode — When You Need Scale

Cluster Mode Disabled: Single shard, all data on one primary. Reads scale via read replicas. Cluster Mode Enabled: Data automatically partitioned across up to 500 shards. Each shard has its own primary + replicas. Use when your dataset exceeds a single node's memory or you need > 1M TPS writes.

11.6 Storage for anihpj on AWS — Complete Mapping

anihpj DataAWS ServiceWhy
Django static files (CSS, JS, images)S3 + CloudFrontObject storage, CDN caching, offload from web servers
User-uploaded resumes (shared)EFS (ReadWriteMany)All Pods need access, multi-AZ, auto-scaling
PostgreSQL databaseRDS (Multi-AZ)Managed backups, failover, patching
Job post application trackingDynamoDBServerless, auto-scaling, high throughput

11.7 Database Migration Strategies

Migrating databases is one of the riskiest DevOps operations. AWS provides tools to make it safer:

🚚
AWS DMS (Database Migration Service)

Migrates databases to AWS with minimal downtime. Supports homogeneous and heterogeneous migrations. Continuous CDC replication keeps source and target in sync until cutover.

🔧
AWS SCT (Schema Conversion Tool)

Converts database schemas and stored procedures between engines — e.g., Oracle PL/SQL → PostgreSQL. Used alongside DMS for heterogeneous migrations.

🔵🟢
Blue/Green Deployments

RDS Blue/Green creates a fully-managed clone (green), replicates changes, and switches over in under a minute. Perfect for Django migrations — test on green, switch when verified.

🔵🟢 DEEP DIVE: RDS Blue/Green for Django Migrations
Scenario: anihpj needs a new column on the jobpost table

WITHOUT Blue/Green (risky):
  1. Run python manage.py migrate on production RDS
  2. If migration fails → downtime, manual recovery
  3. Some migrations lock tables → application errors

WITH Blue/Green (safe):
  1. Create Blue/Green deployment (green = full clone of blue)
  2. Run python manage.py migrate on GREEN (no users affected)
  3. Test application against GREEN database
  4. If OK → SWITCHOVER (< 1 minute downtime)
  5. If FAIL → DELETE green, blue untouched! ✅

💡 This is the DevOps way: test database changes in isolation
   before affecting production. AWS handles the replication.

📊 Chapter 11 — Recap: AWS Storage & Database

📦S3Object storage, 11 9's durability, lifecycle policies
💾EBSBlock storage, single-instance, snapshots
📂EFSNFS file storage, multi-AZ, ReadWriteMany for EKS
🗄️RDSManaged PostgreSQL, Multi-AZ, auto-backups
⚡DynamoDBServerless NoSQL, single-digit ms, global tables
🚀ElastiCacheRedis caching, sub-ms latency, session management

🧠 Chapter 11 — Core Takeaways

  • ✅ S3 = object storage, 11 9's durability. Erasure coding across 3+ AZs. NOT a filesystem.
  • ✅ EBS = single-instance block storage. EFS = multi-AZ file storage. Know when to use each.
  • ✅ RDS Multi-AZ = synchronous standby for failover. Read Replicas = async copies for read scaling.
  • ✅ DynamoDB = serverless, single-digit ms. Partition key design is critical — avoid hot partitions.
  • ✅ ElastiCache Redis = sub-ms caching. Redis for persistence + pub/sub, Memcached for simple cache.
⚠️ STORAGE EXAM TRAP: Don't confuse durability with availability!

S3 has 99.999999999% DURABILITY (virtually never lose data) but 99.99% AVAILABILITY (~53 min downtime/year possible). RDS Multi-AZ = high availability (automatic failover), Read Replicas = scalability (not failover). EBS = single-AZ (unless io2 Multi-Attach), EFS = multi-AZ.

⏱️ 20 minutes
Scenario: Choose Storage for anihpj

Map each anihpj data requirement to the correct AWS storage/database service: (1) Django media files shared across 10 EKS Pods, (2) PostgreSQL database with automatic failover, (3) Static CSS/JS files served globally, (4) Real-time job application counter with high throughput, (5) User session data that must survive Pod restarts.

💡 Hint: Shared files → EFS. Relational → RDS. Static files → S3+CloudFront. High-throughput counters → DynamoDB. Sessions → ElastiCache Redis.
▼ Solution (Click to Reveal) 
# 1. Django media files (shared across Pods) → EFS
#    ReadWriteMany PV, mounted at /mnt/media on all Pods
#    All Pods see the same files, no sync needed

# 2. PostgreSQL → RDS with Multi-AZ
#    Primary in us-east-1a, Standby in us-east-1b
#    Automatic failover, automated backups, point-in-time recovery

# 3. Static CSS/JS → S3 + CloudFront
#    Collectstatic uploads to S3, CloudFront caches at edge
#    Zero load on EKS for static file serving

# 4. Job application counter → DynamoDB
#    Atomic increments, single-digit ms, auto-scaling
#    1000 applications/second? DynamoDB handles it without breaking

# 5. User sessions → ElastiCache Redis
#    Multi-AZ Redis cluster, sub-ms latency
#    Sessions survive Pod restarts (external to Pod)
#    TTL-based automatic cleanup of expired sessions

📝 Chapter 11 — AWS DevOps Practice Questions (10 Questions)

Q1

anihpj Pods across 3 AZs need to access the same set of user-uploaded resume files. Which storage service should you use?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Amazon EFS (Elastic File System). EFS provides ReadWriteMany (RWX) access — multiple Pods across different AZs can mount the same filesystem simultaneously.

📖 EXPLANATION

EBS only attaches to a single EC2 instance (ReadWriteOnce). S3 is object storage, not a filesystem — you can't "mount" it for POSIX file operations. EFS is the only AWS service that provides a shared, POSIX-compliant filesystem accessible across AZs.

Q2

What's the difference between S3 Standard and S3 Glacier?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

S3 Standard is for frequent access (milliseconds retrieval, higher cost). S3 Glacier is for archival (minutes to hours retrieval, much lower cost).

📖 EXPLANATION

Use lifecycle policies to transition objects: Standard → Standard-IA (30 days) → Glacier (90 days) → Glacier Deep Archive (180 days). Deep Archive costs ~$1/TB/month vs Standard at ~$23/TB/month — but retrieval takes 12-48 hours.

Q3

Your RDS PostgreSQL database needs automatic failover. What feature must you enable?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Multi-AZ deployment. RDS maintains a synchronous standby replica in another AZ. If the primary fails, RDS automatically fails over (60-120 seconds) with zero manual intervention.

📖 EXPLANATION

Multi-AZ uses synchronous replication — every write to primary is written to standby before confirmation. This guarantees zero data loss on failover. Read Replicas are ASYNCHRONOUS — they can lag and are NOT for failover. Multi-AZ: durability. Read Replicas: read scaling.

Q4

When would you use DynamoDB instead of RDS for the anihpj jobpost application?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Use DynamoDB for high-throughput, simple query patterns — like job application counters, user activity tracking, or session stores. Use RDS for complex queries, joins, and transactions — like the main job post schema.

📖 EXPLANATION

DynamoDB scales horizontally with no limits but requires you to design your access patterns upfront (partition key + sort key). RDS supports SQL, joins, and ad-hoc queries but has scaling limits. Modern architectures use BOTH: RDS for primary data, DynamoDB for high-throughput features.

Q5

How does ElastiCache Redis improve anihpj's performance?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Redis caches frequent database queries and user sessions in memory, providing sub-millisecond response times and reducing load on RDS.

📖 EXPLANATION

Typical flow: (1) User requests /jobs/ page, (2) Django checks Redis cache for "jobs:latest:50", (3) Cache hit → return from Redis (0.5ms), (4) Cache miss → query RDS (15ms) → store in Redis → return. Result: 95%+ cache hit rate = 30x faster responses and RDS handles far fewer queries.

Q6

How do you ensure S3 objects are not accidentally deleted?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Enable S3 Versioning and MFA Delete. With versioning, a "delete" adds a delete marker instead of removing the object — all previous versions are recoverable. MFA Delete requires multi-factor authentication to permanently delete versions.

📖 EXPLANATION

Also: (1) Enable S3 Object Lock for WORM (write-once-read-many) compliance, (2) Enable cross-region replication to a separate account for disaster recovery, (3) Use S3 Access Logs + CloudTrail to audit all access.

Q7

What's the difference between an RDS Read Replica and Multi-AZ?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Multi-AZ is for HIGH AVAILABILITY — synchronous standby for failover. Read Replica is for READ SCALING — asynchronous copy for offloading read traffic.

📖 EXPLANATION

Multi-AZ: (1) Synchronous replication (no data loss), (2) Automatic failover (60-120s), (3) Standby is inaccessible (can't read from it). Read Replica: (1) Asynchronous (potential lag), (2) No automatic failover, (3) Readable — use for reports/queries/analytics. Production = both Multi-AZ + Read Replicas.

Q8

How do you connect an EKS Pod to an RDS database securely?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Place RDS in the same VPC (private subnet) as EKS worker nodes. Use Security Groups to allow inbound on port 5432 from the EKS node/worker security group only. Store database credentials in AWS Secrets Manager and use IRSA or External Secrets Operator to inject them.

📖 EXPLANATION

NEVER expose RDS to the public internet. RDS SG rule: allow TCP 5432 from EKS Worker SG. Pod connects via RDS endpoint (DNS name). Credentials flow: Secrets Manager → External Secrets Operator → K8s Secret → Pod environment variable.

Q9

You need to process S3 upload events (new resume uploaded → trigger Lambda). How do you set this up?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Configure an S3 Event Notification on the bucket to trigger a Lambda function on s3:ObjectCreated:* events. Or use Amazon EventBridge for more advanced event routing.

📖 EXPLANATION

S3 Event Notifications are simpler — configure directly on the bucket. EventBridge is recommended for production: supports multiple targets, event archiving, replay, and schema discovery. Both deliver events in near real-time (< 1 second).

Q10

What is an EBS snapshot and how does it relate to disaster recovery?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

An EBS snapshot is a point-in-time, incremental backup of an EBS volume stored in S3. Snapshots can be copied across regions for disaster recovery and used to create new volumes or AMIs.

📖 EXPLANATION

Snapshots are incremental — only changed blocks are saved after the first full snapshot. Use AWS Backup or Data Lifecycle Manager to automate: take snapshot every 24 hours, retain 7 days, copy to DR region. In a disaster: create new volume from snapshot in DR region → attach to new EC2 → application recovers.

🟡 Part 4: CI/CD on AWS — The DevOps Engine

Exam Domain: Domain 1 — SDLC Automation (22% of DOP-C02). CI/CD is the heartbeat of DevOps — it automates everything from code commit to production deployment. This part covers the complete AWS CI/CD toolchain: CodeCommit → CodeBuild → CodePipeline → CodeDeploy, plus deployment strategies, package management, and image building.

Chapter 12

12.1 CI vs CD vs CD — What's the Difference?

CI/CD is often used as a single term, but it's actually three distinct practices. Understanding the difference is foundational — DOP-C02 tests this distinction:

🔄
CI — Continuous Integration

Developers merge code into a shared repository multiple times per day. Each merge triggers an automated build + test. The goal: catch integration bugs within minutes, not days.

CodeCommit → CodeBuild → Tests

🚀
CD — Continuous Delivery

Every change that passes CI is automatically deployed to a staging environment. Production deployment requires manual approval (click a button). The goal: always have a releasable artifact.

CI → Staging → [Manual Gate] → Production

⚡
CD — Continuous Deployment

Every change that passes CI + automated tests is automatically deployed to production with NO manual approval. The goal: deploy dozens of times per day with full confidence in your automated tests.

CI → Staging → [Auto Gate] → Production

💻Codegit push
→
🔨BuildCodeBuild
→
🧪TestUnit + Integration
→
📦StageStaging Env
→
🚦Approve?Manual or Auto
→
🌐ProductionLive!
📊 The CI/CD Maturity Spectrum
❌
No CI/CD
→
🔨
CI Only
→
🚀
Continuous Delivery
→
⚡
Continuous Deployment

Most organizations target Continuous Delivery (automated to staging, manual prod gate). Continuous Deployment requires extreme confidence in automated testing — common at Netflix, Amazon, but rare in regulated industries.

12.2 Deployment Strategies — Complete Guide

How you roll out new code matters more than the code itself. A bad deployment strategy can cause outages even with bug-free code. Here are the 6 strategies you need to know for DOP-C02:

💥
All-at-Once (In-Place)

Replace ALL instances simultaneously. Fastest, riskiest. Brief downtime during cutover. No additional cost. Use only for dev/test or non-critical workloads with fast rollback.

🔄
Rolling Update

Replace instances one-by-one or in batches. Zero downtime. Old and new versions run simultaneously during the rollout. Kubernetes default strategy — Deployments use this with maxSurge and maxUnavailable.

🔵🟢
Blue/Green

Run TWO complete environments. Blue = current prod, Green = new version. Test Green thoroughly, then switch ALL traffic via DNS/ALB. Instant rollback — switch back to Blue. Costs 2× during cutover.

🐤
Canary

Send small % of traffic to new version (10% → 25% → 50% → 100%). Monitor error rates at each step. Auto-rollback if errors spike. Minimizes blast radius — only X% of users affected by bad deploy.

🧪
A/B Testing

Route users to different versions based on rules (headers, cookies, user profiles). Test features on a subset. Different from Canary — A/B is about feature experimentation, not risk mitigation.

📈
Linear / Traffic Shifting

Shift traffic in equal increments (10% every N minutes). Predictable, steady rollout. CodeDeploy supports this natively with Linear10PercentEvery1Minute etc. Good for regulated environments needing audit trail.

StrategyDowntimeRollback SpeedCostBest For
All-at-OnceBriefSlow (redeploy)$Dev/Test
Rolling UpdateZeroGradual$K8s default, general purpose
Blue/GreenZeroInstant (switch back)$$ (2× env)Mission-critical, regulated
CanaryZeroAuto (CloudWatch)$High-traffic, risk-averse
A/B TestingZeroRoute-based$Feature experimentation
Linear / Traffic ShiftZeroGradual$Regulated, auditable
🧪 DEEP DIVE: How Each Strategy Handles Failure

Understanding failure modes is what separates memorization from real understanding. Here's what actually happens when each strategy encounters a bad deployment:

💥 All-at-Once Failure

All instances receive the bad code simultaneously. 100% of users affected immediately. Rollback requires redeploying the previous version — another all-at-once operation. Total downtime: 5-15 minutes. Moral: never use for production unless you have a hot standby environment ready.

🔄 Rolling Update Failure

Kubernetes replaces Pods one-by-one. If the new Pods crash (CrashLoopBackOff), the Deployment stops the rollout at the point of failure. Only X% of users affected (where X = number of new Pods already running). Rollback: kubectl rollout undo deploy/anihpj — reverses the rolling update. The old ReplicaSet is still there, ready to scale back up.

🔵🟢 Blue/Green Failure

Green environment is fully deployed and tested BEFORE any traffic shifts. If tests pass but production still fails (e.g., real-user data triggers a latent bug), rollback is instant: switch ALB weight back to Blue=100%. Zero users affected after rollback. The failed Green environment stays intact for debugging — you can inspect logs, run queries, and diagnose without time pressure.

🐤 Canary Failure

Only 10% of traffic hits the new version. CloudWatch monitors error rate and latency continuously. If the CloudWatch alarm triggers (e.g., Errors > 5% for 2 minutes), CodeDeploy auto-rolls back by shifting traffic back to the previous version. Maximum blast radius: 10% of users. The failed canary is documented with CloudWatch metrics for post-mortem analysis — you know exactly when and how it failed, and which metrics triggered the rollback.

12.3 CI/CD Pipeline Architecture

A well-designed CI/CD pipeline follows a consistent pattern. Every stage has a clear purpose and gates that prevent bad code from progressing:

1
Source — Where It All Begins

Code is pushed to a repository: CodeCommit, GitHub, Bitbucket, S3. Every push triggers the pipeline via a CloudWatch Event or webhook. Best practice: protect the main branch — require PR reviews before merge.

2
Build — Compile, Package, Containerize

CodeBuild pulls the source, installs dependencies, runs docker build, and pushes the image to ECR. Artifacts (Docker image tag, test reports) are passed to the next stage. Best practice: use build caching for faster builds.

3
Test — Prove It Works

Run unit tests, integration tests, SAST (SonarQube), dependency scanning (Snyk), and container image scanning (ECR Scan). Gate: if any test fails, the pipeline stops. No bad code progresses.

4
Deploy — Push to Environment

CodeDeploy / EKS / CloudFormation deploys the artifact to the target environment (dev → staging → production). Deployment strategy (rolling, blue/green, canary) is applied here.

5
Monitor — Verify in Production

CloudWatch monitors metrics, logs, and alarms. If error rate spikes above threshold, trigger automatic rollback. Canary deployments use this stage to validate before shifting 100% traffic.

🎯 CI/CD Best Practices for DOP-C02
  • Small, frequent commits — easier to test, easier to rollback. Merge at least daily.
  • Fast feedback (<10 minutes) — developers should know if their change broke something within minutes, not hours.
  • Immutable infrastructure — never patch running instances. Build new AMIs/containers, deploy fresh.
  • Everything as Code — pipeline definition (buildspec, appspec, CloudFormation) in Git alongside application code.
  • Shift-left security — run SAST, dependency scanning, and image scanning IN the pipeline, not after deployment.
  • Artifact promotion — the EXACT same Docker image that passed tests in staging goes to production. Never rebuild between environments.

12.4 CI/CD for anihpj on AWS — Complete Pipeline Design

Here's the complete CI/CD pipeline for the anihpj Django application. Every component maps to a specific AWS service:

📝CodeCommitgit push → main
→
🔨CodeBuildDocker build + test + ECR push
→
☸️EKS DevDeploy to dev namespace
→
🔵🟢Staging B/GBlue/Green on EKS staging
→
✋Manual ApprovalSNS → Slack notification
→
🐤Prod Canary10% → 25% → 50% → 100%
🔗 Full Pipeline — Stage by Stage
1️⃣
Source Stage — CodeCommit

Developer pushes code to the main branch of anihpj repo. A CloudWatch Event triggers CodePipeline. The source artifact (commit SHA + full source) is passed to the next stage via S3 artifact bucket.

2️⃣
Build Stage — CodeBuild

CodeBuild reads buildspec.yml: installs Python deps, runs unit tests (python manage.py test), runs SAST (bandit), builds Docker image, tags with git sha, pushes to ECR as anihpj:abc123. Artifact: image tag + test report.

3️⃣
Deploy Dev — EKS (Rolling Update)

CodeDeploy updates the anihpj-dev Deployment in EKS: kubectl set image deploy/anihpj anihpj=...:abc123 -n anihpj-dev. Rolling update replaces Pods one-by-one. Developers verify on dev.anihpj.internal.

4️⃣
Deploy Staging — Blue/Green

CodeDeploy creates a Green Deployment in EKS staging namespace with the new image. Tests run against Green. If healthy, ALB switches traffic from Blue → Green. Old Blue kept for 1 hour for instant rollback.

5️⃣
Manual Approval Gate

SNS notification sent to #deployments Slack channel: "anihpj v1.2.3 ready for production. Approve?" DevOps engineer reviews test results, clicks Approve in CodePipeline console (or via AWS CLI).

6️⃣
Deploy Prod — Canary (10%→100%)

CodeDeploy shifts 10% of production traffic to new version via weighted ALB target groups. CloudWatch monitors error rate + latency. After 10 minutes of healthy metrics, auto-shifts to 100%. If errors spike, auto-rollback to previous version.

🧪 anihpj Pipeline Scenario — What Happens When You Push
  1. 10:00 AM: Developer commits "fix: pagination bug" → pushes to main
  2. 10:01 AM: CodePipeline triggers automatically. Source stage: 30 seconds.
  3. 10:02 AM: CodeBuild: install deps (45s), run tests (90s), Docker build (60s), ECR push (20s). Total: ~4 min.
  4. 10:06 AM: Deploy to dev. Rolling update: 3 new Pods created, 3 old terminated. Total: ~2 min.
  5. 10:08 AM: Deploy to staging. Blue/Green: Green env created (1 min), tests pass, traffic shifted.
  6. 10:09 AM: Manual approval. Slack notification sent. DevOps reviews and approves at 10:15 AM.
  7. 10:16 AM: Canary to prod: 10% traffic → monitor 10 min → 100%. Total deploy time: ~17 min from commit to production.

📝 Chapter 12 — Exam Questions (CI/CD Fundamentals)

Q1

A DevOps engineer is designing a CI/CD pipeline for anihpj on AWS. The team wants every git push to trigger automated builds and tests, but production deployments require a manager's approval. Which CI/CD practice is this?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Continuous Delivery. In Continuous Delivery, every change is automatically built, tested, and deployed to staging — but production deployment requires a manual approval gate. Continuous Deployment would push to production automatically without human intervention.

📖 EXPLANATION

Continuous Integration: automated build + test on every merge. Continuous Delivery: CI + auto-deploy to staging + manual gate to production. Continuous Deployment: CI + auto-deploy all the way to production, no manual gate. The key differentiator is the presence of a manual approval step before production. DOP-C02 tests this distinction frequently.

Q2

The anihpj team wants zero downtime during deployments and the ability to instantly roll back by switching traffic. Which deployment strategy should they use?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Blue/Green deployment. Two identical environments run simultaneously. Traffic is switched from Blue (current) to Green (new) via DNS or load balancer. Rollback is instant — just switch traffic back to Blue.

📖 EXPLANATION

Why not Rolling? Rolling replaces instances gradually — rollback requires reversing the process, which takes time. Why not Canary? Canary shifts traffic gradually (10%→100%), good for risk mitigation but slower rollback. Blue/Green gives instant rollback because the old environment is still running untouched. The trade-off: Blue/Green costs 2× during cutover since both environments run simultaneously.

Q3

A developer pushes code to CodeCommit. The pipeline should: (1) build a Docker image, (2) run unit tests, (3) push to ECR, and (4) deploy to EKS. Which AWS services should be used for steps 1-3 vs step 4?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Steps 1-3: CodeBuild (buildspec.yml defines all three phases). Step 4: CodeDeploy (handles deployment to EKS with rolling/blue-green/canary strategies). Both orchestrated by CodePipeline which coordinates the transition between stages.

📖 EXPLANATION
# Service mapping in the pipeline:
# CodeCommit  → CodeBuild            → CodeDeploy
# (source)       (build + test + ECR)   (deploy to EKS)
#
# buildspec.yml handles:
#   install: pip install deps
#   pre_build: docker login ECR
#   build: python manage.py test + docker build
#   post_build: docker push ECR
#
# CodeDeploy handles:
#   appspec.yml → EKS deployment with chosen strategy
Q4

The anihpj team wants to test a new feature on 5% of users before full rollout. Which deployment strategy and AWS services should they use?

Show Answer & Explanation
✅ ANSWER

Canary deployment using CodeDeploy with weighted ALB target groups. Shift 5% of traffic to the new version, monitor CloudWatch metrics (error rate, latency), and gradually increase to 100% if healthy. If errors spike, CodeDeploy auto-rolls back.

📖 EXPLANATION

Canary is ideal when you want to minimize blast radius. Only 5% of users are exposed to potential bugs. For A/B testing (feature experimentation based on user attributes), you'd use Route 53 weighted routing or ALB listener rules with header/cookie-based routing instead.

Q5
